\medterm{Acute Kidney Injury} A sudden decline in kidney function over hours or days, causing reduced filtration and possible fluid, electrolyte,

or acid-base disturbances. Causes include reduced blood flow, direct kidney injury, and urinary obstruction.

\synonyms
AKI

\medterm{Acute Kidney Injury Staging} A standardized classification based on changes in serum creatinine and urine output.
KDIGO stages 1 through 3 quantify the severity of abrupt kidney-function decline and
support prognosis, monitoring, and treatment decisions.

\medterm{Acute Leukemia} A leukemia characterized by a rapid onset, clonal proliferation of immature blast cells
(>20\% of bone marrow cells), and rapid progression without treatment. Subtypes: acute
lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL, B-cell or T-cell, most common childhood cancer, peak age
2-5) and acute myeloid leukemia (AML, more common in adults, median age 65-70).

\medterm{Acute Life-Threatening Event (ALTE)} ALTE is an older term for an unexpected infant event involving altered breathing, color, tone, or responsiveness; current practice generally uses the more specific term brief resolved unexplained event (BRUE).

\medterm{Acute Limb Ischemia} Sudden reduction in arterial blood flow to a limb that threatens tissue viability,
usually caused by embolism, thrombosis, or graft occlusion. The six classic findings are
pain, pallor, pulselessness, paresthesia, paralysis, and poikilothermia; urgent vascular
assessment and revascularization are required.

\medterm{Acute Limb Ischemia Six Ps} The classic findings of acute arterial perfusion loss: pain, pallor, pulselessness,
paresthesia, paralysis, and poikilothermia. Motor or sensory deficit indicates
threatened limb and requires urgent vascular assessment and revascularization planning.

\medterm{Acute Liver Failure} Rapid deterioration of liver function in a previously healthy individual without prior
liver disease. Causes include acetaminophen toxicity, viral hepatitis, drug reactions,
autoimmune hepatitis, and Wilson disease. Features include coagulopathy, encephalopathy,
and multiorgan failure. Presents with jaundice, confusion, and bleeding.

\synonyms
Liver Failure, Acute

\medterm{Acute Lymphangitis} Acute inflammation of lymphatic vessels, typically resulting from streptococcal or
staphylococcal infection spreading from a distal wound. It presents with red, tender
streaks extending proximally from the site of infection, along with fever, malaise, and
regional lymphadenopathy. Treatment requires systemic antibiotics.

\medterm{Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia} A clonal malignancy of lymphoid progenitor cells, most common in children (peak age 2-5
years) but also occurring in adults with a worse prognosis with increasing age.

\medterm{Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (ALL)} Acute lymphoblastic leukemia is a rapidly progressive cancer of immature lymphoid precursor cells that crowds the bone marrow and can involve blood, lymph nodes, or the central nervous system.

\medterm{Acute Lymphocytic Leukemia} A malignant proliferation of immature lymphoid precursors in the bone marrow and blood,
commonly presenting with cytopenias, fever, bone pain, or lymphadenopathy.

\synonyms
Leukemia, Acute Lymphocytic

\medterm{Acute Lymphoid Leukemia} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Acute lymphocytic leukemia}.

\medterm{Acute Megakaryoblastic Leukemia} A rare subtype of acute myeloid leukemia characterized by the proliferation of
megakaryoblasts in the bone marrow and peripheral blood.

\medterm{Acute Mesenteric Ischemia} Occlusion or reduced flow in the mesenteric arteries causing bowel ischemia, most
commonly from embolism or atherosclerosis. Presents with severe abdominal pain
disproportionate to physical findings. Delayed treatment leads to bowel necrosis,
perforation, and high mortality.

\medterm{Acute Mountain Sickness} A mild altitude illness caused by rapid ascent, usually producing headache with nausea,
fatigue, dizziness, or sleep disturbance. Prevention and treatment include gradual
acclimatization, descent when symptoms worsen, oxygen, and appropriate medical therapy.

\medterm{Acute Mountain Sickness (AMS)} A high-altitude illness caused by insufficient acclimatization, typically producing headache with nausea, dizziness, fatigue, or sleep disturbance after ascent.

\medterm{Acute Myeloblastic Leukemia} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Acute myelogenous leukemia}.

\medterm{Acute Myelogenous Leukemia} A fast-growing cancer of immature myeloid blood cells in the bone marrow and blood; also called acute myeloid leukemia.

\synonyms
acute myeloid leukemia.
Leukemia, Acute Myelogenous

\medterm{Acute Myeloid Leukemia} A clonal hematologic malignancy characterized by proliferation of immature myeloid
blasts that suppress normal hematopoiesis. Diagnosis requires the appropriate blast
burden or defining genetic abnormality, and presentation commonly includes anemia,
infection, and bleeding from marrow failure.

\medterm{Acute Myeloid Leukemia (AML) - Children} Childhood acute myeloid leukemia is a marrow and blood cancer caused by uncontrolled proliferation of immature myeloid cells, producing anemia, infection, and bleeding risk.

\medterm{Acute Myeloid Leukemia - Adult} Adult acute myeloid leukemia is an aggressive malignancy of myeloid blood-cell precursors that impairs normal blood-cell production.

\medterm{Acute Myocardial Infarction} Sudden death of heart muscle caused by prolonged inadequate coronary blood flow, usually from an acutely obstructed coronary artery; it is a medical emergency.

\medterm{Acute Necrotizing Ulcerative Gingivitis (ANUG)} A painful, rapidly developing infection of the gums characterized by punched-out gingival papillae, ulceration, spontaneous bleeding, gray necrotic tissue, and foul breath. It is associated with anaerobic oral bacteria and risk factors such as poor oral hygiene, smoking, stress, malnutrition, or immunosuppression; urgent dental evaluation, cleaning, and treatment are needed.

\medterm{Acute Nephritic Syndrome} A clinical syndrome of glomerular inflammation marked by hematuria, variable proteinuria, edema, hypertension, and reduced kidney function.

\medterm{Acute Nonlymphocytic Leukemia} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Acute myelogenous leukemia}.

\medterm{Acute Obstructive Uropathy} Sudden impairment of urinary flow caused by obstruction of the urinary tract,
potentially producing hydronephrosis, pain, infection, and acute kidney injury.

\medterm{Acute Onset Pain} Pain that begins suddenly, often over seconds, minutes, or hours.

\synonyms
Sudden-onset pain

\medterm{Acute Otitis Media} Infection of the middle ear space with acute onset of symptoms and signs of middle ear
inflammation. Diagnosis requires middle ear effusion plus acute symptoms (ear pain,
fever, irritability) or signs (bulging tympanic membrane, erythema). Most common
pathogen: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

\medterm{Acute Pain} Pain that begins suddenly and usually lasts for a limited period, often because of injury, illness, or a procedure.

\medterm{Acute Pancreatitis} Sudden inflammation of the pancreas, most often caused by gallstones or alcohol. It causes upper-abdominal pain
and may cause vomiting or systemic complications; severity ranges from mild to life-threatening.

\medterm{Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia} A distinct subtype of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) characterized by the
t(15;17)(q24;q21) translocation, which fuses the promyelocytic leukemia (PML) gene on
chromosome 15 with the retinoic acid receptor alpha (RARA) gene on chromosome 17.

\medterm{Acute Radiation Sickness} Illness caused by a high dose of penetrating radiation received over a short time.

\medterm{Acute Radiation Syndrome} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Radiation sickness}.

\medterm{Acute Rehabilitation} Multidisciplinary rehabilitation provided during or soon after hospitalization for conditions such as stroke,
spinal-cord injury, traumatic brain injury, or major trauma. Treatment is tailored to functional needs.

\medterm{Acute Renal Failure} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Acute kidney injury}.

\medterm{Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome} A severe inflammatory lung injury causing acute hypoxemic respiratory failure and diffuse lung
opacities not fully explained by heart failure. Common triggers include sepsis, pneumonia, aspiration, trauma, and pancreatitis.

\medterm{Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)} ARDS is acute inflammatory lung injury causing severe hypoxemia and diffuse pulmonary opacities that are not fully explained by heart failure or fluid overload.

\medterm{Acute Respiratory Failure} A sudden inability of the respiratory system to maintain adequate oxygenation, carbon-dioxide removal, or both. It may result from lung disease, airway obstruction, neuromuscular weakness, or impaired respiratory drive and requires urgent evaluation.

\medterm{Acute Retinal Necrosis} A rapidly progressive necrotizing viral retinitis, usually caused by herpes viruses, with vitreous inflammation, peripheral retinal necrosis, occlusive vasculitis, and risk of retinal detachment.
\medterm{Acute Retroviral Syndrome} The acute illness that may occur shortly after HIV infection, commonly involving fever, rash, sore throat, lymph-node enlargement, muscle aches, or headache.

\medterm{Acute Rhinitis} Sudden inflammation of the nasal lining, commonly caused by a viral infection or an irritant, producing congestion, nasal discharge, and sneezing.

\medterm{Acute Rhinosinusitis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Acute sinusitis}.

\medterm{Acute Sinusitis} Acute inflammation of the mucosa of one or more paranasal sinuses, usually following a
viral upper-respiratory infection. It causes nasal obstruction, purulent or discoloured
discharge, facial pressure or pain, and reduced smell; persistent, severe, or worsening
symptoms may indicate acute bacterial rhinosinusitis or a complication.

\synonyms
Sinusitis, Acute

\medterm{Acute Stress Disorder} A trauma-related mental-health condition beginning after exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury, or
sexual violence and lasting from three days to one month. Symptoms may include intrusive memories, avoidance,
negative mood, dissociation, and increased arousal; professional assessment and support are recommended.

\medterm{Acute Symptomatic Seizures Secondary To Autoimmune Encephalitis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Autoimmune epilepsy}.

\medterm{Acute Thrombocytopenic Purpura} An acute disorder with a markedly reduced platelet count and bleeding or purpura, historically used for immune thrombocytopenic purpura, often following an infection in children.

\medterm{Acute Transverse Myelitis} Inflammation across a segment of the spinal cord that can cause rapidly developing weakness, sensory changes, and bladder or bowel dysfunction.

\medterm{Acute Tubular Necrosis} Most common cause of intrinsic AKI resulting from ischemic or nephrotoxic injury to
tubular epithelial cells. Ischemic ATN develops from prolonged prerenal states.
Nephrotoxic ATN results from direct injury by aminoglycosides, amphotericin B,
cisplatin, contrast, and myoglobin.

\medterm{Acute Urinary Retention} Sudden inability to empty the bladder, often causing painful lower-abdominal distension and requiring urgent assessment.

\medterm{Acute Viral Illness} A short-duration illness caused by a virus. Symptoms depend on the affected organ system and may
include fever, fatigue, cough, vomiting, diarrhea, or rash.

\medterm{Acute-On-Chronic Liver Failure} Acute deterioration of pre-existing chronic liver disease associated with organ failure
and high short-term mortality. It is commonly precipitated by infection, alcohol-related
hepatitis, bleeding, or other acute insults and requires urgent multidisciplinary
management.

\medterm{Acyclovir} An antiviral drug that inhibits herpesvirus DNA polymerase and is used for infections caused by herpes simplex virus and varicella-zoster virus.

\medterm{Ad Lib} A prescription or clinical instruction meaning as desired or as tolerated, from the Latin ad libitum.

\medterm{ADA Deficiency} An inherited lack of adenosine deaminase activity that causes toxic purine metabolites to accumulate and can produce severe combined immunodeficiency affecting both T- and B-cell function.

\medterm{Adactyly} Adactyly is congenital absence of one or more fingers or toes, ranging from loss of a digit to more extensive limb malformation.

\medterm{Adam's Apple} The common name for the laryngeal prominence, the visible projection at the front of the
neck formed by the angle where the two laminae of the thyroid cartilage meet.

\medterm{Adamantine} Hard or resembling diamond; in dentistry, adamantine refers to enamel or enamel-like tissue.

\medterm{Adaptation} Adaptation is the adjustment of a cell, organism, or person to a changing internal or external condition; in medicine it may involve physiologic compensation, behavior, or rehabilitation.

\medterm{Adaptive Design} A clinical-study design that permits planned changes in response to accumulating data while preserving validity.

\medterm{Adaptive Equipment} Devices that help individuals with disabilities perform daily activities independently.
Examples: reachers, dressing sticks, button hooks, ergonomic utensils, grab bars, shower
chairs, wheelchairs, walkers, canes. Selection based on individual needs and goals.
Occupational therapy assessment recommended.

\medterm{Adaptive Immunity} Antigen-specific immunity mediated principally by B and T lymphocytes. It develops after
antigen exposure, generates immunologic memory, and includes humoral responses from
B-cell antibodies and cellular responses from T cells.

\medterm{Addiction} A chronic, relapsing brain disorder characterized by compulsive substance use or
behavior despite harmful consequences. It involves changes in brain reward, motivation,
and memory circuits, leading to impaired control over the behavior. Treatment often
combines medication, behavioral therapy, and social support.

\medterm{Addiction Sexual (Sexual Addiction)} Alternate index label for Sexual Addiction.

\medterm{Addiction, Alcohol} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Alcohol use disorder}.

\medterm{Addiction, Gambling} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Compulsive gambling}.

\medterm{Addiction, Nicotine} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Nicotine dependence}.

\medterm{Addictive Behavior} Repeated substance use or rewarding behavior that becomes difficult to control despite harmful consequences.

\medterm{Addison Anemia} An older name for pernicious anemia, a vitamin B12-deficiency megaloblastic anemia caused by inadequate intrinsic factor and impaired B12 absorption.

\medterm{Addison Disease} Primary adrenal insufficiency resulting from destruction or dysfunction of the adrenal
cortex, leading to deficiency of cortisol, aldosterone, and adrenal androgens.

\medterm{Addison's Disease} A disorder in which the adrenal glands produce too little cortisol and, often, too little aldosterone.

\synonyms
Chronic Adrenal Insufficiency
Primary Adrenal Insufficiency

\medterm{Addisonian Anemia} Another historical term for pernicious anemia, in which autoimmune loss of gastric parietal-cell function or intrinsic factor causes vitamin B12 deficiency.

\medterm{Addisonian Crisis} Life-threatening emergency resulting from acute cortisol deficiency in patients with
adrenal insufficiency. Precipitated by infection, trauma, surgery, or abrupt
discontinuation of chronic glucocorticoid therapy.

\medterm{Addisons Anemia (Pernicious Anemia)} Alternate index label for Pernicious Anemia.

\medterm{Additive Genetic Effects} The contributions of individual alleles that combine approximately additively to influence a measurable trait, without requiring interaction between those alleles.

\medterm{Adduct} To adduct a limb or body part is to move it toward the body’s midline; the opposite movement is abduction.

\medterm{Adduction} Movement of a body part toward the body's midline.

\medterm{Adductor Muscle} A muscle that moves a body part toward the body’s midline or, for a digit, toward the axis of the hand or foot.

\medterm{Adductor Muscles} A group of muscles on the medial thigh that adduct (pull toward the midline) the hip:
adductor longus, adductor brevis, adductor magnus, gracilis, and pectineus. Innervated
by the obturator nerve (L2-L4); adductor magnus also receives tibial nerve (sciatic).
Adductor longus is a common site of athletic injury ('groin strain').

\medterm{Adenine (A)} In genetics, A denotes adenine, a nucleobase that pairs with thymine in DNA and with uracil in RNA.
\medterm{Adenitis} Inflammation of a gland or lymph node, usually caused by infection or an inflammatory disorder. Lymphadenitis is a common form.

\medterm{Adenitis, Mesenteric} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Mesenteric lymphadenitis}.

\medterm{Adenocarcinoma} A malignant tumor arising from glandular epithelial tissue, forming gland-like
structures. The most common type of carcinoma, occurring in organs such as the colon,
breast, lung, pancreas, and prostate.

\medterm{Adenocarcinoma In Situ Of Cervix} A noninvasive glandular precursor lesion of the cervix, usually associated with high-risk human papillomavirus; abnormal glandular cells remain confined to the epithelium but may progress to invasive adenocarcinoma.

\medterm{Adenocarcinoma Of The Colon} A malignant gland-forming epithelial tumor of the colon, commonly presenting with
altered bowel habits, occult bleeding, anemia, or an obstructing mass.

\medterm{Adenohypophysis} The anterior lobe of the pituitary gland, which produces and secretes hormones including adrenocorticotropic hormone, thyroid-stimulating hormone, growth hormone, prolactin, and gonadotropins.

\medterm{Adenoid Basal Carcinoma} A rare, usually indolent cervical carcinoma composed of small basaloid nests, often associated with squamous intraepithelial lesions and requiring distinction from more aggressive tumors.

\medterm{Adenoid Cystic Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor of glandular tissue, most commonly of the salivary glands,
characterized by slow growth, perineural spread, and late recurrence.

\medterm{Adenoid Cystic Carcinoma Of The Vulva} A rare malignant tumor of vulvar Bartholin-type glands, often causing pain because of perineural invasion. It is usually slow-growing but may recur locally or metastasize after long intervals.

\medterm{Adenoid Removal} Surgical excision of the pharyngeal tonsils (adenoids) from the nasopharynx, typically performed in children for chronic nasal obstruction, recurrent otitis media, or obstructive sleep apnea unresponsive to medical management.

\medterm{Adenoidectomy} The surgical removal of the pharyngeal tonsils (adenoids), which are lymphoid tissue
structures located in the nasopharynx, and it is commonly performed in children for
obstructive sleep apnea, chronic nasal obstruction, recurrent otitis media, and chronic
rhinosinusitis that is refractory to medical management.

\medterm{Adenoiditis} Inflammation or infection of the adenoids, the pharyngeal tonsil behind the nose, which may cause nasal obstruction, mouth breathing, snoring, or ear problems.

\medterm{Adenoids} Lymphoid tissue in the posterior wall and roof of the nasopharynx, part of Waldeyer ring
of pharyngeal lymphoid tissue. Enlarged adenoids (adenoidal hypertrophy) in children
cause nasal obstruction, mouth breathing, snoring, obstructive sleep apnea, serous
otitis media (eustachian tube obstruction), and facial changes ('adenoid facies').

\medterm{Adenoids And Tonsils} The adenoids and tonsils are lymphoid tissues in the upper airway that participate in immune surveillance. Enlargement or recurrent infection can cause mouth breathing, snoring, sleep apnea, swallowing problems, or recurrent throat disease and may require medical or surgical evaluation.

\synonyms
Tonsil Inflammation (Adenoids And Tonsils)

\medterm{Adenolipoma} An adenolipoma is a usually benign tumor containing both glandular tissue and mature fatty tissue.

\medterm{Adenolymphoma} A benign salivary-gland tumor, also called Warthin tumor, composed of oncocytic epithelium and lymphoid stroma and occurring most often in the parotid gland.

\medterm{Adenoma} A benign tumour arising from glandular or gland-like epithelial tissue. Some adenomas,
particularly certain colorectal adenomas, can develop dysplasia and progress to
carcinoma.

\medterm{Adenoma Malignum} A rare, deceptively bland, deeply infiltrative gastric-type adenocarcinoma of the cervix with minimal cytologic atypia; persistent watery discharge may occur and diagnosis requires expert histopathology.

\medterm{Adenoma-Carcinoma Sequence} Established model of stepwise colorectal carcinogenesis progressing from normal
epithelium through adenomas to carcinoma over ten to fifteen years.

\medterm{Adenomatosis} Adenomatosis is the presence of multiple adenomas or diffuse adenomatous overgrowth in an organ or tissue; its implications depend on the site and underlying syndrome.

\medterm{Adenomatous Polyp} A benign gland-forming polyp, most commonly in the colon, that may contain dysplasia and
can progress through the adenoma--carcinoma sequence. Tubular, villous, and
tubulovillous types are recognized.

\medterm{Adenomyoepithelioma Of The Breast} A rare biphasic breast tumor composed of proliferating epithelial and myoepithelial cells. Most are benign, but local recurrence and malignant transformation can occur, so complete excision is usually recommended.

\medterm{Adenomyoma} A benign uterine lesion composed of endometrial glands and stroma within a proliferation of smooth muscle; it may cause abnormal uterine bleeding or mimic other uterine tumors.

\medterm{Adenomyosis} A condition in which endometrial glands and stroma are present within the myometrium
(uterine muscle). Most common in multiparous women in their 40s and 50s. Presents with
heavy menstrual bleeding (menorrhagia), severe dysmenorrhea, chronic pelvic pain, and
uterine enlargement.

\medterm{Adenopathy} Disease or enlargement of a gland, most often used for lymph-node enlargement or abnormal lymph-node involvement.

\medterm{Adenosine} A purine nucleoside made of adenine linked to ribose; it participates in cellular energy transfer and signaling and can transiently slow atrioventricular-node conduction when given medically.

\medterm{Adenosine Deaminase (ADA) Deficiency} An inherited purine-metabolism disorder in which toxic deoxyadenosine metabolites accumulate and damage lymphocytes, commonly causing severe combined immunodeficiency.

\medterm{Adenosine Diphosphate (ADP)} A nucleotide composed of adenine, ribose, and two phosphate groups. Formed by hydrolysis of ATP ($\mathrm{ATP} \rightarrow \mathrm{ADP} + \mathrm{P_i}$, $\Delta G^\circ = -30.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$). Serves as the immediate precursor for ATP
synthesis via oxidative phosphorylation (mitochondrial ATP synthase), substrate-level
phosphorylation (glycolysis, TCA cycle), and photophosphorylation.

\synonyms
Adenosine Diphosphate

\medterm{Adenosine Monophosphate (AMP)} A nucleotide composed of adenine, ribose, and a single phosphate group. Formed by hydrolysis of ADP (ADP -> AMP + Pi) or directly from ATP via adenylate kinase (2 ADP <->
ATP + AMP).

\synonyms
Adenosine Monophosphate

\medterm{Adenosine Triphosphate} A molecule that stores and transfers energy within cells. It is commonly abbreviated ATP.

\medterm{Adenosine Triphosphate (ATP)} ATP is the cell’s principal immediately usable energy carrier; hydrolysis of its high-energy phosphate bonds powers transport, movement, synthesis, and signaling.

\medterm{Adenosine Triphosphate (ATP)} The primary energy currency of the cell, composed of adenine, ribose, and three phosphate groups. Hydrolysis of the terminal phosphoanhydride bond releases $\Delta G^\circ = -30.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$
($-7.3\,\mathrm{kcal/mol}$), driving endergonic cellular processes such as muscle
contraction, active transport, and biosynthesis.

\synonyms
Adenosine Triphosphate

\medterm{Adenosis} A benign or abnormal increase in the number or size of glands or glandular tissue; the clinical meaning depends on the organ, such as breast adenosis or pulmonary adenosis.

\medterm{Adenovirus} A non-enveloped DNA virus that causes respiratory infections, conjunctivitis,
gastroenteritis, and cystitis. Adenoviruses are highly stable in the environment and can
cause outbreaks in closed populations. The virus has over 50 serotypes with different
tissue tropisms. Diagnosis is made by PCR, antigen detection, or viral culture.

\medterm{Adenovirus Infection} Infection with an adenovirus, commonly causing sore throat, cough, fever,
conjunctivitis, diarrhea, or vomiting. Most infections are self-limited, but severe respiratory or
disseminated disease can occur in infants and people with weakened immunity. Treatment is usually
supportive, with medical assessment for breathing difficulty, dehydration, or severe illness.

\medterm{Adenoviruses} Adenoviruses are non-enveloped DNA viruses that can cause respiratory, eye, gastrointestinal, urinary, or other infections, usually self-limited but sometimes severe in vulnerable people.

\medterm{Adequate Intake} A recommended average daily nutrient intake based on observed or experimentally determined levels in healthy people when evidence is insufficient to establish a recommended dietary allowance.

\synonyms
AI; adequate nutrient intake.

\medterm{Adherence} The extent to which a person follows an agreed treatment plan, including prescribed medicines and other care.
Adherence may be affected by cost, adverse effects, treatment complexity, health beliefs,
or difficulty accessing care.

\medterm{Adherence (Geriatric)} Adherence to an agreed treatment plan in an older adult, including medicine use, appointments, and
recommended self-care. Cognitive impairment, polypharmacy, adverse effects, cost, and
support needs may affect adherence.

\synonyms
Geriatric

\medterm{Adherens Junction} A cell-to-cell connection that uses cadherin proteins to link neighboring cells through actin filaments.

\medterm{Adhesiolysis} Surgical division or removal of abnormal fibrous adhesions that bind tissues or organs together, often after inflammation, infection, surgery, or trauma.

\medterm{Adhesion} A band of scar-like tissue that abnormally connects surfaces or organs inside the body.

\medterm{Adhesion Molecules} Cell-surface proteins that mediate attachment between cells or between cells and the extracellular matrix; they are important in leukocyte trafficking, inflammation, tissue organization, and metastasis.

\medterm{Adhesive Capsulitis} A condition causing progressive shoulder pain and stiffness due to inflammation and fibrosis of
the glenohumeral joint capsule. See Frozen Shoulder.

\medterm{Adhesive Capsulitis Of Shoulder (Frozen Shoulder)} Alternate index label for Frozen Shoulder.

\medterm{Adhesive Tape} A material used to secure dressings, devices, or tubing to the skin. It is available in paper, cloth, plastic, and other forms.

\medterm{ADI} Acceptable daily intake: the estimated amount of a substance that can be consumed every day over a lifetime without appreciable health risk, based on available evidence and safety factors.

\medterm{Adie Tonic Pupil} Uncommon cause of anisocoria, a benign parasympathetic denervation of the iris sphincter
and ciliary muscle, typically unilateral in young women, idiopathic or associated with
diabetes, herpes zoster, or autoimmune neuropathy.

\medterm{Adipocere (Grave Wax)} Waxy, grayish-white substance formed postmortem in warm, moist, anaerobic environments
(water, damp soil) by the hydrolysis and hydrogenation of body fat into free fatty
acids; retards putrefaction and preserves anatomical details for months to years.

\synonyms
Grave Wax

\medterm{Adipocyte} A cell specialized to store and release fat.

\synonyms
Fat cell

\medterm{Adipogenesis} The formation and maturation of fat cells.

\medterm{Adipokine} A signaling molecule released by adipose tissue that influences metabolism or inflammation.

\medterm{Adiponectin} A hormone produced mainly by adipose tissue that influences fatty-acid metabolism, insulin sensitivity, and inflammatory responses.

\medterm{Adipose} Relating to body fat or composed of adipose tissue, which stores energy and also functions as an endocrine and immune-active organ.

\medterm{Adipose Tissue} A specialized connective tissue composed mainly of adipocytes that store energy as
triglycerides. It provides thermal insulation, cushioning for organs, and endocrine
regulation through hormones and signaling molecules such as leptin and adiponectin.

\medterm{Adiposis Dolorosa} Adiposis dolorosa, or Dercum disease, is a rare disorder characterized by painful fatty deposits, often with obesity, fatigue, and other systemic symptoms.

\medterm{Adiposity} The amount or distribution of body fat; excess adiposity is associated with increased risk of metabolic, cardiovascular, and mechanical disease.

\medterm{Adipsia} Absent or markedly reduced thirst, which can impair normal fluid intake and lead to dehydration or hypernatremia.

\medterm{Adjacent} Next to, close to, or sharing a boundary with another structure, tissue, organ, or lesion.

\medterm{Adjustment Disorder} Emotional or behavioral symptoms in response to an identifiable stressor occurring
within three months of the onset of the stressor. The symptoms are disproportionate to
the severity of the stressor and cause significant distress or impairment. It resolves
within six months of the termination of the stressor.

\medterm{Adjustment Disorder With Anxiety} An emotional or behavioral response to an identifiable stressor that causes distress or
impairment but does not meet criteria for another mental disorder.

\medterm{Adjustment Disorders} Emotional or behavioral symptoms occurring in response to an identifiable stressor and causing significant distress or impairment.

\synonyms
Adjustment disorder

\medterm{Adjuvant} An added treatment or substance that enhances the effect of a primary treatment; in vaccines, an adjuvant strengthens the immune response to an antigen.

\medterm{Adjuvant Therapy} Additional treatment after primary treatment to reduce recurrence risk. Examples:
chemotherapy after surgery.

\medterm{ADL Training} Training for activities of daily living: dressing, bathing, grooming, feeding,
toileting. Adaptive techniques, equipment.

\medterm{ADLs (Activities Of Daily Living)} Basic self-care activities such as bathing, dressing, toileting, transferring, eating, and maintaining continence; ADL ability is used to assess functional independence.

\medterm{ADME} Absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion: the processes governing a substance’s movement through the body.

\synonyms
Pharmacokinetics

\medterm{Administered Activity} The measured amount of radioactive material given to a patient for imaging or therapy,
usually expressed in becquerels. It should be prescribed and recorded with the
radionuclide, radiopharmaceutical, route, date, and time of administration.

\medterm{Administration Procedure} A procedure used to deliver a medicine, fluid, vaccine, or other intervention to a patient.

\medterm{Admission} The process of accepting a patient into a hospital or other facility for evaluation, treatment, monitoring, or care.

\medterm{Adnexa} Structures adjacent to an organ; in gynecology, the uterine adnexa include the ovaries, fallopian tubes, and supporting tissues.

\medterm{Adnexal Dysplasia} Abnormal precancerous or developmental cellular changes in tissue beside the uterus, such as the ovary or tube.

\medterm{Adnexal Mass} A growth or enlargement involving structures beside the uterus, such as an ovary or fallopian tube.

\medterm{Adnexal Tumors And Masses} Abnormal growths or masses involving the ovary, fallopian tube, or nearby supporting tissues; they may be benign or malignant.

\medterm{Adnexitis} Inflammation of an adnexal structure, usually the fallopian tube or ovary.

\synonyms
Salpingo-oophoritis

\medterm{Adolescence} The developmental period between childhood and adulthood, involving physical maturation, puberty, and major psychological and social changes.

\medterm{Adolescent Confidentiality And Consent} The principles governing private healthcare discussions with adolescents and their ability to participate in medical decisions. The exact rules for confidentiality, consent, and parental involvement depend on age, capacity, local law, and safeguarding concerns.

\medterm{Adolescent Development} The physical, cognitive, emotional, and social changes that occur as a person develops from childhood into adulthood, including puberty and the development of greater independence.

\medterm{Adolescent Health} The health and wellbeing of young people (10-19 years), addressing the physical,
psychological, and social transitions of puberty and adolescence.

\medterm{Adolescent Medicine} A medical specialty focused on the physical, mental, reproductive, and social health needs of adolescents and young adults, including prevention, risk behavior, chronic disease, and confidentiality-sensitive care.

\medterm{Adolescent Obesity} Obesity during adolescence, assessed using age- and sex-specific growth charts rather than adult BMI thresholds alone. It may affect metabolic, cardiovascular, orthopedic, and psychological health.

\medterm{Adolescent Schizophrenia} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Childhood schizophrenia}.

\medterm{Adolescent Test Or Procedure Preparation} Age-appropriate preparation of an adolescent for a medical test or procedure, including explanation of the procedure, expectations, sensation, and coping strategies. It aims to reduce anxiety, improve cooperation, and support informed assent.

\medterm{Adoption} In healthcare and genetics, adoption may be relevant to family history, consent, psychosocial care, and assessment when biological family information is unavailable or differs from the caregiving family.

\medterm{Adoptive Cell Therapy} A personalized cellular immunotherapy in which a patient's own lymphocytes are expanded
or engineered ex vivo and reinfused to attack the tumor; examples include
tumor-infiltrating lymphocyte (TIL) therapy and chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell
therapy.

\medterm{Adoptive Immunotherapy} Treatment in which immune cells are collected, modified or activated, and returned to the patient.

\medterm{ADP-Ribosylation} Addition of an ADP-ribose group to a protein or other molecule, altering its activity or interactions.

\medterm{ADPKD} Alternate index label; see \textit{Autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease}.

\medterm{Adrenal Cancer} Cancer arising in the adrenal gland.

\synonyms
Adrenocortical cancer

\medterm{Adrenal Cortex} The outer part of an adrenal gland that produces steroid hormones, including cortisol and aldosterone.

\medterm{Adrenal Cortex Physiology} The adrenal cortex produces steroid hormones in three zones: the zona glomerulosa produces
mineralocorticoids, mainly aldosterone; the zona fasciculata produces glucocorticoids,
mainly cortisol; and the zona reticularis produces adrenal androgens. Secretion is
regulated by the renin-angiotensin system, plasma potassium, and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis.

\medterm{Adrenal Cortical Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor arising from the adrenal cortex. It may be functional,
producing excess adrenal hormones, or nonfunctional.

\medterm{Adrenal Crisis} An acute, life-threatening manifestation of severe adrenal insufficiency. Causes:
primary (Addison disease), secondary (pituitary failure), or tertiary (exogenous
corticosteroid withdrawal). Precipitants: systemic infection, trauma, surgery, or abrupt
steroid cessation.

\medterm{Adrenal Failure} Inadequate production of adrenal hormones, especially cortisol and sometimes aldosterone; severe acute deficiency can cause adrenal crisis with hypotension and shock.

\medterm{Adrenal Gland} Paired endocrine glands atop each kidney (suprarenal), composed of cortex and medulla.
Cortex (three zones): zona glomerulosa (aldosterone, mineralocorticoids), zona
fasciculata (cortisol, glucocorticoids), zona reticularis (androgens). Medulla:
chromaffin cells secrete epinephrine and norepinephrine (catecholamines).

\medterm{Adrenal Gland Removal} Surgical excision of an adrenal gland, performed for tumors, hyperplasia, or other adrenal disorders. Approaches include open, laparoscopic, or retroperitoneal; perioperative management requires assessment of hormone production and potential adrenal insufficiency.

\medterm{Adrenal Glands} Adrenal glands sit above the kidneys and produce cortisol, aldosterone, adrenal androgens, and catecholamines that regulate stress, blood pressure, salt balance, and metabolism.

\medterm{Adrenal Hemorrhage} Bleeding into the adrenal gland most commonly occurring in critically ill patients with
sepsis, coagulopathy, or anticoagulation therapy. Bilateral adrenal hemorrhage may cause
acute adrenal insufficiency with circulatory collapse. Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome:
adrenal hemorrhage associated with meningococcal sepsis.

\medterm{Adrenal Hyperplasia} Enlargement or overgrowth of adrenal tissue that may increase production of adrenal
hormones. Causes include congenital adrenal hyperplasia, chronic stimulation by ACTH, and some
adrenal tumors. Symptoms depend on the hormone affected and may include abnormal genital development,
early puberty, excess body hair, high blood pressure, or electrolyte disturbances.

\medterm{Adrenal Insufficiency} Deficient production of cortisol by the adrenal cortex classified as primary adrenal
insufficiency or Addison disease from adrenal gland destruction, secondary from
pituitary ACTH deficiency, and tertiary from chronic exogenous glucocorticoid
suppression of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis.

\medterm{Adrenal Mass} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Benign adrenal tumors}.

\medterm{Adrenal Medulla} The inner part of the adrenal gland that produces epinephrine and norepinephrine.

\medterm{Adrenal Medulla Physiology} The adrenal medulla produces catecholamines primarily epinephrine and norepinephrine in
response to sympathetic nervous system activation. Epinephrine accounts for
approximately eighty percent and norepinephrine approximately twenty percent of
catecholamine output.

\medterm{Adrenal Myelolipoma} A usually benign adrenal tumor composed of mature fat and blood-forming tissue. It is often
found incidentally on imaging and causes no symptoms, but larger lesions may produce mass
effect or bleeding. Management depends on size, symptoms, growth, and diagnostic
certainty.

\medterm{Adrenal Tumors} Tumors arising in the adrenal cortex or medulla may be benign or malignant and may or may not produce
hormones. Evaluation uses clinical assessment, hormone testing when indicated, and
imaging to assess size and appearance; treatment depends on the diagnosis, hormone
secretion, symptoms, growth, and risk of malignancy.

\medterm{Adrenal Venous Sampling} Interventional radiology procedure for lateralizing aldosterone secretion in primary
hyperaldosteronism. Catheterization of both adrenal veins allows measurement of
aldosterone and cortisol levels to calculate selectivity index confirming successful
cannulation and lateralization ratio.

\medterm{Adrenalectomy} Surgical removal of one or both adrenal glands, performed for selected adrenal tumors,
severe hormone excess, or other localized disease. Removal of one gland may leave adequate hormone
production; bilateral removal causes permanent adrenal insufficiency and requires lifelong hormone
replacement and emergency-steroid planning.

\medterm{Adrenaline} Epinephrine, a hormone and neurotransmitter released during stress that increases heart rate and contractility, constricts some blood vessels, and relaxes bronchial muscle.

\medterm{Adrenergic Agent} A substance that affects receptors responsive to adrenaline-like neurotransmitters.

\medterm{Adrenergic Agonist} A substance that activates adrenergic receptors.

\synonyms
Sympathomimetic

\medterm{Adrenergic Antagonists} Substances that block the effects of adrenaline-like signaling at adrenergic receptors.

\synonyms
Adrenergic blockers

\medterm{Adrenergic Bronchodilator Overdose} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to Adrenergic bronchodilator. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Adrenergic Receptor} A cell receptor activated by epinephrine or norepinephrine.

\medterm{Adrenergic Receptors} Cell-surface receptors activated by adrenaline and noradrenaline. Alpha and beta subtypes regulate cardiovascular, respiratory, metabolic, and other physiological responses.

\medterm{Adrenocortical Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor of the adrenal cortex that may secrete cortisol, androgens, or
aldosterone or be non-functional. Often large at diagnosis; treatment is en bloc
resection, with mitotane for advanced disease.

\medterm{Adrenocorticotrophic Hormone (ACTH)} ACTH is a pituitary hormone that stimulates the adrenal cortex to produce cortisol and some adrenal androgens; testing helps evaluate pituitary and adrenal disorders.
\medterm{Adrenocorticotrophic Hormone (ACTH)} Adrenocorticotrophic hormone, or ACTH, is released by the pituitary and stimulates the adrenal cortex, especially cortisol production; abnormal levels help localize endocrine disease.

\medterm{Adrenocorticotropic Hormone} ACTH (corticotropin): a peptide hormone from the anterior pituitary (cleaved from POMC)
that stimulates the adrenal cortex to produce cortisol (zona fasciculata) and adrenal
androgens; regulated by hypothalamic CRH and cortisol negative feedback (circadian).

\medterm{Adrenogenital Syndrome} A group of disorders, usually caused by congenital adrenal hyperplasia, in which excess adrenal androgens cause atypical genital development or early puberty.

\medterm{Adrenoleukodystrophy} An inherited disorder causing abnormal buildup of very-long-chain fatty acids and progressive nervous-system or adrenal damage.

\synonyms
ALD

\medterm{Adrenomedullin} A peptide hormone that promotes blood-vessel dilation and helps regulate circulation and fluid balance.

\medterm{Adsorption} Attachment of molecules to the surface of a solid or liquid rather than absorption into its interior.

\medterm{ADU} Accessory dwelling unit, a secondary independent residential unit located on the same property as a primary home.

\medterm{Adult Day Services} Community-based daytime programs providing supervision, social activities, health-related support, or assistance with daily activities for adults who cannot safely remain alone throughout the day.

\synonyms
Adult day care; adult day program.

\medterm{Adult Polycystic Kidney Disease} An inherited disorder, usually autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease, in which multiple kidney cysts enlarge over time and may cause hypertension, pain, hematuria, and chronic kidney failure.

\medterm{Adult Primary Liver Cancer} Cancer that begins in the liver of an adult, most commonly hepatocellular carcinoma or intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma, rather than cancer that has spread to the liver from another organ.

\medterm{Adult Rickets} Defective bone mineralization in adulthood, usually called osteomalacia.

\synonyms
Osteomalacia

\medterm{Adult Soft Tissue Sarcoma} A malignant tumor arising from connective or supporting tissues, such as muscle, fat, fibrous tissue, blood vessels, or peripheral nerves. It often presents as a painless enlarging mass and requires imaging and biopsy for diagnosis.

\medterm{Adult Spinal Deformity} Abnormal curvature or alignment of the spine in an adult, including scoliosis, kyphosis, or loss of
normal sagittal balance. It may cause back pain, nerve compression, difficulty walking,
or functional disability.

\medterm{Adult Still Disease} A systemic inflammatory disorder characterized by fever, joint pain or arthritis, and a transient salmon-colored rash.

\medterm{Adult Still's Disease} A systemic inflammatory disorder characterized by recurrent high fevers, arthritis or joint pain, a transient
salmon-colored rash, sore throat, and possible involvement of other organs.

\medterm{Adult-Onset Asthma} Adult-onset asthma is variable airway inflammation and narrowing that begins in adulthood, producing episodic wheezing, cough, chest tightness, or shortness of breath. Diagnosis uses symptoms and lung-function testing, and treatment reduces triggers and airway inflammation.

\synonyms
Asthma Adult-Onset (Adult-Onset Asthma)

\medterm{Adult-Onset Still Disease} A systemic inflammatory disorder beginning in adulthood, characterized by high-spiking fevers, arthritis or
joint pain, a transient salmon-pink rash, sore throat, and possible liver, spleen, heart, or blood-clotting complications.

\medterm{Adults Autism (Autism And Communication)} Alternate index label for Autism and Communication.

\medterm{Advance Directive} Legal document expressing healthcare wishes. Components: living will (treatment
preferences), durable power of attorney for healthcare (surrogate decision-maker).
Should be reviewed periodically, discussed with surrogate and healthcare team.

\medterm{Advance Directives} Written instructions about a person’s future medical-care preferences and/or designation of a health-care proxy if the person later cannot make or communicate decisions.

\medterm{Advance Directives (Advance Medical Directives)} Alternate index label for Advance Medical Directives.

\medterm{Advance Medical Directives} Advance medical directives record a person's preferences for future medical care and identify a health-care proxy when the person cannot decide. They may address resuscitation, life support, artificial nutrition, and other treatments, and should be reviewed as circumstances change.

\synonyms
Advance Directives (Advance Medical Directives)

\medterm{Advance Statements About Medical Treatment} Advance statements about medical treatment record a person’s preferences, values, and wishes for future care if illness later prevents effective communication; legal status varies by jurisdiction.

\medterm{Advanced Cancer} Cancer that has grown beyond its original site. It may be locally advanced when it has
invaded nearby tissues or metastatic when it has spread to distant organs.

\medterm{Advanced Cardiac Life Support} A structured set of interventions for cardiac arrest and other life-threatening cardiovascular emergencies, including high-quality CPR, rhythm recognition, defibrillation, airway management, and medications.

\medterm{Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support} A standardized set of advanced resuscitation measures for cardiac arrest and other
life-threatening cardiovascular emergencies. It includes high-quality CPR, defibrillation
when indicated, airway management, medicines, and treatment of reversible causes.

\medterm{Advanced Maternal Age} Maternal age of 35 years or older at the estimated time of delivery. It is associated with increased risks of
some chromosomal conditions and pregnancy complications.

\medterm{Advanced Sleep-Wake Phase Disorder} A circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorder in which a person becomes sleepy and falls asleep earlier than desired and wakes earlier than desired; symptoms persist despite an opportunity for a preferred schedule.

\medterm{Advantages And Disadvantages Of A Hospital Birth} A hospital birth provides immediate access to obstetric, anesthesia, newborn, and surgical services, which can be important when complications arise. Tradeoffs may include more routine interventions, less control over setting, and separation or transfer policies that vary by facility.

\medterm{Advantages And Disadvantages Of Hysterectomy} Hysterectomy removes the uterus and permanently ends menstruation and the ability to carry a pregnancy. It may treat cancer, fibroids, severe bleeding, adenomyosis, endometriosis, chronic pelvic pain, or prolapse. Benefits and risks depend on the indication and surgical approach and may include bleeding, infection, injury to nearby organs, blood clots, recovery time, and possible effects on ovarian function or pelvic support.

\medterm{Adventitia} The outer connective-tissue layer of a blood vessel or hollow organ.

\synonyms
Tunica adventitia

\medterm{Adventitious} Abnormal, unexpected, or arising from an unusual location or process; adventitious lung sounds include crackles, wheezes, and other added breath sounds.

\medterm{Adverse Effect} An unintended and harmful or bothersome effect of a medicine or other treatment given at normal doses or for an intended purpose.

\medterm{Adverse Event} Any untoward medical occurrence during or after a treatment or intervention, whether or
not it is considered causally related to that treatment. Severity, seriousness,
expectedness, and relatedness are assessed separately.

\medterm{Adverse Reaction} An unintended, harmful response to a medicine, treatment, or other exposure.

\synonyms
Adverse drug reaction

\medterm{Adverse Reactions To Drugs} Adverse drug reactions are harmful, unintended responses to medicines taken at normal doses, ranging from predictable side effects to allergic, organ-toxic, or idiosyncratic reactions.

\medterm{AE} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Autoimmune encephalitis}.

\medterm{Aedes Aegypti} Aedes aegypti is a mosquito that can transmit dengue, yellow fever, chikungunya, Zika, and other viruses.

\medterm{Aegophony} Aegophony is an altered “E” sound heard through a stethoscope as an “A,” classically suggesting lung consolidation or fluid-related change when present over the chest.

\medterm{Aerobe} A microorganism that requires molecular oxygen for growth or uses oxygen efficiently for
energy production. Obligate aerobes are unable to grow without oxygen, whereas
facultative organisms can grow with or without it.

\medterm{Aerobic} Requiring or involving molecular oxygen. In microbiology, aerobic organisms use oxygen
for growth or energy production, either obligatorily or as one option among several; in
physiology, aerobic metabolism uses oxidative phosphorylation in mitochondria to
generate ATP from nutrients.

\medterm{Aerobic Bacteria} Bacteria that grow using oxygen or tolerate oxygen, although individual species differ in their exact oxygen requirements.

\medterm{Aerobic Bacterium} An aerobic bacterium grows using oxygen, although some species can tolerate or use alternative metabolic pathways; oxygen requirements help guide culture and infection assessment.

\medterm{Aerobic Exercise} Physical activity that uses sustained oxygen-dependent energy production, such as walking or cycling.

\synonyms
Cardio exercise

\medterm{Aerobiosis} A state or process requiring the presence of oxygen.

\medterm{Aerophagia} Excessive swallowing of air, which may cause belching, abdominal bloating, distention, or increased intestinal gas.

\medterm{Aerophagy} Excessive swallowing of air, also called aerophagia, which can lead to frequent
belching, abdominal bloating, distension, and discomfort.

\synonyms
aerophagia, which can lead to frequent.

\medterm{Aerophobia} An excessive fear of flying or air travel that causes significant anxiety, avoidance, or impairment.

\medterm{Aerosol} A suspension of fine solid particles or liquid droplets in a gas.

\medterm{Aerotitis} Ear pain or middle-ear injury caused by failure to equalize pressure during rapid changes in altitude, such as during aircraft ascent or descent.

\medterm{Aesculapius} The Roman name for Asclepius, the Greek god of healing; the name is associated with medicine and the rod-and-serpent medical symbol.

\medterm{Aesthetic Dentistry} Dental care intended to improve the appearance of the teeth, gums, or smile. Procedures may include
tooth-colored restorations, veneers, whitening, or selected crowns.

\medterm{Aesthetic Medicine} Medical care intended to improve appearance or reduce visible signs of aging through procedures, devices, medicines, or skin care; appropriate assessment includes risks, contraindications, and realistic expectations.

\medterm{Aesthetician} A trained skin-care professional who provides nonmedical cosmetic skin treatments, such as cleansing, superficial exfoliation, and selected skin-care procedures within the scope of local regulation.

\synonyms
Esthetician; skin-care specialist.

\medterm{Aetiology} The cause or causes of a disease, injury, symptom, or other medical condition; also spelled etiology.

\medterm{Aetiology (Etiology)} The cause or group of causes underlying a disease, injury, or other medical condition.

\synonyms
Etiology

\medterm{Affect} The observable expression of a person’s emotional state, including facial expression, voice, posture, and emotional responsiveness.

\medterm{Affective Flattening} Markedly reduced outward expression of emotion, including limited facial expression, voice modulation, and gestures.
It may occur in schizophrenia and other psychiatric or neurologic conditions.

\medterm{Afferent} Carrying signals toward a central organ or structure; afferent nerves transmit sensory information from the body toward the brain and spinal cord.

\medterm{Afferent Loop Syndrome} Obstruction or impaired emptying of the afferent intestinal limb created by some gastric operations, causing bile and pancreatic-secretions to accumulate with pain, nausea, vomiting, or malabsorption.

\medterm{Afferent Neuron} A sensory nerve cell that carries impulses from receptors toward the central nervous system.

\medterm{Afferent Pathway} A sensory nerve route carrying information from the body toward the brain or spinal cord.

\synonyms
Sensory pathway

\medterm{Affinity} The strength of attraction or binding between two molecules, such as an antibody and antigen, receptor and ligand, or enzyme and substrate.

\medterm{Affinity Chromatography} A laboratory separation method that isolates a molecule through its specific binding to an immobilized partner.

\medterm{Affordable Care Act (ACA Or ObamaCare)} Alternate index label for ACA or ObamaCare.

\medterm{Affordable Care Act (ACA Or ObamaCare) (ACA)} Alternate index label for Affordable Care Act (ACA or ObamaCare).
\medterm{Afib (Atrial Fibrillation (AFib) Warning Symptoms)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation (AFib) Warning Symptoms.

\medterm{Afib (Atrial Fibrillation)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation.


\medterm{Afibrinogenaemia} Afibrinogenaemia is severe or complete deficiency of fibrinogen, an essential clotting protein, causing a bleeding tendency; it is usually inherited but may be acquired.

\medterm{Afibrinogenemia} A rare inherited or acquired absence of fibrinogen, causing impaired blood clotting.

\medterm{Aflatoxin} Aflatoxin is a toxin produced by certain Aspergillus molds in contaminated foods; chronic exposure can injure the liver and increase hepatocellular-carcinoma risk.

\medterm{AFM} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Acute flaccid myelitis (AFM)}.

\medterm{African Trypanosomiasis} A parasitic infection transmitted by tsetse flies and affecting the blood and nervous system.

\synonyms
African sleeping sickness

\medterm{After A C-Section - In The Hospital} Inpatient care after cesarean delivery, including wound monitoring, pain management, early ambulation, uterine bleeding assessment, and initiation of breastfeeding. Complications to watch for include infection, hemorrhage, and venous thromboembolism.

\medterm{After A Fall In The Hospital} Assessment and management of a patient who falls during hospitalization, including injury evaluation, neurological observation, and prevention of future falls. It addresses environmental modifications, medication review, and mobility support.

\medterm{After A Positive Ovulation Test Are You Fertile?} A positive ovulation test detects a rise in luteinizing hormone that usually precedes ovulation by about one to two days. Fertility is highest around this interval, but timing varies and the test does not confirm that an egg was released.

\medterm{After An Exposure To Sharps Or Body Fluids} Post-exposure evaluation after possible contact with blood or body fluids, including wound care, source assessment, and timely consideration of HIV, hepatitis B, or hepatitis C prevention and testing.

\medterm{After Endolymphatic Sac Decompression Surgery} After endolymphatic sac decompression, follow-up focuses on wound healing, vertigo, hearing, balance, and recurrence of inner-ear symptoms. Activity and ear-care instructions depend on the operation, and persistent severe vertigo, drainage, fever, or facial weakness needs prompt review.

\medterm{After Vaginal Delivery - In The Hospital} Inpatient care following vaginal delivery, including monitoring of uterine involution, lochia, perineal wound care, pain management, and initiation of breastfeeding. Assessment for postpartum hemorrhage, infection, and mood changes is part of routine care.

\medterm{After-Cataract} Clouding of the posterior lens capsule that develops after cataract surgery and may cause blurred or hazy vision. It is also called posterior capsule opacification.

\synonyms
Posterior capsule opacification; secondary cataract.

\medterm{Afterbirth} The placenta and fetal membranes expelled from the uterus after delivery of the baby.

\medterm{Afterload} The resistance against which the ventricles must eject blood during systole. Left
ventricular afterload is primarily determined by systemic vascular resistance (SVR);
right ventricular afterload is determined by pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR).
Afterload is estimated by blood pressure or calculated as wall stress.

\medterm{Afterpains} Intermittent uterine cramps occurring after childbirth as the uterus contracts and returns toward its pre-pregnancy size. They may be more noticeable during breastfeeding.

\medterm{Aftershave Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to aftershave products. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Agammaglobulinaemia} Agammaglobulinaemia is profound deficiency of immunoglobulins and mature B cells, leading to recurrent bacterial infections; X-linked disease is a classic form.

\medterm{Agammaglobulinemia} A severe deficiency or absence of immunoglobulins, causing recurrent infections.

\medterm{Agar} A gelatinous polysaccharide from red algae used to solidify culture media. Melting near
85°C and solidifying at 35--40°C, it remains solid at incubation temperatures and is
largely undegraded by bacteria, enabling isolation of pure colonies on media such as
blood, MacConkey, and Sabouraud agars.

\medterm{Age By Decade} Classification of a person’s age into ten-year groups, such as the twenties, thirties, or seventies, for demographic description or analysis.

\medterm{Age Related Macular Degeneration (Macular Degeneration)} Alternate index label for Macular Degeneration.

\medterm{Age Spots (Liver Spots)} Flat brown, gray, or black skin spots caused by long-term ultraviolet exposure, usually appearing on sun-exposed areas.

\synonyms
Lentigines, Solar
Solar Lentigines

\medterm{Age-Related Cognitive Decline} A modest, age-associated change in memory, processing speed, or other cognitive abilities that does not necessarily interfere substantially with independent daily functioning.

\synonyms
Age-associated cognitive decline; normal cognitive aging.

\medterm{Age-Related Hearing Loss} Presbycusis is bilateral, progressive sensorineural hearing loss associated with aging. It primarily affects high-frequency sounds and results from degeneration of cochlear hair cells, the stria vascularis, and auditory nerve fibers. It is the most common cause of hearing impairment in older adults.

\medterm{Age-Related Macular Degeneration} A degenerative disorder of the macula that can impair central vision in older adults.
The dry form involves retinal and pigment-cell changes and may progress gradually; the
neovascular form involves abnormal blood-vessel growth and may cause more rapid vision
loss. Risk is influenced by age, smoking, family history, and other factors.

\medterm{Age-Related Macular Degeneration And Retinal Disease} Age-related macular degeneration is a degenerative disease of the macula causing central-vision loss; retinal diseases also include vascular, inflammatory, inherited, and tractional disorders requiring ophthalmic evaluation.

\medterm{Age-Related Macular Degeneration, Dry} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Dry macular degeneration}.

\medterm{Age-Related Macular Degeneration, Wet} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Wet macular degeneration}.

\medterm{Age-Standardized Rate} A rate adjusted to a specified standard population age distribution so that populations
with different age structures can be compared more fairly. It is commonly used for
mortality, cancer, and disease-incidence comparisons.

\medterm{Ageing} Ageing is the progressive biological and functional change over time, including altered repair, immunity, metabolism, organ reserve, and susceptibility to disease.

\medterm{Ageism} Stereotyping, prejudice, or discrimination directed at people because of their age,
which can impair access to appropriate medical care.

\medterm{Agenesis} Complete failure of an organ or body structure to develop, usually because of a congenital developmental abnormality.

\medterm{Agenesis Of The Corpus Callosum} Complete or partial absence of the corpus callosum, the major commissural fiber bundle
connecting the cerebral hemispheres. May be isolated or associated with other CNS
anomalies (Dandy-Walker, Chiari, heterotopias) and genetic syndromes (Aicardi,
Andermann, acrocallosal syndromes).

\medterm{Ageusia} Complete loss of the sense of taste. Causes include viral infections, oral or nasal
disease, head injury, damage to the facial or glossopharyngeal nerves, medications,
chemotherapy, zinc deficiency, and neurologic disorders.

\medterm{Agglutination} The visible clumping of cells or particles caused by antibodies or other binding
molecules linking antigens on their surfaces. It is used in blood-grouping and
crossmatching, infectious-disease serology, and some laboratory identification tests.

\medterm{Agglutination Test} A laboratory test in which particles visibly clump when a specific antibody or antigen is present.

\medterm{Agglutinin} An antibody or other substance that causes cells or particles to clump together.

\medterm{Agglutinogen} An agglutinogen is an antigen, often on a red blood cell, that can trigger production of a corresponding agglutinin and cause visible cell clumping in a compatible reaction.

\medterm{Aggregation, Platelet} The process by which activated platelets adhere to one another to form a primary
platelet plug at a site of vascular injury. Platelet agonists such as ADP, thromboxane
A2, and thrombin activate glycoprotein IIb/IIIa, which binds fibrinogen and links
adjacent platelets.

\medterm{Aggression} Verbal or physical behavior intended to threaten, harm, or dominate another person or
object. It may occur with mania, psychosis, personality disorders, dementia, delirium,
substance intoxication or withdrawal, traumatic brain injury, or severe frustration.

\medterm{Aggressive} Characterized by forceful or hostile behavior, or by rapid growth and invasive or destructive clinical behavior, depending on context.

\medterm{Aggressive Angiomyxoma} A locally infiltrative, usually slow-growing mesenchymal tumor of the pelvis and perineum, predominantly affecting reproductive-age women. It may be mistaken for a Bartholin cyst and has a tendency to recur.

\medterm{Aggressive Cancer} A cancer that grows, invades, or spreads rapidly and may require prompt diagnosis and
treatment. Aggressiveness depends on tumour biology, grade, stage, and response to
therapy.

\medterm{Agility} The ability to move or change direction quickly while maintaining control and balance.

\medterm{Aging} Biological process of growing older, involving cumulative changes at molecular,
cellular, tissue, organ levels.

\medterm{Aging Spots - Should You Be Concerned?} A topic about age-related lentigines, which are usually flat brown skin patches caused by cumulative sun exposure. A changing or bleeding lesion requires assessment.

\medterm{Agita} An informal term for heartburn, indigestion, or an uneasy feeling of stomach discomfort; its meaning can vary by region and context.

\medterm{Agitation} A state of excessive motor activity, emotional excitement, and restlessness. In
psychiatric settings, it may be a symptom of mania, psychosis, delirium, substance
intoxication, or withdrawal.

\medterm{Agnathia} A rare congenital absence or severe underdevelopment of the mandible, maxilla, or both.
It is usually part of a craniofacial developmental disorder and may cause profound
facial deformity, airway obstruction, feeding difficulty, and impaired dentition.

\medterm{Agnogenic Myeloid Metaplasia} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Myelofibrosis}.

\medterm{Agnosia} A higher-order neurological deficit characterized by the inability to recognize or
process sensory stimuli (visual, auditory, tactile) despite intact primary sensory
organs and pathways. Caused by focal lesions in specific cortical association areas
(parietal, temporal, or occipital lobes).

\medterm{Agonist} A substance that binds to a receptor and activates it, producing a biological response.
A full agonist can produce the receptor's maximum response, whereas a partial agonist
produces a lower maximum response even when it occupies all available receptors.

\medterm{Agoraphobia} Anxiety disorder characterized by fear and avoidance of situations where escape might be
difficult or help unavailable during a panic-like episode. Common feared situations
include public transportation, open spaces, enclosed spaces, standing in line, being in
crowds, and being outside the home alone.

\medterm{Agranulocytes} White blood cells without prominent cytoplasmic granules; the main types are lymphocytes and monocytes.

\medterm{Agranulocytosis} A severe reduction or near-absence of circulating neutrophils, usually defined as an
absolute neutrophil count below $0.5\times10^9/\mathrm{L}$. It may be caused by medications,
chemotherapy, autoimmune disease, infections, or bone-marrow disorders and presents with
fever, sore throat, oral ulcers, or recurrent serious infections.

\medterm{Agraphesthesia} Inability to recognize numbers or letters traced on the skin, indicating a lesion of the
contralateral parietal lobe (somatosensory association cortex, postcentral gyrus).
Tested with a blunt object on the palm or fingertip while the patient's eyes are closed.

\medterm{Agraphia} An acquired inability to write or express language in written form despite adequate
education and motor ability. It may result from dominant-parietal or language-network
lesions, especially stroke, traumatic brain injury, dementia, or brain tumors, and may
occur with aphasia, alexia, or acalculia.

\medterm{Ague} A fever, often associated with malaria, marked by recurrent episodes of chills, fever, and sweating; the term can also mean a fit of shivering.

\medterm{Agyrophobia} An excessive, persistent fear of crossing streets, highways, or other thoroughfares, causing anxiety or avoidance despite limited objective danger.

\medterm{Atherosclerotic Heart Disease (AHD)} A condition in which fatty deposits called plaques narrow or block the arteries that supply
blood to the heart. It is a form of coronary artery disease and may cause chest pain, heart attack, or other heart problems.
\medterm{AI} In medicine, AI means artificial intelligence: computer methods that analyze data or recognize patterns to support research, diagnosis, prediction, treatment, or clinical workflow; they require validation and oversight.

\medterm{Aicardi Syndrome} Aicardi Syndrome is a rare developmental brain disorder classically involving agenesis of the corpus callosum, chorioretinal lacunae, and infantile seizures.

\medterm{Aicardi-Goutieres Syndrome} Rare autosomal recessive inflammatory disorder mimicking congenital viral infection.
Characterized by cerebral calcifications, white matter disease, cerebral atrophy, and
cerebrospinal fluid lymphocytosis. Presents with severe neurodevelopmental impairment,
seizures, and spasticity.

\medterm{AIDP} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Guillain-Barre syndrome}.

\medterm{AIDS} Acquired immunodeficiency syndrome: the advanced stage of HIV infection, defined by CD4
count <200 cells/$\mu$L or the presence of AIDS-defining illnesses.

\synonyms
Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (AIDS)

\medterm{AIDS Dementia} A disorder of thinking, memory, behavior, or movement caused by advanced HIV-related disease affecting the brain.


\medterm{AIDS-Associated Nephropathy} Kidney injury associated with advanced HIV infection.

\synonyms
HIV-associated nephropathy

\medterm{AIDS/HIV} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{HIV/AIDS}.

\medterm{Ainhum} A rare disorder causing a constricting groove around a toe that may eventually lead to autoamputation.

\synonyms
Dactylolysis spontanea

\medterm{Air Abrasion} Minimally invasive caries removal using abrasive particles. Alternative to drilling for
small lesions.

\medterm{Air Bag} An inflatable vehicle safety device that rapidly deploys during a collision to reduce impact with the steering wheel, dashboard, or window; it supplements seat belts.

\medterm{Air Bronchogram} A radiographic sign showing air-filled bronchi visible against opacified lung
parenchyma, indicating that the airways remain patent while the surrounding alveoli are
filled with fluid, pus, or cells.

\medterm{Air Contaminants} Solid, liquid, or gaseous substances in air that may harm health, such as particulate matter, fumes, gases, allergens,
or microorganisms. Effects depend on the contaminant, concentration, and duration of exposure.

\medterm{Air Embolism} Entry of gas into the venous or arterial circulation, where it can obstruct blood flow and injure tissues. It may
occur during medical procedures, trauma, or rapid changes in environmental pressure.
Symptoms and treatment depend on the amount and location of gas and require urgent
clinical assessment when significant embolism is suspected.

\medterm{Air Passages} The air passages are the conducting airways from the nose and mouth through the pharynx, larynx, trachea, bronchi, and bronchioles; obstruction can impair ventilation.

\medterm{Air Polishing} Removal of stains using air-abrasive device. Glycine or erythritol powder.

\medterm{Air Quality} The condition of air as determined by the concentration of pollutants such as particulate matter, ozone, nitrogen dioxide, and sulfur dioxide.

\medterm{Air Sac} A cavity or thin-walled space that contains air, such as a pulmonary alveolus or bird air sac.

\synonyms
Air space

\medterm{Air Trapping} Retention of air in the lungs because it cannot be fully exhaled, often from airway obstruction.

\medterm{Air-Conditioner Lung} Hypersensitivity pneumonitis caused by inhaled mold, fungus, or other contaminants from air-conditioning or humidifying systems.

\medterm{Air-Sickness} Air-sickness is motion sickness triggered by aircraft movement or conflicting visual and vestibular signals, causing nausea, dizziness, sweating, and sometimes vomiting.

\medterm{Airborne Disease} An infection that can spread through small suspended particles or aerosols under suitable conditions. Transmission depends on
the organism, environment, ventilation, and duration of exposure.

\medterm{Airborne Precautions} Infection-control measures for infections that can remain suspended in the air and spread over a distance.
They include an airborne infection isolation room with negative pressure, limiting patient movement, and
appropriate respiratory protection, often a fit-tested N95 or higher-level respirator. They are used for tuberculosis,
measles, chickenpox, and disseminated shingles.

\medterm{Airplane Ear} Ear pain, pressure, or temporary hearing loss caused by unequal air pressure across the eardrum during flight.

\synonyms
Barotitis Media

\medterm{Airway} A passage through which air moves between the outside environment and the lungs, including the nose, mouth, pharynx, larynx, trachea, and bronchi.

\medterm{Airway Clearance Techniques} Physical or mechanical methods used to mobilize and remove airway secretions. Examples include
directed coughing, breathing exercises, postural drainage, positive expiratory pressure,
and high-frequency chest-wall oscillation. Selection depends on the disease, secretion
burden, strength, and ability to cooperate.

\medterm{Airway Management} Airway management encompasses techniques and devices used to maintain ventilation and
oxygenation in patients with compromised airways.

\medterm{Airway Obstruction} Partial or complete blockage of airflow through the upper or lower respiratory tract. It
may result from a foreign body, secretions, swelling, trauma, tumor, or loss of airway
muscle tone and can cause stridor, wheeze, hypoxaemia, respiratory distress, or arrest.

\medterm{Airway Resistance} The opposition to airflow through the conducting airways, determined largely by airway
radius and flow pattern. Bronchoconstriction, mucus, edema, and reduced elastic traction
increase resistance; because resistance varies strongly with radius, small changes can
markedly affect work of breathing.

\medterm{AK} Actinic keratosis, a rough, scaly precancerous skin lesion caused by chronic ultraviolet exposure.

\medterm{Akathisia} Subjective feeling of inner restlessness accompanied by a compulsive need to move, most
commonly an extrapyramidal side effect of antipsychotic drugs (typical and atypical
neuroleptics), also seen with dopamine antagonists (metoclopramide), selective serotonin
reuptake inhibitor withdrawal, and in Parkinson disease.

\medterm{AKI} Alternate index label; see \textit{Acute Kidney Injury}.

\medterm{Akinesia} Impairment or absence of voluntary movement, classically a hypokinetic feature of basal
ganglia disorders such as Parkinson disease and related parkinsonian syndromes.

\medterm{Akinetic} Characterized by little or no voluntary movement; akinesia may result from neurologic disease, medication effects, or severe motor impairment.

\medterm{Akinetic Mutism} A state of profound lack of movement and speech despite wakefulness and preserved basic alertness.

\medterm{ALA} An abbreviation for alpha-linolenic acid, an essential omega-3 fatty acid, or aminolevulinic acid, a heme-pathway compound used topically in some photodynamic treatments; context determines the meaning.

\medterm{Alagille Syndrome} An inherited disorder of Notch signaling that commonly affects the liver, heart, skeleton,
eyes, and characteristic facial features. It is usually caused by a pathogenic variant in JAG1 or, less often, NOTCH2.

\medterm{Alanine} An amino acid used in protein synthesis and glucose metabolism.

\medterm{Alanine Aminotransferase (ALT)} An enzyme found mainly in liver cells. When these cells are damaged, ALT enters the blood; high levels
may indicate liver injury or disease. It is usually interpreted with AST and other liver tests.

\synonyms
ALT

\medterm{Alanine Transaminase (ALT) Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures serum ALT activity, a hepatocellular enzyme released during liver injury. Elevated ALT is more specific for hepatocellular damage than AST and is used to evaluate hepatitis, drug toxicity, and other liver diseases.

\medterm{ALARA (As Low As Is Reasonably Achievable) Principle} A radiation-safety principle that aims to keep exposure to ionizing radiation as low as
practical while still achieving the needed diagnostic or treatment benefit. It uses measures such as
justification, optimization, shielding, shorter exposure time, and greater distance.

\medterm{Alarmin} A molecule released by damaged or stressed cells that activates innate immune responses.

\medterm{Alastrim} Alastrim is a historical name for a generally milder form of smallpox, caused by variola minor; smallpox has been eradicated worldwide.

\medterm{Albendazole} Albendazole is a benzimidazole antiparasitic drug that disrupts parasite microtubules and is used for several intestinal and tissue helminth infections.

\medterm{Albinism} An inherited condition involving reduced or absent melanin production, affecting the skin, hair, and eyes.

\synonyms
Oculocutaneous Albinism

\medterm{Albinism (Oculocutaneous Albinism)} A group of inherited disorders that reduce melanin production in the skin, hair, and eyes. It may
cause light pigmentation, reduced visual acuity, nystagmus, and increased sensitivity
to sunlight; the severity depends on the genetic subtype.

\synonyms
Oculocutaneous Albinism

\medterm{Albinism, Oculocutaneous} An inherited disorder of melanin production affecting the skin, hair, and eyes, often causing light sensitivity, nystagmus, reduced visual acuity, and increased sun sensitivity.

\medterm{Albino} A person or animal with albinism; person-first language such as “person with albinism” is generally preferred.

\medterm{Albright's Hereditary Osteodystrophy} A group of inherited features associated with abnormalities of GNAS signaling, including short
metacarpal bones, a round facial appearance, short stature, and subcutaneous
ossification. Some associated disorders also cause resistance to parathyroid hormone
or other hormones.

\medterm{Albumen} The white of an egg, composed largely of water and proteins; it is distinct from albumin, the major protein in blood plasma.

\medterm{Albumin} The main protein in blood plasma, produced by the liver. It helps maintain oncotic pressure and
transports various substances, including hormones, fatty acids, and medications.

\medterm{Albumin Blood (Serum) Test} A laboratory test that measures serum albumin concentration, the principal plasma protein synthesized by the liver. Low levels indicate hepatic insufficiency, nephrotic syndrome, malnutrition, or chronic inflammation; it is also used to assess nutritional status and prognosis.

\medterm{Albumin Clearance} The removal rate of albumin from blood or another body compartment, used in selected physiologic studies.

\medterm{Albumin-To-Creatinine Ratio} The urinary albumin concentration standardized to urinary creatinine concentration,
usually measured in a spot urine sample. Persistent albuminuria is a marker of kidney
damage and is categorized as A1 (<30 mg/g), A2 (30--300 mg/g), or A3 (>300 mg/g) in
commonly used CKD classification.

\medterm{Albumins} Albumins are water-soluble proteins, especially serum albumin made by the liver, that maintain oncotic pressure and transport hormones, fatty acids, drugs, and other substances.

\medterm{Albuminuria} Abnormally increased excretion of albumin in the urine, indicating disruption of the
glomerular filtration barrier or another form of kidney disease. It is commonly detected
with a urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio and classified as moderately or severely
increased according to the quantity and persistence of excretion.

\medterm{ALCL} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Anaplastic large cell lymphoma}.

\medterm{Alcohol} Ethanol, a psychoactive substance that can impair judgment and coordination and cause intoxication or dependence.

\medterm{Alcohol Abuse} A nonpreferred older term for a pattern of alcohol use that causes recurrent problems or harm. Current clinical classification generally uses alcohol use disorder and specifies its severity.

\synonyms
