Problem drinking; harmful alcohol use.

\medterm{Alcohol Abuse And Alcoholism} Alcohol use disorder is a medical condition involving impaired control over drinking despite harm, craving, tolerance, or withdrawal. Treatment can include medically supervised withdrawal, counseling, peer support, and medications that reduce drinking or relapse risk.

\synonyms
Alcohol Dependence (Alcohol Abuse And Alcoholism)

\medterm{Alcohol Addiction} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Alcohol use disorder}.

\medterm{Alcohol And Pregnancy} Alcohol crosses the placenta and can cause fetal alcohol spectrum disorders; no known amount or timing during pregnancy is established as safe.

\medterm{Alcohol And Teens} Alcohol exposure during adolescence can impair judgment, increase injury and unsafe-sex risk, and affect developing brain systems involved in learning and impulse control. Early screening, supportive communication, treatment of coexisting mental-health problems, and limiting access reduce harm.

\medterm{Alcohol Dehydrogenase} An enzyme, found mainly in the liver, that catalyzes the first major step in ethanol metabolism by converting alcohol to acetaldehyde.

\synonyms
ADH; ethanol dehydrogenase.

\medterm{Alcohol Dependence} A pattern of alcohol use characterized by impaired control, compulsive use, tolerance, withdrawal, and continued drinking despite harm. In current terminology, it is generally classified under alcohol use disorder.

\synonyms
Alcohol use disorder; alcohol addiction; alcoholism.

\medterm{Alcohol Dependence (Alcohol Abuse And Alcoholism)} Alternate index label for Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism.

\medterm{Alcohol Dependency} A chronic relapsing disorder characterized by compulsive ethanol use, loss of control
over intake, and negative emotional state during withdrawal, leading to tolerance,
dependence, and organ damage over time.

\medterm{Alcohol Intolerance} An unpleasant reaction to alcohol caused by difficulty metabolizing alcohol or one of its components.

\medterm{Alcohol Intoxication} Acute impairment caused by ethanol, which may produce disinhibition, impaired coordination, slurred speech,
vomiting, reduced consciousness, or respiratory depression. Severe intoxication can be
life-threatening and requires assessment of breathing, circulation, glucose, trauma, and
other substances.

\medterm{Alcohol Poisoning} A life-threatening condition caused by drinking enough alcohol to impair breathing, consciousness, and other vital functions.

\medterm{Alcohol Pregnancy (Fetal Alcohol Syndrome (FAS))} Alternate index label for Fetal Alcohol Syndrome (FAS).

\medterm{Alcohol Swab} A single-use pad containing alcohol for cleaning intact skin before selected procedures. It should be used according to the product
instructions and allowed to dry.

\medterm{Alcohol Use} Consumption of alcoholic beverages. Health effects depend on amount, frequency, pattern, and individual risk.

\medterm{Alcohol Use And Safe Drinking} Guidance on reducing alcohol-related harm, including standard-drink awareness, lower-risk limits, and situations in which alcohol should be avoided.

\medterm{Alcohol Use Disorder} A problematic pattern of alcohol use leading to significant impairment or distress.
Severity is classified as mild (2--3 criteria), moderate (4--5 criteria), or severe (6
or more criteria).


\medterm{Alcohol Withdrawal} A syndrome that may occur after abrupt cessation or substantial reduction of prolonged heavy alcohol use.
Symptoms can include tremor, anxiety, sweating, nausea, hallucinations, seizures, or
delirium; onset and severity vary, and severe withdrawal requires urgent medical care.

\medterm{Alcohol Withdrawal Syndrome} A set of symptoms occurring after abrupt reduction or cessation of prolonged heavy alcohol use. Symptoms range from tremor, sweating, anxiety, and nausea to seizures and delirium tremens.

\medterm{Alcohol-Associated Hepatitis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Alcoholic hepatitis (Alcohol-associated hepatitis)}.

\medterm{Alcoholic Cardiomyopathy} Dilated cardiomyopathy caused by chronic excessive alcohol consumption, one of the most
common reversible causes of dilated cardiomyopathy. Direct toxic effect of ethanol and
its metabolite acetaldehyde on cardiomyocytes. Presentation is identical to other
dilated cardiomyopathies.

\medterm{Alcoholic Hepatitis} An acute inflammation and injury of the liver caused by heavy alcohol use. It commonly causes jaundice, fatigue, fever, nausea, and abdominal pain and can become severe enough to cause liver failure.

\medterm{Alcoholic Hepatitis (Alcohol-Associated Hepatitis)} An acute inflammation and injury of the liver caused by heavy alcohol use. It commonly causes jaundice, fatigue, fever, nausea, and abdominal pain and can become severe enough to cause liver failure.

\medterm{Alcoholic Intoxication} Impaired judgment, coordination, or consciousness caused by acute alcohol exposure.

\synonyms
Alcohol intoxication

\medterm{Alcoholic Ketoacidosis} A high-anion-gap metabolic acidosis caused by prolonged heavy alcohol use, poor
nutritional intake, and vomiting. It typically presents with abdominal pain, nausea,
vomiting, dehydration, and tachypnea, often with a normal or low blood glucose
concentration.

\medterm{Alcoholic Liver Disease} Alcoholic liver disease includes steatosis, alcohol-associated hepatitis, and cirrhosis caused by sustained alcohol exposure.

\medterm{Alcoholic Neuropathy} A length-dependent peripheral neuropathy associated with chronic excessive alcohol use
and nutritional deficiency, especially thiamine deficiency. It causes burning pain,
numbness, paraesthesia, and weakness, most prominently in the feet and lower legs.

\medterm{Alcoholism} A chronic disorder characterized by impaired control over alcohol use and continued use despite harm.

\synonyms
Alcohol use disorder

\medterm{Alcuronium} Alcuronium is a nondepolarizing neuromuscular-blocking drug that causes skeletal-muscle paralysis during anesthesia; it requires airway and respiratory support.

\medterm{Aldolase Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures serum aldolase activity, a glycolytic enzyme present in skeletal muscle, liver, and brain. Elevated levels suggest muscle disease such as dermatomyositis or muscular dystrophy, and may complement creatine kinase in evaluating myopathy.

\medterm{Aldosterone} A mineralocorticoid hormone produced by the zona glomerulosa of the adrenal cortex, the
final effector of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS).

\medterm{Aldosterone Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures serum aldosterone concentration, the principal mineralocorticoid hormone produced by the adrenal cortex. It is used to evaluate primary aldosteronism, secondary hypertension, and adrenal insufficiency, often interpreted with plasma renin activity.

\medterm{Alembic} A distillation apparatus historically used in chemistry and laboratory work to separate or purify substances by differences in volatility.

\medterm{Alexia} An acquired inability to comprehend written language despite intact vision and otherwise preserved language function, usually caused by stroke or other lesions affecting the dominant-hemisphere reading networks. When reading is impaired but writing remains possible, the pattern is called alexia without agraphia.

\medterm{Alexithymia} Difficulty identifying, describing, or distinguishing one’s emotional experiences.

\medterm{Alfacalcidol} Alfacalcidol is a vitamin-D analogue converted in the liver to an active form that increases calcium and phosphate absorption and is used in selected bone and mineral disorders.

\medterm{Algia} A word ending meaning pain, as in neuralgia, myalgia, and arthralgia.

\medterm{Alglucerase} Alglucerase is a modified form of the lysosomal enzyme glucocerebrosidase formerly used as enzyme-replacement therapy for Gaucher disease; newer preparations are more commonly used.

\medterm{Algor Mortis} Post-mortem cooling of the body following death until it reaches ambient environmental
temperature. Serves as one of the physical estimators of the post-mortem interval (time
since death).

\medterm{Algorithm} A medical algorithm is a defined sequence of rules or calculations used to support diagnosis, risk assessment, treatment, triage, or data analysis; it does not replace clinical judgment.

\medterm{Aliasing Artifact} An ultrasound display artifact in which blood flow exceeds the selected velocity scale and appears to reverse direction.
It can be reduced by increasing the scale or changing the imaging settings.

\medterm{Alimentary} Relating to food, nourishment, or the organs and processes of digestion.

\medterm{Alimentary Canal} The continuous muscular tube from the mouth to the anus through which food passes; it includes the pharynx, esophagus, stomach, small intestine, and large intestine.

\medterm{Alkali} An alkali is a basic substance that accepts hydrogen ions or produces hydroxide in solution; caustic alkalis can cause severe chemical burns, while controlled bases have medical and laboratory uses.

\medterm{Alkaline Phosphatase} A group of enzymes that catalyze the hydrolysis of phosphate esters at alkaline pH,
found in high concentrations in the liver, bone, placenta, and intestinal mucosa.
Elevated serum levels occur in obstructive liver disease, bone disorders including Paget
disease and fractures, pregnancy, and certain malignancies.

\medterm{Alkaloid} A naturally occurring nitrogen-containing organic compound, often produced by plants, with potentially potent physiologic or pharmacologic effects.

\medterm{Alkaloids} Alkaloids are nitrogen-containing plant or microbial compounds with diverse biologic effects; examples include morphine, quinine, nicotine, and atropine.

\medterm{Alkalosis} A metabolic (elevated bicarbonate) or respiratory (low PaCO2) disturbance that raises
arterial pH above 7.45. Metabolic alkalosis: vomiting/NG suction (HCl loss), diuretics,
mineralocorticoid excess, hypokalemia, and alkali ingestion—compensated by
hypoventilation; chloride-responsive vs. chloride-resistant distinguishes causes.

\medterm{Alkaptonuria} An inherited disorder of tyrosine metabolism causing homogentisic-acid accumulation and darkening of urine or tissues.

\medterm{Alkylating Agents} Alkylating agents are drugs or chemicals that add alkyl groups to DNA or other molecules; anticancer agents exploit this to inhibit replication, while normal-tissue toxicity includes myelosuppression and mutagenic risk.

\medterm{Allantoin} Allantoin is a compound used in some topical products for its skin-protective, moisturizing, and keratolytic properties; formulation and indication determine its clinical role.

\medterm{Allantois} The allantois is an embryonic membrane involved in early waste handling, blood-vessel development, and formation of structures related to the umbilicus and urinary bladder.

\medterm{Allele} Alternative form of a gene at a particular locus. Individuals inherit two alleles for
each gene (one from each parent). Alleles can be dominant, recessive, or codominant.

\medterm{Allelic} Relating to an allele, one of two or more alternative forms of a gene at a particular genomic location.

\medterm{Allen Test} A bedside test of collateral blood flow to the hand through the radial and ulnar arteries, commonly performed before radial-artery puncture or cannulation.

\medterm{Allergen} A substance capable of causing an allergic immune response in a susceptible person. Common allergens include
proteins in foods, pollens, medicines, animal dander, insect venom, and natural rubber.

\medterm{Allergen Immunotherapy} Treatment that gives gradually increasing doses of a specific allergen by injection or under the tongue to
reduce allergic sensitivity. It may be used for selected allergic rhinitis, conjunctivitis, asthma, or insect-venom allergy.

\medterm{Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis} A hypersensitivity reaction to Aspergillus fumigatus in asthma or CF patients. Causes
central bronchiectasis, elevated total IgE, and eosinophilia. Treatment includes
corticosteroids and itraconazole.

\medterm{Allergic Cascade} The allergic cascade is the sequence in which an allergen activates immune cells, including IgE-bearing mast cells, causing release of histamine and other mediators. This produces symptoms such as itching, swelling, mucus, wheezing, hives, or anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergic Conjunctivitis} An inflammatory condition of the conjunctiva mediated by IgE-dependent mast cell
degranulation in response to environmental allergens, characterized by bilateral ocular
itching, tearing, conjunctival injection, and eyelid edema.

\medterm{Allergic Contact Dermatitis} A delayed, T-cell-mediated skin reaction after contact with a substance to which the person has become sensitized, causing itching, redness, swelling, and sometimes blisters or scaling.

\medterm{Allergic Dermatitis} An inflammatory skin reaction caused by an immune response to an external allergen, most
commonly allergic contact dermatitis. It is a delayed, T-cell-mediated hypersensitivity
reaction that typically develops after prior sensitization.

\medterm{Allergic Granulomatosis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{EGPA (formerly Churg-Strauss syndrome)}.

\medterm{Allergic Granulomatosis And Angiitis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{EGPA (formerly Churg-Strauss syndrome)}.

\medterm{Allergic Reaction} An immune response to an otherwise harmless substance, ranging from mild symptoms to anaphylaxis.

\synonyms
Allergy

\medterm{Allergic Reactions} Immune reactions to substances that are usually harmless, such as foods, medicines, pollens, or insect venom.
They may be immediate or delayed and can range from localized symptoms to anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergic Rhinitis} An IgE-mediated inflammatory response of the nasal mucosa to inhaled allergens, causing
sneezing, nasal congestion, clear rhinorrhea, nasal itching, and often conjunctival
symptoms. Management may include allergen avoidance, intranasal corticosteroids,
antihistamines, and allergen immunotherapy when indicated.

\medterm{Allergic Rhinitis (Hay Fever)} Allergies occur when the immune system overreacts to harmless environmental substances
such as pollen, dust mites, mold, or pet dander. Hay fever (allergic rhinitis)
specifically involves nasal symptoms like sneezing, congestion, and itchy eyes triggered
by airborne allergens.

\synonyms
Hay Fever




\medterm{Allergies} Immune reactions to normally harmless substances such as foods, pollens, medicines,
insect venom, or animal dander. Reactions range from localized rhinitis or dermatitis to
life-threatening anaphylaxis.

\medterm{Allergies (Allergy)} Alternate index label for Allergy.

\medterm{Allergies, Asthma, And Dust} Dust can carry allergens such as dust-mite particles that trigger allergic rhinitis or asthma in susceptible people. Symptoms may include sneezing, congestion, wheezing, cough, and shortness of breath.

\medterm{Allergies, Asthma, And Molds} Mold spores and fragments can trigger allergic rhinitis, conjunctivitis, or asthma in sensitized people. Reducing exposure and treating the specific allergic or airway condition are guided by clinical assessment.

\medterm{Allergies, Asthma, And Pollen} Pollen is a seasonal airborne allergen that can trigger allergic rhinitis, conjunctivitis, or asthma. Typical symptoms include sneezing, itchy eyes, nasal congestion, cough, and wheezing.

\medterm{Allergy} An allergy is an inappropriate immune response to a normally harmless substance such as pollen, food, medicine, insect venom, or animal proteins. Symptoms range from localized rhinitis or hives to life-threatening anaphylaxis.

\synonyms
Allergies (Allergy)

\medterm{Allergy Diaper (Intertrigo)} Alternate index label for Intertrigo.

\medterm{Allergy Eczema (Atopic Dermatitis)} Alternate index label for Atopic Dermatitis.

\medterm{Allergy Eye (Eye Allergy)} Alternate index label for Eye Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy Food (Food Allergy)} Alternate index label for Food Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy Insect (Insect Sting Allergies)} Alternate index label for Insect Sting Allergies.

\medterm{Allergy Latex (Latex Allergy)} Alternate index label for Latex Allergy.

\medterm{Allergy Plant Contact (Poison Ivy, Oak, And Sumac)} Alternate index label for Poison Ivy, Oak, and Sumac.

\medterm{Allergy Rash (Rash)} Alternate index label for Rash.

\medterm{Allergy Shots} Subcutaneous allergen immunotherapy involving gradually increasing doses of selected allergens to reduce allergic sensitivity; administration requires monitoring for systemic reactions.

\medterm{Allergy Testing} Identification of IgE-mediated allergic sensitization. Skin prick test (SPT): allergen
extract applied to skin via lancet, read after 15 min (wheal >3mm = positive).
Intradermal test: more sensitive, used when SPT negative but suspicion remains.

\medterm{Allergy Testing - Skin} A diagnostic test in which small amounts of allergen extracts are applied to the skin by prick or intradermal injection to detect IgE-mediated allergic sensitization. A wheal-and-flare reaction at the test site indicates sensitization to the corresponding allergen.

\medterm{Allergy, Dust Mite} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Dust mite allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Egg} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Egg allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Food} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Food allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Latex} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Latex allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Milk} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Milk allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Mold} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Mold allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Nickel} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Nickel allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Peanut} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Peanut allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Penicillin} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Penicillin allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Pet} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Pet allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Shellfish} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Shellfish allergy}.

\medterm{Allergy, Wheat} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Wheat allergy}.

\medterm{Allis Clamp} A toothed surgical clamp used to grasp and hold firm tissue during an operation.

\medterm{Allodynia} Pain caused by a stimulus that is normally not painful, such as light touch or gentle pressure. It may
occur with neuropathic, nociplastic, or other pain conditions.

\medterm{Allogeneic} Derived from a genetically different individual of the same species, as in an allogeneic blood, tissue, stem-cell, or organ transplant.

\medterm{Allograft} Tissue transplanted between genetically different individuals of the same species.
Allografts may provoke immune rejection and require appropriate donor screening,
processing, and, in some settings, immunosuppression.

\medterm{Allopathy} A historical label for conventional medical treatment, often contrasted with homeopathy; modern clinical practice is more accurately described by its specific methods and evidence.

\medterm{Allosteric Regulation} The regulation of enzyme activity by the binding of an effector molecule at a site other
than the active site (allosteric site), causing a conformational change that alters the
enzyme's activity. Allosteric enzymes typically have multiple subunits with cooperative
binding kinetics (sigmoidal rather than hyperbolic velocity curves).

\medterm{Aloe} A plant-derived substance used in some topical products and supplements; topical preparations may soothe minor irritation, while oral latex-containing products can cause cramps, diarrhea, and electrolyte problems.

\medterm{Alogia} Poverty of speech or poverty of content of speech, a negative symptom of schizophrenia.
It manifests as brief, empty replies to questions, reduced spontaneous speech, and lack
of elaboration. It reflects dysfunction in the mesocortical dopamine pathway and is less
responsive to antipsychotic medications than positive symptoms.

\medterm{Alongshan Virus (ALSV)} A tick-associated segmented RNA virus reported to cause a febrile illness, often with headache, fatigue, nausea, or neurologic symptoms; its epidemiology and clinical significance remain under study.

\medterm{Alopecia} The general medical term for loss of hair from the scalp or body, rather than a single specific disease. Causes include disorders of the hair follicle, immune disease, hormones, medicines, systemic illness, or physical injury. Androgenetic alopecia (male- or female-pattern hair loss) is common and is associated with genetic susceptibility and androgen, particularly dihydrotestosterone (DHT), effects. Alopecia areata is an autoimmune, usually non-scarring, patchy hair-loss disorder that may progress to alopecia totalis (entire scalp) or universalis (whole body).

\medterm{Alopecia (Hair Loss)} Alternate index label for Hair Loss.

\medterm{Alopecia Areata} A common, autoimmune, non-scarring hair loss disorder affecting approximately 1 to 2
percent of the population worldwide, with a lifetime risk of 2 percent, occurring
equally across sexes and ethnicities, with peak onset between 15 and 29 years.

\synonyms
AA (Alopecia Areata)
Patchy Hair Loss (Alopecia Areata)

\medterm{Alopecia Areata (AA)} Alternate index label for Alopecia Areata.
\medterm{Alopecia Totalis} Complete loss of hair from the scalp, usually caused by an autoimmune disorder.

\medterm{Alopecia Universalis} Loss of hair from the entire body, including the scalp, eyebrows, eyelashes, and other hair-bearing areas.

\medterm{ALP - Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures serum alkaline phosphatase activity, an enzyme present in liver, bone, intestine, and placenta. Elevated levels may indicate hepatobiliary disease, bone turnover, or pregnancy; isoenzyme testing helps distinguish the source.

\medterm{ALP Isoenzyme Test} A laboratory test that identifies the tissue source of elevated alkaline phosphatase by separating liver, bone, intestinal, and placental isoenzymes. It helps determine whether elevated ALP arises from hepatobiliary disease or bone disorders.

\medterm{Alpha 1} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Alpha-1-antitrypsin deficiency}.

\medterm{Alpha Blockers} Medicines that block alpha-adrenergic receptors and may lower blood pressure or improve urine flow in prostate enlargement.

\medterm{Alpha Cells} Endocrine cells in the pancreatic islets that produce and release glucagon, a hormone that raises blood glucose during fasting or low-glucose states.

\synonyms
Pancreatic alpha cells; glucagon-secreting cells.

\medterm{Alpha Hydroxy Acids} A group of organic acids used in dermatology and cosmetics to exfoliate the outer skin layer and improve texture or pigmentation. Examples include glycolic and lactic acid.

\synonyms
AHAs; fruit acids.

\medterm{Alpha Thalassemia} An inherited blood disorder caused by reduced production of alpha-globin chains of hemoglobin. Severity ranges from mild microcytosis or anemia to severe fetal or newborn disease.

\medterm{Alpha Waves} Rhythmic electroencephalographic waves, typically around 8--13 Hz, most prominent when a relaxed, awake person has the eyes closed.

\synonyms
Alpha rhythm; posterior dominant rhythm.

\medterm{Alpha-1 Antitrypsin Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures serum alpha-1 antitrypsin concentration, a protease inhibitor synthesized by the liver. Low levels suggest alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, a genetic disorder predisposing to early emphysema and liver disease; phenotyping or genotyping confirms the diagnosis.

\medterm{Alpha-1 Antitrypsin Deficiency} An autosomal codominant genetic disorder caused by mutations in the SERPINA1 gene. It
predisposes to early-onset panlobular emphysema (lower lobes), liver disease, and
panniculitis. PiZZ genotype is most severe. Treatment includes AAT augmentation therapy
and smoking avoidance.

\medterm{Alpha-Agonists} Medicines that stimulate alpha-adrenergic receptors. Depending on the receptor and tissue, they can constrict
blood vessels, alter autonomic activity, or reduce aqueous-humor production in the eye.
Their uses and adverse effects depend on the specific medicine and route.

\medterm{Alpha-Delta Sleep} A sleep pattern in which alpha-frequency activity intrudes into delta-wave deep sleep and may be associated with nonrestorative sleep or chronic pain.

\synonyms
Alpha-delta intrusion; alpha-delta rhythm.

\medterm{Alpha-Fetoprotein} A protein produced mainly by the fetal liver; maternal or blood levels can help assess certain fetal and adult conditions.

\synonyms
AFP; alpha fetoprotein (alternate spelling).

\medterm{Alpha-Fetoprotein (AFP)} A protein produced mainly by the fetal liver. Maternal blood AFP is used in prenatal screening, and serum AFPmay help evaluate selected liver and germ-cell tumors. Results are not diagnostic by themselves.

\synonyms
Alpha-Fetoprotein

\medterm{Alpha-Gal Syndrome} An allergy to the carbohydrate alpha-gal found in most nonhuman mammalian meat and some products derived from mammals.

\medterm{Alpha-Globulin} A group of blood proteins that includes transport and acute-phase proteins.

\medterm{Alpha-Glucosidase Inhibitors} Oral glucose-lowering medications including acarbose, miglitol, and voglibose that delay
carbohydrate digestion and glucose absorption in the small intestine by inhibiting brush
border alpha-glucoside enzymes. Reduce postprandial glucose excursions without affecting
fasting glucose significantly.

\medterm{Alpha-Ketoglutarate} A five-carbon intermediate of the citric acid cycle formed from isocitrate and converted
to succinyl-CoA. It also serves as a carbon skeleton for amino-acid metabolism and
participates in oxidative deamination and cellular signaling.

\medterm{Alpha-Synuclein} A neuronal protein that can accumulate abnormally in disorders such as Parkinson disease and Lewy-body dementia.

\medterm{Alpha-Thalassemia} An inherited disorder in which reduced alpha-globin production impairs hemoglobin formation.

\medterm{Alport Syndrome} Hereditary nephritis caused by mutations in type four collagen genes leading to
progressive sensorineural hearing loss, ocular anomalies, and renal failure. X-linked
form from COL4A5 mutations is the most common. Autosomal recessive and dominant forms
exist.

\medterm{Alprazolam} A short- to intermediate-acting benzodiazepine used mainly for anxiety and panic disorder; it can cause sedation, dependence, withdrawal, and dangerous respiratory depression with opioids or other depressants.

\medterm{ALS} Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (motor neuron disease; Lou Gehrig disease) is the progressive neurodegenerative disease characterized by degeneration of upper and lower motor neurons controlling voluntary movement. It causes progressive weakness, muscle wasting, fasciculations, spasticity, dysarthria, or dysphagia; sensation is usually relatively preserved, but respiratory muscles may eventually be affected.




\medterm{Alstrom Syndrome} A rare autosomal-recessive disorder caused by ALMS1 variants, associated with cone-rod dystrophy, hearing loss, obesity, insulin resistance, cardiomyopathy, and progressive kidney or liver disease.

\medterm{Alternative Therapy For Multiple Sclerosis} Complementary approaches for multiple sclerosis may support symptoms such as fatigue, pain, stress, or mobility but do not replace disease-modifying treatment. Exercise, rehabilitation, mindfulness, and selected supplements should be discussed with clinicians because interactions and unproven claims are common.

\synonyms
MS Alternative And Complementary Therapies (Alternative Therapy For Multiple Sclerosis)

\medterm{Alternative Treatments For Hot Flashes} Nonhormonal approaches to hot flashes include cooling strategies, layered clothing, sleep and stress measures, cognitive behavioral therapy, and selected prescription medicines. Herbal products have variable evidence and can interact with medicines; treatment choice depends on symptoms and medical history.

\medterm{Altitude Illness} Acute mountain sickness (AMS): headache + 1 symptom (nausea, fatigue, dizziness,
insomnia) at >2500m. HACE: AMS + ataxia/altered mental status (cerebral edema). HAPE:
dyspnea at rest, cough, frothy sputum, crackles, hypoxemia (noncardiogenic pulmonary
edema).

\medterm{Altitude Sickness} Illness caused by rapid ascent to high altitude without adequate acclimatization, ranging from acute mountain sickness to life-threatening pulmonary or cerebral edema.

\medterm{Aluminum Phosphide} A highly toxic fumigant that releases phosphine gas when exposed to moisture or stomach acid. Poisoning can
cause severe cardiovascular, respiratory, gastrointestinal, and metabolic effects and
requires immediate emergency treatment and supportive intensive care.

\medterm{Alvarado Score} Clinical scoring system for acute appendicitis. Components (MANTRELS): Migration of pain
(1), Anorexia (1), Nausea/vomiting (1), Tenderness in RLQ (2), Rebound pain (1),
Elevated temperature (1), Leukocytosis (2), Left shift (1).

\medterm{Alveolar} Relating to an alveolus, especially one of the tiny air sacs in the lungs where oxygen and carbon dioxide are exchanged.

\medterm{Alveolar Abnormalities} Structural or functional changes in the lung alveoli that can impair gas exchange and occur with infection, edema, emphysema, interstitial disease, or developmental disorders.

\medterm{Alveolar Bone} Bone surrounding and supporting tooth roots. Resorbs with tooth loss. Maintained by
function. Bone grafting may be needed for implants.

\medterm{Alveolar Gas Equation} PAO2 = (FiO2 x (Patm - PH2O)) - (PaCO2 / R). Patm = 760 mmHg, PH2O = 47 mmHg, R = 0.8.
Allows calculation of A-a gradient.

\medterm{Alveolar Hydatid Disease} A serious parasitic infection caused by the larval stage of Echinococcus multilocularis, usually producing an infiltrative, tumor-like lesion in the liver that can spread to other organs.

\medterm{Alveolar Macrophage} An immune cell in the lung air sacs that removes particles, microbes, and cellular debris.

\synonyms
Dust cell

\medterm{Alveolar Osteitis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Dry socket}.

\medterm{Alveolar Osteitis (Dry Socket Overview)} Alternate index label for Dry Socket Overview.

\medterm{Alveolar Rhabdomyosarcoma} A malignant small-round-cell tumor showing skeletal-muscle differentiation, usually arising in the extremities, trunk, or head and neck of children and adolescents.
It may disseminate early through lymphatic and hematogenous routes and often carries characteristic PAX3-FOXO1 or PAX7-FOXO1 fusions.
Diagnosis requires biopsy with immunophenotyping and molecular testing, followed by staging for local and distant disease.
Treatment is multimodal and commonly includes systemic chemotherapy with surgery and/or radiotherapy according to risk group.

\medterm{Alveolar Ventilation} The volume of fresh air reaching the alveoli per minute, representing the portion of
minute ventilation that participates in gas exchange. Calculated as (tidal volume - dead
space volume) x respiratory rate. Normal alveolar ventilation is approximately 4-5
L/min.

\medterm{Alveolar Ventilation Equation} The relationship between alveolar ventilation and arterial carbon-dioxide tension,
approximately PaCO2 = 0.863 times carbon-dioxide production divided by alveolar
ventilation. It explains why reduced effective ventilation causes hypercapnia.

\medterm{Alveoli} Microscopic air sacs in the lungs where oxygen enters the blood and carbon dioxide leaves it.

\medterm{Alveolitis} Inflammation of the pulmonary alveoli, usually causing cough and breathlessness, or inflammation of a tooth socket after extraction, called alveolar osteitis or dry socket; the organ involved distinguishes the two meanings.

\medterm{Alveolus} The terminal air sac (200-300 microns) where gas exchange occurs. Approximately 300-500
million per lung, providing ~70 m2 surface area. Wall: type I pneumocytes (95\% surface,
thin for diffusion), type II pneumocytes (5\%, secrete pulmonary surfactant reducing
surface tension), alveolar macrophages (dust cells, phagocytosis).

\medterm{Alzheimer Disease} A progressive neurodegenerative disorder characterized pathologically by amyloid-beta
plaques, tau neurofibrillary pathology, and neuronal loss. It most commonly presents
with insidious impairment of episodic memory followed by broader cognitive and
functional decline.


\medterm{Alzheimer's} A progressive neurodegenerative disease causing memory loss and decline in thinking and daily function.

\synonyms
Alzheimer disease

\medterm{Alzheimer's Disease} A progressive brain disorder causing deterioration of memory, thinking, behavior, and the ability to perform daily activities.

\medterm{Alzheimer's Disease Causes, Stages, And Symptoms} Alzheimer disease is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder causing decline in memory and other thinking abilities, followed by increasing difficulty with communication, judgment, movement, and self-care. Progression varies, and evaluation looks for reversible contributors and other dementias.

\medterm{Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms And Stages} Alzheimer disease commonly progresses from subtle memory and planning problems to loss of independence, language and recognition difficulties, and eventually extensive assistance needs. Symptoms and speed of progression differ, so staging supports care planning rather than predicting an exact timeline.

\synonyms
Alzheimers Disease Stages And Symptoms (Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms And Stages)

\medterm{Alzheimers Disease Stages And Symptoms (Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms And Stages)} Alternate index label for Alzheimer's Disease Symptoms and Stages.

\medterm{Am I Depressed} Depression is more than temporary sadness: it involves persistent low mood or loss of interest with changes in sleep, appetite, energy, concentration, movement, self-worth, or hope. Thoughts of self-harm require immediate emergency or crisis support.

\medterm{Amalgam} Restorative material composed of mercury alloyed with silver, tin, copper, and other
metals. Properties: strong, durable, cost-effective. Concerns: mercury toxicity debates,
aesthetic limitations. Placement: requires cavity preparation, condensation, carving.
Removal: sectioning, cooling to prevent mercury vapor.

\medterm{Amastia} Congenital absence of one or both breasts, sometimes accompanied by absence of the nipple or pectoral muscles and other developmental anomalies.

\medterm{Amathophobia} An excessive or persistent fear of dust that causes significant anxiety, avoidance, or impairment.

\medterm{Amaurosis Fugax} Transient monocular vision loss lasting seconds to minutes, classically described as a
curtain descending over the field, caused by retinal or optic nerve ischemia.

\medterm{Ambidextrous} Able to use the right and left hands with approximately equal skill or preference.

\medterm{Ambient} Surrounding or present in the immediate environment, as in ambient temperature, light, noise, or air.

\medterm{Ambiguous Genitalia} External genital anatomy that does not have typical male or female features, usually
because of a difference in sex development. Evaluation includes examination, chromosome
and hormone testing, pelvic or abdominal imaging, and assessment for congenital adrenal
hyperplasia.

\medterm{Amblyopia} A developmental reduction in vision caused by abnormal visual input during early childhood, without a sufficient structural explanation.
It commonly results from misalignment, unequal focusing power, or obstruction of vision
in one eye.

\medterm{Amblyopia Lazy Eye} Amblyopia is reduced vision in an eye that did not develop normal visual processing during childhood, often because of unequal focusing power, misalignment, or obstruction. Early treatment may include glasses, patching or blurring the stronger eye, and correction of the underlying problem.

\medterm{Ambu Bag} A self-inflating manual resuscitation bag used with a mask or airway device to provide positive-pressure ventilation.

\medterm{Ambulance} A vehicle equipped to transport and provide emergency medical care to ill or injured people.

\medterm{Ambulatory Blood Pressure Monitoring} Measurement of blood pressure at regular intervals, usually over twenty-four hours, with a portable cuff worn during normal activities and sleep. It detects white-coat and masked hypertension, evaluates nighttime blood-pressure patterns, and helps confirm hypertension diagnosed in the clinic.

\medterm{Amebiasis} Infection with the protozoan parasite Entamoeba histolytica, acquired mainly by
ingestion of faecally contaminated food or water. It may be asymptomatic or cause
amoebic colitis with abdominal pain, diarrhoea, or dysentery, and can spread through the
portal circulation to produce a liver abscess.

\medterm{Amebiasis Entamoeba Histolytica Infection} Amebiasis is infection with Entamoeba histolytica, acquired through fecally contaminated food, water, or hands. It may cause diarrhea or dysentery and can spread to the liver; stool testing and appropriate tissue-active and luminal treatment are used.

\synonyms
Amoebic Dysentery (Amebiasis Entamoeba Histolytica Infection)

\medterm{Amebic Colitis} Inflammation of the colon caused by infection with Entamoeba histolytica.

\synonyms
Amoebic colitis

\medterm{Amebic Liver Abscess} A focal collection of necrotic material in the liver caused by Entamoeba histolytica,
usually following intestinal infection. It commonly causes fever, right-upper-quadrant
pain, and tender hepatomegaly; diagnosis is supported by imaging and serology.

\medterm{Amelanotic Melanoma} A variant of melanoma that lacks significant melanin production, presenting as a pink,
red, or flesh-coloured papule or nodule rather than a typical dark lesion. Its paucity
of pigment can delay diagnosis, making it a diagnostic challenge. Histopathology with
immunohistochemical markers confirms the diagnosis.

\medterm{Amelia} A rare congenital anomaly characterized by complete absence of one or more limbs. It may occur alone or as part of a
genetic or developmental syndrome. Prenatal imaging can identify the anomaly and help
assess associated abnormalities.

\medterm{Amelioration} Improvement or reduction in the severity of a symptom, disease, impairment, or other adverse condition.

\medterm{Ameloblastoma} Locally aggressive odontogenic tumor. Multicystic radiolucency in posterior mandible.
High recurrence rate. Treatment: surgical resection with margins.

\medterm{Amelogenesis} The formation and maturation of tooth enamel by ameloblasts during tooth development.

\medterm{Amelogenesis Imperfecta} A group of inherited disorders in which enamel formation is abnormal, causing enamel that may be thin, poorly mineralized, weak, or easily worn on most or all teeth.

\medterm{Amenorrhea} Amenorrhea is the absence of menstrual periods. Primary amenorrhea is failure to experience menarche by age 15 despite normal secondary sexual development, or by age 13 when secondary sexual development is absent. Secondary amenorrhea is the absence of menstruation for 3 months in someone with previously regular cycles or 6 months in someone with irregular cycles. It may be physiological or caused by pregnancy, endocrine disorders, reproductive-system abnormalities, medications, illness, inadequate energy availability, or excessive exercise.

\medterm{American Trypanosomiasis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Chagas disease}.

\medterm{AMI} Acute myocardial infarction, the sudden death of heart muscle caused by prolonged inadequate coronary blood flow.

\medterm{Amino Acid} An organic molecule containing an amino group, a carboxyl group, a hydrogen atom, and a variable side chain attached
to a central carbon. Amino acids are the building blocks of proteins; cells use some
also in energy metabolism and other biochemical pathways.

\medterm{Amino Acid Catabolism} The breakdown of amino acids after protein digestion, with the amino group removed and
the carbon skeleton metabolized for energy or gluconeogenesis/ketogenesis.
Transamination: amino groups are transferred to alpha-ketoglutarate, forming glutamate,
by aminotransferases requiring pyridoxal phosphate (PLP, vitamin B6) as a cofactor.

\medterm{Amino Acid Symbols} Standard one-letter and three-letter abbreviations used to identify the 20 common protein-building amino acids in biochemical and genetic notation.

\medterm{Amino Acids} Amino acids are organic molecules that form proteins and also serve as precursors or fuels in human metabolism.

\medterm{Aminoaciduria} Aminoaciduria is abnormal urinary loss of amino acids caused by renal tubular transport defects, inherited metabolic disease, or generalized tubular injury.

\medterm{Aminoglycoside Nephrotoxicity} Kidney damage caused by aminoglycoside antibiotics, characterized by acute tubular
necrosis. Nephrotoxicity is caused by accumulation of aminoglycosides in the proximal
tubular cells. The risk is increased with prolonged therapy, high doses, concurrent
nephrotoxic drugs, and renal impairment.

\medterm{Aminoglycoside Ototoxicity} Damage to the inner ear caused by aminoglycoside antibiotics, resulting in hearing loss
and vestibular dysfunction. Ototoxicity is caused by accumulation of aminoglycosides in
the inner ear, damaging hair cells. The risk is increased with prolonged therapy, high
doses, concurrent ototoxic drugs, and renal impairment.

\medterm{Aminoglycosides} A class of bactericidal antibiotics that bind irreversibly to the 30S ribosomal subunit,
causing misreading of messenger RNA and inhibiting protein synthesis. They include
gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin, and streptomycin.

\medterm{Aminophylline Overdose} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to Aminophylline. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Aminotransferase} An enzyme that transfers an amino group between an amino acid and an alpha-keto acid; clinically measured examples include ALT and AST.

\medterm{Amiodarone-Induced Skin Discoloration} A slate-grey to bluish hyperpigmentation of sun-exposed skin caused by long-term
amiodarone therapy, due to deposition of the drug and its metabolites in dermal
macrophages. It is dose-related, more pronounced in photodistributed areas, and may be
partially reversible upon drug cessation.

\medterm{Amiodarone-Induced Thyroid Dysfunction} Thyroid abnormalities occurring in approximately fifteen to twenty percent of patients
on amiodarone due to the high iodine content of the molecule. Type one results from
iodine-induced increased thyroid hormone synthesis in patients with underlying thyroid
disease. Type two results from destructive thyroiditis.

\medterm{Amiodarone-Induced Vortex Keratopathy} Whorl-like corneal epithelial deposits caused by amiodarone accumulation, appearing as
golden-brown verticillata on slit-lamp examination, usually asymptomatic but reversible
upon drug discontinuation.

\medterm{Amitriptyline} A tricyclic antidepressant that affects serotonin and norepinephrine signaling and is also used for selected chronic pain, migraine-prevention, and sleep-related indications.

\medterm{Amitriptyline And Perphenazine Overdose} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to Amitriptyline and perphenazine. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Amitriptyline Hydrochloride Overdose} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to Amitriptyline hydrochloride. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{AML} Acute myeloid leukemia, an aggressive cancer of immature myeloid blood-cell precursors that crowds the bone marrow and impairs normal blood-cell production.

\medterm{Ammonia} A nitrogen-containing waste product produced during protein metabolism and normally converted to urea by the liver. Elevated
blood ammonia may occur with liver failure, urea-cycle disorders, some medicines, or other metabolic conditions.

\medterm{Ammonia Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures venous or arterial blood ammonia concentration, a nitrogenous waste product of protein metabolism normally cleared by the liver. Elevated levels may indicate hepatic failure, urea-cycle disorders, or valproic acid toxicity, and correlate with risk of encephalopathy.

\medterm{Ammonia Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to ammonia. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Ammonium Hydroxide Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to ammonium hydroxide. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Amnesia} Loss or impairment of memory that is greater than ordinary forgetting. It may affect the
ability to form new memories (anterograde amnesia), recall past events (retrograde
amnesia), or both.

\medterm{Amnesia, Transient Global} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Transient global amnesia}.

\medterm{Amnestic Syndrome} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Amnesia}.

\medterm{Amniocentesis} A procedure in which a needle is used to obtain amniotic fluid for genetic, chromosomal, infectious, or other
prenatal testing. It is usually performed after the first trimester when clinically
indicated; risks and benefits depend on the reason for testing and gestational age.

\medterm{Amnioinfusion} Infusion of sterile fluid into the amniotic sac, often to reduce umbilical-cord compression during labor.

\medterm{Amnion} The innermost fetal membrane, lining the amniotic cavity and containing the amniotic
fluid. A thin, avascular membrane composed of a single layer of cuboidal epithelium
overlying a basement membrane and connective tissue. The amnion produces and resorbs
amniotic fluid. It fuses with the chorion by 12-14 weeks.

\medterm{Amnion Nodosum} Multiple small nodules of vernix or amniotic debris attached to the amnion, usually associated with reduced or absent fetal urine contribution to amniotic fluid and oligohydramnios.

\medterm{Amnioscope} An instrument used to inspect amniotic fluid and the membranes through the cervix.

\medterm{Amniotic Band} A strand of amniotic tissue that can entangle or constrict developing fetal body parts.

\synonyms
Amniotic band sequence

\medterm{Amniotic Band Sequence} Amniotic band sequence results when fibrous amniotic strands entangle developing fetal parts, producing constriction rings, limb defects, or craniofacial and body-wall abnormalities.

\medterm{Amniotic Band Syndrome} A spectrum of congenital anomalies caused by strands of a ruptured amniotic membrane that constrict or entangle developing fetal parts. Effects range from ring-like limb constrictions, swelling, or loss of digits to facial and body-wall disruptions; severity varies widely, and management depends on the structures involved.

\medterm{Amniotic Constriction Bands} Alternate descriptive label; see \textit{Amniotic band sequence}.

\medterm{Amniotic Fluid} The clear to pale-yellow fluid surrounding the fetus within the amniotic sac. Early in
pregnancy it is derived largely from maternal plasma; later, fetal urine and lung fluid
are major sources, and the fetus continuously swallows and recycles it.

\medterm{Amniotic Fluid Embolism} A rare obstetric emergency involving sudden respiratory or cardiovascular collapse and often severe coagulopathy
during labor, delivery, or shortly afterward. It requires immediate supportive and multidisciplinary treatment.

\medterm{Amniotic Fluid Index} An ultrasound estimate of amniotic-fluid volume obtained by dividing the uterus into four quadrants,
measuring the deepest unobstructed vertical pocket in each quadrant, and adding the
measurements. Interpretation uses gestational-age and practice-specific thresholds to
identify unusually low or high fluid volume.

\medterm{Amoebiasis} Infection caused by the protozoan parasite Entamoeba histolytica, transmitted via the
fecal-oral route (contaminated food or water).

\medterm{Amoebic Dysentery (Amebiasis Entamoeba Histolytica Infection)} Alternate index label for Amebiasis Entamoeba histolytica Infection.

\medterm{Amok} A historical culture-related term for a sudden episode of uncontrolled, often violent behavior followed by exhaustion or amnesia; modern assessment considers psychiatric, neurologic, substance-related, and cultural factors.

\medterm{Amoxicillin Rash In Infectious Mononucleosis} A maculopapular, non-allergic rash that develops in up to 90 percent of patients with
Epstein-Barr virus infectious mononucleosis who receive amoxicillin or ampicillin. The
rash is often widespread and pruritic, and its presence does not indicate true
penicillin allergy.

\medterm{Amphetamine} A central nervous system stimulant that increases catecholamine signaling and is used in selected attention-deficit/hyperactivity and sleep disorders; misuse can cause dependence and cardiovascular or psychiatric harm.

\medterm{AMPK} AMP-activated protein kinase. Energy sensor. Activated by AMP. Inhibits anabolism,
stimulates catabolism.

\medterm{Amplified Musculoskeletal Pain Syndrome} A chronic pain condition, often beginning in childhood or adolescence, in which pain is unusually intense or widespread and may be accompanied by sensitivity, fatigue, or functional limitation despite no single structural explanation; it is also called AMPS.

\medterm{Ampulla} A widened portion of a tubular structure, such as the ampulla of the bile and pancreatic ducts or the ampulla of a fallopian tube.

\medterm{Ampullary Cancer} Cancer arising in the ampulla of Vater, where the bile duct and pancreatic duct empty into the small intestine.

\medterm{Ampullary Carcinoma} A malignant epithelial tumor arising in or around the ampulla of Vater, where the bile duct and pancreatic duct enter the duodenum.
It commonly presents with obstructive jaundice, abnormal liver tests, pancreatitis, or gastrointestinal bleeding.
Histologic subtype and local extension influence prognosis, staging, and treatment selection.
Diagnosis uses endoscopy, cross-sectional imaging, and tissue sampling, with surgery considered for resectable disease.

\medterm{Amputation} Surgical removal of a limb or part of a limb. The level may involve a digit, foot, lower leg,
thigh, or hip, or a corresponding part of the upper limb. The level is selected according to tissue viability,
function, and potential for rehabilitation.

\medterm{Amputation Stump} The remaining part of a limb after amputation. It must be monitored for wound healing,
infection, poor circulation, neuroma pain, phantom-limb pain, and skin problems caused by prosthetic use.
Care includes hygiene, compression or shaping when advised, progressive strengthening, and professional
prosthetic and rehabilitation assessment.

\medterm{Amsler Grid} A simple chart used to detect or monitor distortions and missing areas in central vision, especially in macular disease.

\medterm{Amusia} An acquired or developmental inability to perceive, recognize, produce, or understand music despite otherwise adequate hearing and language ability.

\medterm{Amygdala} Almond-shaped nucleus cluster in the anterior medial temporal lobe, part of the limbic
system, primarily processing emotions (especially fear/anxiety). Receives
thalamic/cortical sensory input; projects to hypothalamus, brainstem, and cortex for
autonomic/behavioral fear responses.

\medterm{Amygdaloid Nucleus} A group of nuclei in the medial temporal lobe involved in processing emotional significance, fear responses, learning, memory, and autonomic responses.

\medterm{Amylase} An enzyme that breaks down starch and is produced mainly by the salivary glands and pancreas. Blood levels may rise in several
conditions, including some pancreatic disorders.

\medterm{Amylase - Blood} A blood test measuring amylase, an enzyme produced mainly by the pancreas and salivary glands; elevated values may occur in pancreatitis and other conditions.

\medterm{Amylase - Urine} A urine test measuring amylase excretion, sometimes used with blood amylase when evaluating pancreatic or salivary-gland disease.

\medterm{Amylase And Lipase} Digestive enzymes measured in blood when pancreatic disease is suspected. Lipase is generally more specific
for pancreatic injury and remains elevated longer than amylase, but neither test alone
diagnoses acute pancreatitis; results must be interpreted with symptoms and imaging.

\medterm{Amylin Analogues} Medicines that mimic amylin, a hormone released with insulin by pancreatic beta cells. Pramlintide slows gastric
emptying, reduces post-meal glucagon secretion, and may be used with insulin in selected patients with diabetes.

\medterm{Amyloid} An abnormal protein that can accumulate in tissues and impair their function in several diseases.

\medterm{Amyloid Disease} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Amyloidosis}.

\medterm{Amyloid Disease (Amyloidosis)} Alternate index label for Amyloidosis.

\medterm{Amyloid Neuropathy} Peripheral nerve damage caused by deposition of abnormal amyloid protein.

\medterm{Amyloid PET} A positron-emission tomography scan using a tracer that binds to amyloid deposits in the brain. It can support evaluation of selected
cognitive disorders but is interpreted with clinical assessment and other tests.

\medterm{Amyloid Precursor Protein} A normal transmembrane protein that can be processed into amyloid-beta peptides, which may accumulate in the brain in Alzheimer disease.

\synonyms
APP; amyloid-beta precursor protein.

\medterm{Amyloid Protein} A normally soluble protein that has misfolded into insoluble fibrils and may accumulate in tissues.

\synonyms
Amyloid

\medterm{Amyloid Purpura} Purpuric discoloration of the eyelids and periorbital skin (raccoon eyes) caused by
cutaneous amyloid deposition, which weakens vessel walls and leads to extravasation of
erythrocytes. It may be a sign of systemic amyloidosis, particularly AL amyloidosis, and
warrants further workup including serum free light chains and tissue biopsy.

\medterm{Amyloidosis} A group of diseases in which misfolded proteins form insoluble deposits called amyloid in tissues and organs. The deposits may be limited to one site or spread throughout the body and can impair organs such as the kidneys, heart, nerves, liver, or digestive tract. On biopsy, amyloid typically binds Congo red dye and shows apple-green birefringence under polarized light.

\synonyms
Amyloid Disease (Amyloidosis)

\medterm{Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis} Progressive neurodegenerative disorder affecting both upper and lower motor neurons,
causing weakness, atrophy, fasciculations, and eventually respiratory failure. Most
cases are sporadic (90 percent); 10 percent are familial. Average age of onset 55-60
years.

\medterm{Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS)} A progressive neurodegenerative disease that damages motor neurons, causing muscle weakness, loss of movement, speech or swallowing difficulty, and eventually respiratory failure.

\synonyms
Lou Gehrig's Disease

\medterm{ANA} Antinuclear antibody, an autoantibody directed against components of cell nuclei; ANA testing can support evaluation of autoimmune disease but is not diagnostic by itself.

\medterm{Anabolic Steroids} Synthetic derivatives of testosterone used in selected medical conditions and misused for muscle building or performance enhancement. Nonmedical use can cause acne, infertility, testicular shrinkage, breast enlargement, mood disturbance, and cardiovascular disease, and sudden cessation may produce symptoms of hormone deficiency.

\medterm{Anabolism} The set of metabolic pathways that construct complex molecules from simpler precursors,
requiring energy input (ATP, reducing equivalents NADPH). Examples: protein synthesis
from amino acids, glycogen synthesis (glycogenesis), fatty acid synthesis (lipogenesis),
cholesterol synthesis, nucleotide synthesis, gluconeogenesis.

\medterm{Anaemia} A condition in which the number of red blood cells or the hemoglobin concentration is
below the normal range, reducing the oxygen-carrying capacity of the blood.

\medterm{Anaerobe} A microorganism that grows without molecular oxygen. Obligate anaerobes may be injured
or killed by oxygen, while aerotolerant anaerobes tolerate oxygen but do not use it for
energy production.

\medterm{Anaerobic} Able to live, grow, or function without oxygen.

\medterm{Anaerobic Bacteria} Bacteria that grow without oxygen; some are harmed by oxygen.

\medterm{Anaesthesia} Reversible loss of sensation, with or without loss of consciousness, produced by medicines or other techniques; also spelled anesthesia.

\medterm{Anagen} The active growth phase of the hair cycle.

\medterm{Anal Abscess} Collection of pus in the perianal or ischiorectal space usually from infected anal
glands. Presents with throbbing anal pain, swelling, fever, and difficulty sitting.
Diagnosed clinically with tenderness and fluctuance. Treatment is incision and drainage
under local anesthesia. Antibiotics are not routinely required for simple abscesses.

\medterm{Anal Canal} The terminal 3-4 cm of the gastrointestinal tract, extending from the anorectal junction
(puborectalis/pectinate line) to the anal verge. Above the pectinate line: lined by
columnar epithelium, innervated by autonomic nerves, draining to portal system, supplied
by superior rectal artery.

\medterm{Anal Cancer} Malignant tumor of the anal canal or margin, most commonly squamous cell carcinoma
associated with high-risk human papillomavirus infection. Presents with bleeding, pain,
or a mass; primary treatment is chemoradiation, with surgery reserved for residual or
recurrent disease.

\medterm{Anal Carcinoma} Cancer arising in the anus or anal canal.

\synonyms
Anal cancer

\medterm{Anal Condyloma} A benign wart-like squamous lesion of the anus or perianal skin, usually caused by low-risk HPV types. Multiple or recurrent lesions may coexist with high-grade intraepithelial neoplasia.

\medterm{Anal Fissure} Linear tear in the distal anal mucosa usually posterior in the midline. Presents with
painful defecation and bright red blood on wiping or in the toilet. Associated with
constipation, hard stools, and chronic diarrhea. The internal anal sphincter spasm
perpetuates the fissure by reducing blood flow to the posterior midline.

\synonyms
Anal Ulcer (Anal Fissure)

\medterm{Anal Fistula} Abnormal tract connecting the anal canal to the perianal skin usually following a
drained abscess. Presents with recurrent drainage, pain, and pruritus. Parks
classification categorizes fistulae by relationship to the sphincter muscles. Diagnosed
by examination under anesthesia and MRI.

\medterm{Anal Intercourse} The insertion of the penis into the anus and rectum for sexual pleasure. The anal canal
is rich in nerve endings, making it a sensitive erogenous zone. The rectum lacks natural
lubrication, so artificial lubricants are essential to prevent mucosal trauma.

\medterm{Anal Intraepithelial Neoplasia} A premalignant squamous lesion of the anal canal or perianal skin, usually related to persistent high-risk HPV infection. It ranges from low- to high-grade disease and may progress to anal squamous cell carcinoma.

\medterm{Anal Itching} Pruritus around the anus. Common causes include irritation, moisture, dermatitis, hemorrhoids, infections, and
occasionally pinworms or other disease. Gentle cleansing, keeping the area dry, and avoiding irritants may help;
persistent, severe, bleeding, or painful symptoms require medical evaluation.

\synonyms
Itching Anal (Anal Itching)
Pruritis Ani
Rectal Itching


\medterm{Anal Mucositis} Inflammation and ulceration of the anal mucous membrane, often after radiation or chemotherapy.

\medterm{Anal Necrosis} Death of tissue in or around the anus.

\medterm{Anal Pain} Pain felt in or around the anus.

\synonyms
Proctalgia

\medterm{Anal Papilla} A small normal projection of mucosa at the anal canal’s dentate line, where columnar and squamous epithelium meet.

\medterm{Anal Papillae} Multiple small mucosal projections at the dentate line of the anal canal; they are usually normal but may become enlarged or symptomatic.

\medterm{Anal Sphincter} A muscular structure that controls passage of stool and gas from the anal canal. It includes the involuntary internal anal sphincter and the voluntary external anal sphincter.

\medterm{Anal Ulcer (Anal Fissure)} Alternate index label for Anal Fissure.

\medterm{Analgesia} Absence or reduction of pain without necessarily causing loss of consciousness.

\medterm{Analog} A structure, substance, or process that resembles another and performs a similar function but is not necessarily chemically or structurally identical.

\medterm{Analogous} Similar in function or purpose to another structure or process, despite having a different origin or structure.

\medterm{Analysis} Systematic examination or interpretation of a specimen, measurement, dataset, symptom pattern, or clinical problem.

\medterm{Analysis Of Urine} Laboratory examination of urine for cells, blood, protein, glucose, microorganisms, and other
substances.

\medterm{Analytic Sensitivity} The smallest amount or concentration of an analyte that a laboratory method can reliably
detect. It concerns measurement performance and differs from clinical sensitivity, which
describes detection of disease in affected patients.

\medterm{Anaphia} Loss or absence of the sense of touch or tactile sensation.

\medterm{Anaphylactic Shock} Anaphylactic shock is severe anaphylaxis causing distributive shock, profound hypotension, and inadequate tissue perfusion. It may include airway swelling, bronchospasm, skin or mucosal findings, vomiting, abdominal pain, dizziness, collapse, or cardiac arrest. It is a medical emergency requiring immediate treatment.

\medterm{Anaphylactoid Purpura} An older term for IgA vasculitis, an immune-mediated small-vessel vasculitis causing palpable purpura, often with joint, abdominal, or kidney involvement.

\medterm{Anaphylaxis} A severe, potentially life-threatening systemic hypersensitivity reaction characterized
by rapid onset of multisystem involvement, primarily affecting the skin, respiratory
tract, cardiovascular system, and gastrointestinal tract.

\medterm{Anaphylaxis During Anesthesia} A life-threatening systemic hypersensitivity reaction that may cause sudden hypotension,
bronchospasm, cardiovascular collapse, and sometimes urticaria or flushing. Common
perioperative triggers include neuromuscular-blocking agents, antibiotics,
chlorhexidine, and latex.

\medterm{Anaplasia} A lack of differentiation, describing cells that have reverted to a more primitive,
embryonic phenotype with loss of the specialized structural and functional
characteristics of their tissue of origin.

\medterm{Anaplastic Astrocytoma} A high-grade astrocytic brain tumor with rapid growth and infiltrative behavior.

\medterm{Anaplastic Ependymoma} An aggressive, high-grade tumor arising from cells lining the brain’s ventricles or spinal canal.

\medterm{Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma} A rare T-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma characterized by large abnormal lymphocytes and expression of the CD30 marker; it may be systemic or skin-limited.

\medterm{Anaplastic Thyroid Cancer} A rare, rapidly growing, and highly aggressive thyroid cancer that often presents as a
large fixed neck mass with dysphagia or airway compromise. Prognosis is poor; management
is usually palliative.

\medterm{Anaptic} Lacking a normal sense of touch or tactile sensation; the term is rarely used in modern clinical writing.

\medterm{Anasarca} Severe, generalized edema involving large areas of the body, often associated with heart failure, kidney disease, liver disease, or profound hypoalbuminemia.

\medterm{Anastomosis} A natural or surgically created connection between two tubular structures, such as blood vessels or segments of the intestine.

\synonyms
Anastomosis, Intestinal

\medterm{Anastomosis (Vascular)} A surgical or natural connection between blood vessels that permits blood to flow from one vessel
to another.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Anastomosis, Intestinal} Alternate index label; see \textit{Anastomosis}.

\medterm{Anastomotic Leak} Breakdown of a surgical anastomosis in the gastrointestinal tract causing spillage of
luminal contents into the peritoneal cavity. Presents with fever, tachycardia, abdominal
pain, leukocytosis, and peritonitis. Diagnosed by CT with oral or rectal contrast
showing extraluminal contrast or fluid collection.

\medterm{Anastrozole} An aromatase inhibitor that lowers estrogen production and is used mainly in hormone-receptor-positive breast cancer, particularly after menopause.

\medterm{Anatomic Orientation Terms} Standard words used to describe the position and relationships of body structures, including superior, inferior, anterior, posterior, medial, lateral, proximal, distal, superficial, and deep.

\medterm{Anatomic Snuffbox} A triangular depression on the lateral wrist that becomes visible when the thumb is extended. Tenderness there may suggest a scaphoid fracture.

\medterm{Anatomical Axis} An imaginary straight line around which a body part moves or rotates.

\medterm{Anatomical Conduit} A natural or surgically created passage that carries fluid, air, tissue, or another structure.

\medterm{Anatomical Orifice} A natural opening into or through a body structure.

\synonyms
Body opening

\medterm{Anatomical Planes} Hypothetical flat surfaces that pass through the body in anatomical position, used to
slice the body for anatomical descriptions or medical imaging.

\medterm{Anatomical Position} The standard reference position of the body used to describe anatomy, defined as
standing erect, facing forward, with arms at the sides and palms facing forward.

\medterm{Anatomical Snuffbox} Triangular depression on the dorsal wrist, bounded by extensor pollicis longus medially
and extensor pollicis brevis/abductor pollicis longus laterally. Floor contains the
scaphoid, trapezium, and radial artery. Tenderness after a fall on an outstretched hand
suggests scaphoid fracture. The radial artery pulse is palpable here.

\medterm{Anatomist} A specialist who studies the structure of the body.

\medterm{Anatomy} Study of body structure. Regions: head, neck, thorax, abdomen, pelvis, extremities.
Systems: skeletal, muscular, nervous, circulatory, respiratory, digestive, urinary,
reproductive, endocrine, immune.

\medterm{Anatomy Survey (Level II Ultrasound)} A comprehensive fetal ultrasound performed at 18-22 weeks to evaluate fetal anatomy and
screen for structural anomalies.

\synonyms
Level II ultrasound; anatomy scan.

\medterm{Anatomy, Gross} The study of body structures visible without a microscope, including organs, regions, bones, muscles, vessels, and their relationships.

\medterm{ANCA-Associated Vasculitis} A group of small-vessel vasculitides associated with antineutrophil cytoplasmic
antibodies. Includes granulomatosis with polyangiitis (c-ANCA/PR3), microscopic
polyangiitis (p-ANCA/MPO), and eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis (p-ANCA/MPO
with asthma/eosinophilia).

\medterm{Ancillary Test (Pathology)} A test performed in addition to routine morphology to refine a pathologic diagnosis.
Examples include IHC, flow cytometry, cytogenetics, molecular testing, special stains,
and electron microscopy.

\synonyms
Pathology

\medterm{Ancylostoma (Hookworm)} Nematode infestation by Ancylostoma duodenale (Old World) or Necator americanus (New
World), common in tropical and subtropical regions where sanitation is poor.

\synonyms
Hookworm

\medterm{Androgen} A sex hormone, such as testosterone, that contributes to male reproductive development and other body functions.

\medterm{Androgen Deficiency} Hypogonadism in males: reduced testosterone production causing symptoms and signs.
Causes: primary (testicular—Klinefelter, mumps orchitis, chemotherapy, trauma) and
secondary (pituitary/hypothalamic—prolactinoma, iron overload, pituitary lesions,
opioids, obesity, aging).

\medterm{Androgen Excess} Abnormally high androgen activity or concentration, which can cause acne, excess hair, or menstrual changes.

\synonyms
Hyperandrogenism

\medterm{Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome} A difference of sex development in which the cells of a genetically male fetus cannot respond to androgens because of a defect in the androgen receptor. Affected individuals have typical female external genitalia and breast development but no uterus, and testes that may lie in the groin or abdomen; diagnosis and care require specialist endocrine and genetic assessment.

\medterm{Androgen Insufficiency} Inadequate androgen production or activity for a person’s physiologic needs.

\synonyms
Androgen deficiency

\medterm{Androgen Receptor} A cellular protein that binds testosterone or related hormones and mediates their effects.

\medterm{Androgen Suppression} Reduction of androgen production or action, used in selected hormone-sensitive conditions.

\synonyms
Androgen deprivation

\medterm{Androgenetic Alopecia} The most common cause of hair loss, caused by progressive miniaturization of genetically
susceptible hair follicles under androgen influence. Male pattern commonly affects the
frontal hairline and vertex; female pattern commonly causes diffuse crown thinning with
relative preservation of the frontal hairline.

\medterm{Androgenic} Relating to androgens or producing masculine sexual development and other androgen-mediated effects.

\medterm{Android Pelvis} A relatively heart-shaped or funnel-shaped pelvic configuration with a narrower midpelvis, which may increase the risk of obstructed labor compared with a gynecoid pelvis.

\medterm{Andropause} Age-related testosterone decline in men. Symptoms: fatigue, decreased libido,
depression. Treatment controversial.

\medterm{Anejaculation} Absence of ejaculation. Causes: neurological, surgical, pharmacological, psychological.

\medterm{Anemia} A reduction in the oxygen-carrying capacity of the blood, defined by a hemoglobin
concentration below the normal range for age and sex: less than 13.5 g/dL in adult
males, less than 12.0 g/dL in adult females.

\synonyms
Blood Low Red Cell Count (Anemia)

\medterm{Anemia (Cancer)} Anemia occurring in a person with cancer, caused by the cancer itself, bleeding, inflammation, nutritional deficiency,
or treatment. Evaluation identifies the cause; management may include treating the
underlying problem, iron or other replacement, transfusion, or selected medicines.

\synonyms
Cancer

\medterm{Anemia Caused By Low Iron - Children} Iron-deficiency anemia in a child caused by insufficient iron intake, increased requirements, blood loss, or impaired absorption. It reduces hemoglobin production and may cause pallor, fatigue, irritability, or impaired development.

\medterm{Anemia Caused By Low Iron - Infants And Toddlers} Iron-deficiency anemia in an infant or toddler, commonly related to inadequate dietary iron, excessive cow's milk intake, increased growth requirements, or blood loss. It may cause pallor, fatigue, irritability, and developmental effects.

\medterm{Anemia In Pregnancy} Anemia occurring during pregnancy, commonly because of iron deficiency or the normal plasma-volume expansion of pregnancy.
Assessment considers hemoglobin, mean corpuscular volume, iron status, folate, vitamin B12, bleeding, and other causes.

\synonyms
Gestational anemia; pregnancy-associated anemia.

\medterm{Anemia Of Chronic Disease} Anemia of inflammation. Normocytic or microcytic. Low iron, low TIBC, normal/high
ferritin.

\medterm{Anemia Of Inflammation} An anemia caused by chronic infection, inflammatory disease, or cancer, involving impaired iron availability, reduced erythropoietin response, and shortened red-cell survival.

\medterm{Anemic} Having anemia, meaning reduced red-cell mass or hemoglobin and therefore reduced blood oxygen-carrying capacity.

\medterm{Anencephaly} A fatal neural tube defect where the cerebral hemispheres and cranial vault fail to
develop due to failed closure of the rostral neuropore during the fourth week of
gestation. The brainstem and spinal cord are present, and reflex movements may occur,
but there is no cognitive function.

\medterm{Anergy} State of T-lymphocyte non-responsiveness to nominal antigen despite prior sensitization,
reflecting functional inactivation rather than cell death.

\medterm{Anesthesia} The use of medications to prevent sensation, particularly pain, during medical
procedures or surgery.

\medterm{Anesthesia For Coagulopathy} Coagulopathy can increase bleeding risk during an operation or invasive procedure. Anesthetic planning
considers the cause, bleeding history, platelet count, coagulation tests, fibrinogen,
the procedure, and available correction or replacement therapies.

\medterm{Anesthesia For Epilepsy} Patients with epilepsy generally require continuation of antiseizure medication
perioperatively because missed doses increase seizure risk. Propofol and benzodiazepines
are anticonvulsant; high concentrations of some volatile agents may produce epileptiform
activity.

\medterm{Anesthesia For Hemophilia} Hemophilia A results from factor VIII deficiency and hemophilia B from factor IX
deficiency. Perioperative care is coordinated with hematology and uses factor
replacement or, in selected mild hemophilia A cases, desmopressin.

\medterm{Anesthesia For Neurosurgery} Neuroanesthesia aims to maintain cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP = mean arterial
pressure minus intracranial pressure), optimize brain relaxation, and minimize secondary
brain injury. Induction agents (propofol, etomidate) reduce cerebral metabolic rate and
intracranial pressure.

\medterm{Anesthesia For Pheochromocytoma} Anesthetic care for pheochromocytoma requires control of catecholamine effects before surgery,
careful management of blood pressure and heart rate, and preparation for rapid hemodynamic changes.

\medterm{Anesthesia For Porphyria} Anesthetic care in acute porphyria requires avoidance of medicines known to precipitate attacks and attention to
nutrition, pain, and metabolic stability. The specific drug plan should be checked against a current porphyria-safety reference.

\medterm{Anesthesia Gas Analyzers} Anesthesia gas analyzers measure delivered and exhaled concentrations of oxygen, carbon
dioxide, and volatile anesthetic agents. The oxygen analyzer measures inspired oxygen
concentration; pulse oximetry measures peripheral hemoglobin oxygen saturation.

\medterm{Anesthesia Informatics} Anesthesia informatics integrates electronic health records, decision support systems,
and data analytics into clinical practice. Automated documentation reduces charting
time. Clinical decision support alerts for drug interactions and dosing errors. Big data
analytics identify patterns in anesthesia outcomes.

\medterm{Anesthesia Machine} The anesthesia machine delivers precise mixtures of oxygen, air, and volatile
anesthetics to the patient.

\medterm{Anesthesia Monitoring} Continuous assessment of vital functions during anesthesia, typically including oxygenation, ventilation, circulation,
temperature, and depth of anesthesia as clinically indicated.

\medterm{Anesthesia Pharmacogenomics} Anesthesia pharmacogenomics studies genetic variations affecting drug response.
Pseudocholinesterase deficiency causes prolonged succinylcholine paralysis. CYP2D6
polymorphisms affect codeine metabolism to morphine. Malignant hyperthermia
susceptibility is linked to RYR1 gene mutations.

\medterm{Anesthesiologist} A physician who provides anesthesia and manages pain and vital functions during procedures.

\medterm{Anesthesiology} The medical specialty concerned with anesthesia, perioperative care, and pain management.

\medterm{Aneuploidy} The presence of an abnormal number of chromosomes in a cell, with either extra or missing copies of whole chromosomes, such as trisomy 21. It results from errors in cell division and is a major cause of miscarriage, congenital syndromes, and chromosomal disorders identified by prenatal testing.

\medterm{Aneurysm} A localized weakening and enlargement of a blood vessel. Aneurysms may occur in arteries or, rarely, veins and may rupture,
dissect, or form emboli depending on their location and cause.

\medterm{Aneurysm Clip} Surgical clip occluding aneurysm neck. Materials: titanium, stainless steel.
Indications: intracranial aneurysm. Placed via craniotomy. Complications: clip
migration, vessel occlusion, rebleeding.

\medterm{Aneurysm Coils} Flexible coils, often made of platinum, placed inside an aneurysm to promote clotting and reduce blood flow into the aneurysm.

\medterm{Aneurysm In The Brain} A brain aneurysm is a focal weakness and ballooning of a cerebral-artery wall; rupture causes subarachnoid hemorrhage and sudden severe headache.

\medterm{Aneurysm Repair} Surgical or catheter-based treatment that excludes or replaces a weakened, dilated section of a blood vessel when the risk of rupture or other complications justifies intervention.

\medterm{Aneurysms} Abnormal bulges or enlargements in weakened areas of blood-vessel walls that may enlarge, clot, dissect, or rupture.

\medterm{Angelman Syndrome} A genetic disorder caused by loss of function of the UBE3A gene on chromosome 15
(maternal deletion or paternal uniparental disomy). Features: severe intellectual
disability, absent speech, seizures, ataxia, and frequent laughter. Characteristic happy
demeanor with hand-flapping movements.

\medterm{Anger} An emotional response involving displeasure or perceived threat, ranging from mild irritation to intense hostility; persistent or harmful anger may require clinical assessment.

\medterm{Angiitis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Vasculitis}.

\medterm{Angiitis (Vasculitis)} Alternate index label for Vasculitis.

\medterm{Angina} Chest discomfort caused by inadequate oxygen-rich blood flow to the heart muscle, usually from coronary artery disease.


\medterm{Angina - When You Have Chest Pain} Angina with chest pain reflects transient myocardial ischemia; new, severe, prolonged, or rest pain may signal acute coronary syndrome and requires emergency assessment.

\medterm{Angina Pectoris} Chest discomfort caused by myocardial ischemia without infarction. Stable angina occurs
predictably with exertion and resolves with rest. Unstable angina occurs at rest or with
minimal exertion and is an acute coronary syndrome. Prinzmetal (variant) angina occurs
at rest due to coronary vasospasm.

\medterm{Angina Symptoms} Angina is chest discomfort caused by inadequate blood flow to heart muscle, commonly from coronary artery disease. Pressure or squeezing may spread to the arm, jaw, back, or shoulder and can occur with exertion; new, severe, or resting symptoms require emergency assessment.

\synonyms
Ischemic Chest Pain (Angina Symptoms)

\medterm{Angioblastoma} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Hemangioblastoma}.

\medterm{Angiocardiography} Imaging of the heart and major vessels after injection of contrast material.

\medterm{Angiodysplasia} Dilated, fragile blood vessels in the gastrointestinal mucosa or submucosa, often occurring in the right colon of older
adults. They may cause intermittent or recurrent gastrointestinal bleeding and anemia.
