\medterm{Angiodysplasia Of The Colon} A degenerative abnormality of small blood vessels in the colon that can cause intermittent or recurrent lower gastrointestinal bleeding, often without pain. Diagnosis and treatment depend on the bleeding pattern and endoscopic findings.

\medterm{Angioedema} Rapid swelling of the deep dermis/subcutaneous and submucosal tissues (lips, eyelids,
tongue, throat, hands, GI tract) due to mast cell mediator release
(histamine—allergic/idiopathic, urticaria associated) or bradykinin-mediated (hereditary
angioedema—C1 esterase inhibitor deficiency; ACE-inhibitor-induced).

\medterm{Angioedema (Hives)} Alternate index label for Hives.

\medterm{Angioedema And Hives} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Hives and angioedema}.

\medterm{Angioedema Hereditary (Hereditary Angioedema HAE)} Alternate index label for Hereditary Angioedema HAE.

\medterm{Angiofibroma} A benign tumor composed of blood vessels and fibrous tissue.

\medterm{Angiofollicular Lymph Node Hyperplasia} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Castleman disease}.

\medterm{Angiogenesis} Formation of new blood vessels. Tumors induce angiogenesis for growth. Targeted by
anti-angiogenic drugs (bevacizumab).

\medterm{Angiogenesis Inhibition} Suppression of new blood-vessel formation, a strategy used in some cancer treatments.

\synonyms
Antiangiogenic therapy

\medterm{Angiogram} An image of blood vessels, usually obtained after contrast material is injected, used to identify narrowing, blockage, aneurysm, malformation, or bleeding.

\medterm{Angiography} Invasive imaging of blood vessels by injecting contrast material under fluoroscopy.
Coronary angiography (cardiac catheterization): assesses coronary artery stenosis,
guides PCI. Cerebral angiography: evaluates aneurysms, AVMs, and vasculitis. Peripheral
angiography: evaluates PAD and guides intervention.

\medterm{Angioid Streak} Breaks in a tissue layer beneath the retina that appear as irregular dark lines.

\medterm{Angiomyofibroblastoma} A benign mesenchymal tumor of the vulvovaginal region, usually presenting as a small, well-circumscribed painless mass in reproductive-age or middle-aged women.

\medterm{Angiomyolipoma} A benign renal tumor composed of blood vessels, smooth muscle, and fat; it is associated
with tuberous sclerosis and may cause retroperitoneal hemorrhage.

\medterm{Angioneurotic Edema, Hereditary} Hereditary angioedema, an inherited bradykinin-mediated disorder causing recurrent swelling of the skin, gastrointestinal tract, or airway, usually without hives.

\medterm{Angiopathy} Disease or abnormality of blood vessels, including changes caused by diabetes, inflammation, atherosclerosis, trauma, or inherited disorders.

\medterm{Angioplasty} A procedure that widens a narrowed blood vessel, usually with a balloon and sometimes a stent. It is used most often for
coronary or peripheral arterial disease.


\medterm{Angioplasty And Stent Placement - Heart} A catheter-based procedure that widens a narrowed coronary artery, usually with an inflatable balloon, and may place a stent to help keep the artery open and improve blood flow to the heart muscle.

\medterm{Angioplasty And Stent Placement - Peripheral Arteries} A catheter-based procedure that widens a narrowed peripheral artery, usually with an inflatable balloon, and may place a stent to improve blood flow to an affected limb or organ.


\medterm{Angioplasty Balloon (Vascular)} An inflatable catheter balloon used to widen a narrowed blood vessel or expand a stent.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Angiosarcoma} A rare, aggressive malignant tumor of blood vessel or lymphatic endothelial cells.
Common sites include skin, breast, liver, and bone. Treatment typically involves wide
surgical excision with chemotherapy and radiation.

\medterm{Angiosarcoma Of The Breast} A malignant vascular tumor of the breast that may arise primarily or, more rarely, after radiation or chronic lymphoedema. It often presents as an enlarging mass or bruise-like skin discoloration and requires oncologic management.

\medterm{Angiosarcoma Of The Heart} A rare aggressive primary cardiac sarcoma, most often arising in the right atrium and potentially causing obstruction, pericardial effusion, arrhythmia, or metastases. Diagnosis requires imaging and tissue evaluation.

\medterm{Angiotensin} A family of peptides in the renin--angiotensin system that regulate blood pressure and fluid balance, with angiotensin II causing vasoconstriction and stimulating aldosterone release.

\medterm{Angiotensin Converting Enzyme} An enzyme that converts angiotensin I to vasoconstricting angiotensin II and also breaks down bradykinin; it is inhibited by ACE-inhibitor medicines.

\medterm{Angiotensin I} An inactive precursor that is converted to angiotensin II in the renin-angiotensin system.

\medterm{Angiotensin II} A hormone that narrows blood vessels and promotes sodium retention, thereby increasing blood pressure.

\medterm{Angiotensin Receptor Blockers} A class of cardiovascular drugs that selectively block the angiotensin II type 1
receptor, preventing the binding of angiotensin II and its effects including
vasoconstriction, aldosterone secretion, and sympathetic activation.

\medterm{Angle-Closure Glaucoma} Glaucoma caused by narrowing or closure of the drainage angle, which can abruptly raise eye pressure and threaten vision; acute attacks require emergency treatment.

\medterm{Anhedonia} The inability to experience pleasure from activities that were previously enjoyable. It
is a core symptom of major depressive disorder and a negative symptom of schizophrenia.
It is mediated by dysfunction in the brain's reward circuitry, particularly the
mesolimbic dopamine system.

\medterm{Anhidrosis} Absent or greatly reduced sweating. It may result from nerve damage, skin disease, dehydration, medications,
or inherited conditions. Inability to sweat can cause dangerous overheating, especially during
exercise or hot weather.


\medterm{Animal-Assisted Therapy} A structured intervention in which trained animals are incorporated into care to support defined therapeutic
goals. It may be used to support engagement, mood, communication, or activity when
appropriate safeguards are in place.

\medterm{Anion} Anion is an ion with a net negative electrical charge, such as chloride, bicarbonate, or phosphate in body fluids.

\medterm{Anion Gap} A calculated estimate of unmeasured serum anions, commonly sodium minus chloride and
bicarbonate. An increased anion gap supports the presence of unmeasured acids in
metabolic acidosis, but interpretation must account for albumin concentration and
laboratory method.

\medterm{Anisocoria} Unequal pupil size. It may be physiologic or caused by disorders of the eye, autonomic nerves, or brain; sudden anisocoria
with headache, eye pain, or neurologic symptoms requires urgent assessment.

\medterm{Anisocytosis} Increased variation in red blood cell size on a peripheral blood film (e.g., macrocytes
with microcytes together), not a diagnosis in itself. Derived from the Greek
"anisokytosis" (unequal cells). Quantified by an elevated red cell distribution width
(RDW).

\medterm{Anisometropia} A difference in the refractive power of the two eyes, which can cause unequal image focus, blurred vision, or amblyopia.

\medterm{Ankle} The joint connecting the lower leg to the foot.

\medterm{Ankle Arthroplasty} Surgical replacement of the ankle joint with a prosthesis, usually for severe symptomatic
ankle arthritis. Suitability depends on deformity, bone quality, infection risk, activity level, and the condition
of the surrounding joints.

\medterm{Ankle Arthroscopy} A minimally invasive surgical technique using small incisions and an endoscope to visualize and treat intra-articular ankle pathology, including loose bodies, osteochondral lesions, synovitis, and impingement. It offers faster recovery compared with open arthrotomy.

\medterm{Ankle Bone} A bone forming the ankle joint, especially the talus or the distal tibia and fibula that form the ankle mortise.

\medterm{Ankle Dislocated (Dislocated Ankle (Ankle Dislocation))} Alternate index label for Dislocated Ankle (Ankle Dislocation).

\medterm{Ankle Dislocation} Complete displacement of the ankle joint surfaces, usually resulting from a high-energy injury and often
accompanied by a fracture, ligament damage, or neurovascular compromise. It causes severe pain, deformity, and
inability to bear weight. The joint requires urgent reduction, imaging, and immobilization, with surgical management
when instability or associated fractures demand it.

\synonyms
Dislocated Ankle (Ankle Dislocation)

\medterm{Ankle Fracture} A break in one or more bones forming the ankle joint.


\medterm{Ankle Fractures} Ankle fractures: Weber classification (fibular fracture relative to syndesmosis): A
(below syndesmosis, stable), B (at syndesmosis, variable stability), C (above
syndesmosis, unstable). Lauge-Hansen: supination-external rotation (SER, most common),
supination-adduction, pronation-external rotation, pronation-abduction.

\medterm{Ankle Joint} Uniaxial hinge synovial joint between distal tibia, lateral malleolus, and superior
talus, allowing dorsiflexion/plantarflexion. Medial (deltoid) ligament resists eversion;
lateral complex resists inversion. The ankle mortise provides bony stability.

\medterm{Ankle Pain} Pain in or around the ankle, commonly caused by sprain, fracture, tendinitis, arthritis,
bursitis, or infection. Inability to bear weight, deformity, marked swelling, or fever
requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Ankle Pain And Tendinitis} Ankle pain may result from sprain, fracture, arthritis, tendon inflammation, nerve disease, infection, or referred pain. Tendinitis causes pain along an affected tendon and often worsens with loading; swelling, deformity, inability to bear weight, fever, or numbness warrants evaluation.

\medterm{Ankle Replacement} Total ankle arthroplasty, the surgical replacement of the ankle joint with a prosthetic implant for severe arthritis when joint-sparing options are insufficient. It aims to preserve motion and reduce pain; outcomes depend on deformity correction, alignment, and implant selection.


\medterm{Ankle Sprain} An injury to the ligaments surrounding the ankle joint, most commonly involving the
lateral ligament complex (anterior talofibular ligament [ATFL, most commonly injured],
calcaneofibular ligament [CFL], and posterior talofibular ligament [PTFL]) from an
inversion mechanism.


\medterm{Ankle Swelling} Enlargement around the ankle caused by injury, inflammation, infection, venous disease, lymphatic problems, or fluid retention.
One-sided sudden swelling with pain, warmth, or shortness of breath may indicate a blood clot or infection and
requires urgent assessment; swelling after trauma may require evaluation for fracture.

\medterm{Ankle Swollen (Swollen Ankles And Swollen Feet)} Alternate index label for Swollen Ankles and Swollen Feet.

\medterm{Ankle-Brachial Index} The ratio of ankle systolic blood pressure to brachial systolic blood pressure. A low result supports peripheral
arterial disease, while a very high result may indicate noncompressible arteries.

\medterm{Ankle-Foot Orthosis} A brace that supports the ankle and foot and helps control alignment, stability, or foot drop.

\medterm{Ankle-Foot Orthosis (AFO)} A brace that supports the ankle and foot, helps control alignment and motion, and may improve walking in people with weakness, spasticity, instability, or foot drop.

\medterm{Ankyloglossia} An abnormally short or tight lingual frenulum that restricts tongue movement.

\synonyms
Tongue-tie

\medterm{Ankyloglossia (Tongue-Tie)} Short or thick lingual frenulum restricting tongue movement. May cause breastfeeding
difficulties, speech problems, oral hygiene issues. Treatment: frenotomy (simple
release) or frenuloplasty.

\synonyms
Tongue-Tie

\medterm{Ankylose} To become stiff or immobile at a joint, sometimes because the joint surfaces become fused.

\medterm{Ankylosing} Causing or involving progressive stiffness and fixation of a joint, often through fibrous or bony fusion.

\medterm{Ankylosing Spondylitis} A long-term form of arthritis that mainly affects the joints between the spine and pelvis and the spine itself. It causes back pain and stiffness. Over time, new bone may form around the spine, causing some spinal bones to join together and making the back less flexible. It can also affect other joints, the eyes, skin, or bowel.

\synonyms
Arthritis Ankylosing Spondylitis (Ankylosing Spondylitis)

\medterm{Ankylosis} Abnormal stiffness or fusion of a joint. In dentistry, it may refer to fusion of a tooth root to the surrounding bone.

\medterm{Ankyrin} A structural protein that links membrane proteins to the cell’s internal cytoskeleton.

\medterm{Annihilation} Positron-electron annihilation producing two 511 keV photons in opposite directions.

\medterm{Annular Pancreas} A congenital anomaly where a ring of pancreatic tissue completely or partially encircles
the second portion of the duodenum, causing duodenal obstruction. It results from
failure of the ventral pancreatic bud to completely rotate around the duodenum during
embryogenesis.

\medterm{Annulus} A ring-shaped structure or band; examples include the annulus fibrosus around an intervertebral disk and the fibrous rings around heart valves.

\medterm{ANOCA} Angina with non-obstructive coronary arteries: angina symptoms without a flow-limiting obstructive stenosis in the epicardial coronary arteries. Causes may include coronary microvascular dysfunction or vasospastic angina, and further evaluation can help identify the mechanism.

\medterm{Anodontia} Complete absence of teeth. Rare, associated with ectodermal dysplasia.

\medterm{Anogenital Tract} The connected anatomic region comprising the anus, perianal tissues, genital structures, and related mucosal surfaces.

\medterm{Anomalous Anatomy} A structural variation or congenital abnormality in the expected arrangement, number, origin, or course of an organ, vessel, duct, or other body structure. It may be incidental or clinically important during diagnosis, surgery, or interventional procedures.

\medterm{Anomalous Left Coronary Artery From The Pulmonary Artery} A rare congenital anomaly in which the left coronary artery arises from the pulmonary artery instead of the aorta. It can cause myocardial ischemia, heart failure, arrhythmia, or sudden death, particularly in infancy or exertion.

\medterm{Anomaly} A deviation from the usual structure, development, function, or measurement of the body; it may be harmless or clinically significant.

\medterm{Anomia} Difficulty retrieving or naming familiar words.

\medterm{Anophthalmia} Congenital absence of the eye due to failure of optic vesicle development, often
associated with syndromes (e.g., SOX2 mutations). Diagnosis by absence of globe on
imaging or clinical examination; management requires early socket expansion and
prosthetic fitting for orbital and facial development.

\medterm{Anorchia} Complete absence of testicular tissue, present from birth or caused by loss of previously formed testes. It results in absent or severely reduced testosterone production and infertility unless treated with appropriate hormone replacement.

\medterm{Anorectal Abscess} A localized collection of pus in the tissues around the anus or rectum, usually caused
by infection of an anal gland. It commonly causes severe anal pain, swelling, fever, and
difficulty sitting. Prompt incision and drainage is the standard treatment.

\medterm{Anorectal Fistula} An abnormal channel connecting the anus or rectum with nearby skin or another organ.

\synonyms
Anal fistula

\medterm{Anorectal Infection} Infection involving the anus, rectum, or surrounding tissues.

\medterm{Anorectal Malformation} A congenital abnormality in which the anus or rectum does not develop normally or does not connect appropriately
to the perineum. Types vary in anatomy and severity and usually require specialist surgical assessment.

\medterm{Anorectal Manometry} Measurement of pressures in the anal canal and rectum to assess sphincter function and
rectal sensation. A pressure-sensing catheter is inserted into the rectum and slowly
withdrawn through the anal canal. Measures resting and squeeze pressures reflecting
internal and external sphincter function respectively.

\medterm{Anorexia} Loss of appetite, distinct from anorexia nervosa (the eating disorder).

\medterm{Anorexia Nervosa} An eating disorder characterized by restriction of energy intake, intense fear of weight
gain, and distorted body image. Highest mortality of any psychiatric disorder. Features:
emaciation, lanugo, bradycardia, hypotension, amenorrhea, and electrolyte abnormalities.
Diagnosis by DSM-5 criteria.

\synonyms
Eating Disorders, Anorexia

\medterm{Anorgasmia} Persistent or recurrent delay in, or absence of, orgasm following a normal sexual
excitement phase.

\medterm{Anorgasmia In Women} Persistent or recurrent difficulty achieving orgasm despite adequate sexual stimulation, causing personal distress.

\medterm{Anorgasmy} Inability to achieve orgasm despite adequate sexual stimulation; possible contributors include medications, hormonal or neurologic disorders, psychological factors, and relationship difficulties.

\medterm{Anoscopy} Examination of the anal canal with a short lighted instrument called an anoscope. It is
used to evaluate rectal bleeding, hemorrhoids, fissures, fistulas, and other anorectal
disorders.

\medterm{Anoscopy And High-Resolution Anoscopy} An examination of the anal canal and lower rectum using an anoscope; high-resolution anoscopy provides magnified views for evaluation of abnormal tissue.

\medterm{Anosmia} Complete loss of the sense of smell. Causes include post-viral olfactory dysfunction,
chronic rhinosinusitis with polyps, head trauma, neurodegenerative disease, congenital
disorders such as Kallmann syndrome, medications, and toxic exposure.

\medterm{Anosognosia} Denial or unawareness of one's own neurologic deficit, classically hemiplegia following
a nondominant (right) parietal lobe lesion. Associated with right middle cerebral artery
strokes and sometimes with neglect of the left side of space.

\medterm{Anovulation} Failure to release an egg from the ovary during the menstrual cycle. It may cause irregular or absent periods,
infertility, or abnormal bleeding and can result from hormonal disorders, excessive exercise,
low body weight, stress, or polycystic ovary syndrome.

\medterm{Anoxia} Complete absence of oxygen supply to tissues, distinguished from hypoxia (reduced
oxygen). Anoxia causes rapid cell death, particularly in highly aerobic tissues (brain,
heart, kidneys).

\medterm{Antacids} Medications that neutralise gastric acid, providing rapid symptomatic relief in
dyspepsia, gastro-esophageal reflux, and peptic ulcer disease. Commonly contain
aluminium hydroxide, magnesium hydroxide, calcium carbonate, or sodium bicarbonate.

\medterm{Antagonist} A substance, drug, muscle, or other factor that opposes or blocks the action of another; a receptor antagonist binds without activating the receptor.

\medterm{Antecubital Fossa} Triangular depression on the anterior elbow, bounded laterally by brachioradialis,
medially by pronator teres, and superiorly by a line connecting the epicondyles. Floor
formed by brachialis and supinator. Contains biceps tendon, brachial artery, and median
nerve. Primary site for venipuncture and blood pressure measurement.

\medterm{Antegrade} Moving or occurring in the normal forward direction of flow or conduction, such as blood flow away from the heart or electrical conduction through the usual pathway.

\medterm{Antemortem Injury} Injury inflicted upon a body prior to death, characterized microscopically and
macroscopically by physiological tissue responses including hemorrhage, blood clotting,
inflammatory cell infiltration, tissue edema, and active healing reactions
(differentiating it from postmortem artifact).

\medterm{Antenatal} The period before birth, also called prenatal. The medical supervision and screening
provided to pregnant women to monitor the health of the mother and fetus, detect
complications early, and provide health education.

\synonyms
prenatal.

\medterm{Antepartum Fetal Surveillance} The monitoring of fetal well-being in the third trimester for high-risk pregnancies.
Methods: fetal kick counts (daily maternal counting of fetal movements), non-stress test
(NST), biophysical profile (BPP, AFI, fetal tone, movement, breathing), and Doppler
(umbilical artery, MCA).

\medterm{Antepartum Hemorrhage} Bleeding from the genital tract after the threshold of fetal viability and before birth.
Important causes include placenta previa, placental abruption, vasa previa, and local
cervical or vaginal lesions; maternal stabilization and fetal assessment are priorities.

\medterm{Anterior} Relating to the front of the body or positioned closer to the front.

\medterm{Anterior Chamber} The fluid-filled space between the cornea and iris of the eye.

\medterm{Anterior Chamber Angle} The anterior chamber angle is the anatomical region where the cornea and iris meet, and
it contains the trabecular meshwork, the primary drainage structure for aqueous humor
from the eye.

\medterm{Anterior Cord Syndrome} A spinal-cord injury syndrome causing impaired motor function and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the lesion, with relative preservation of vibration and position sense.

\medterm{Anterior Cruciate Ligament} A strong ligament inside the knee that runs from the femur to the tibia and limits forward movement and rotation of the tibia.

\medterm{Anterior Cruciate Ligament (ACL) Injury} A sprain or tear of the ligament that limits forward movement and rotation of the tibia relative to the femur. It commonly occurs during sudden stopping, pivoting, or landing and may cause a pop, rapid swelling, pain, and knee instability.


\medterm{Anterior Cruciate Ligament Injury} Tear of the ACL, a common sports injury (non-contact deceleration, pivoting, or
hyperextension). Presents with a pop, immediate swelling (hemarthrosis), and giving way
of the knee. Positive Lachman test (most sensitive), anterior drawer test, and pivot
shift test.

\medterm{Anterior Cruciate Ligament Tear} ACL tear: common knee injury, often non-contact pivoting sports. Symptoms: popping
sound, swelling, instability, inability to continue play. Diagnosis: Lachman test,
anterior drawer, MRI. Treatment: surgical reconstruction (autograft/allograft),
rehabilitation.

\medterm{Anterior Knee Pain} Pain localized to the front of the knee around or behind the patella, often worsened by stair climbing, squatting, or prolonged sitting. Common causes include patellofemoral pain syndrome, chondromalacia patellae, patellar tendinopathy, and plica syndrome. Evaluation includes physical examination, imaging, and assessment of biomechanical factors.

\medterm{Anterior Myocardial Infarction} A myocardial infarction involving the anterior wall of the left ventricle, usually because of obstruction in the left anterior descending coronary artery.

\synonyms
Anterior heart attack; anterior MI.

\medterm{Anterior Pituitary} The glandular portion of the pituitary that secretes ACTH, TSH, growth hormone,
prolactin, FSH, and LH in response to hypothalamic releasing hormones. Its hormones
regulate adrenal, thyroid, growth, lactation, and reproductive function.

\medterm{Anterior Tibial Artery} Arises from the popliteal artery, passes through the anterior intermuscular septum to
the anterior compartment, continuing to the ankle as the dorsalis pedis artery. Supplies
anterior leg and dorsal foot muscles. Palpable anterior to the lateral malleolus as the
dorsalis pedis pulse.

\medterm{Anterior Uveitis} Anterior uveitis, also known as iritis or iridocyclitis when the ciliary body is
involved, is inflammation of the anterior segment of the eye involving the iris and/or
ciliary body, and it is the most common form of uveitis.

\synonyms
iritis or iridocyclitis when the ciliary body is.

\medterm{Anterior Vaginal Prolapse} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Anterior vaginal prolapse (cystocele)}.

\medterm{Anterior Vaginal Prolapse (Cystocele)} Bulging of the front wall of the vagina caused by weakening of the tissue supporting the bladder, sometimes producing urinary symptoms.

\synonyms
Bladder Prolapse
Prolapsed Bladder

\medterm{Anterior Vaginal Wall Repair} Surgical repair of the anterior vaginal wall to correct cystocele (bladder prolapse), in which the bladder bulges into the vaginal canal. The procedure restores pelvic-floor support and may be performed with or without mesh depending on the defect and surgeon judgment.

\medterm{Anterior/Posterior Drawer Test} A clinical test of knee stability. Anterior displacement assesses the anterior cruciate ligament, and posterior
displacement assesses the posterior cruciate ligament.

\medterm{Anterolisthesis} Forward displacement of one vertebral body relative to the vertebra below it, usually in the spine.

\medterm{Anteroposterior} Directed from front to back; an anteroposterior radiographic view passes through the body from the anterior surface toward the posterior surface.

\medterm{Anteroposterior (AP)} Directed from the front of the body toward the back; an AP radiograph is made with the X-ray beam entering anteriorly and exiting posteriorly.

\medterm{Anthrax} A serious infectious disease caused by the gram-positive bacterium Bacillus anthracis,
which forms highly resistant spores that can persist in the environment for decades, and
it is primarily a zoonotic disease of herbivores (cattle, sheep, goats) that can be
transmitted to humans through contact with infected animals or their products.

\medterm{Anthrax Blood Test} A laboratory test that detects Bacillus anthracis antigens, antibodies, or nucleic acid in blood or other specimens. It is used to confirm suspected anthrax infection, which may present as cutaneous, inhalational, or gastrointestinal disease with potential for severe toxemia.

\medterm{Anthropometry} Body measurements: weight, height, BMI, waist, hip, skinfold thickness. Assessment of
nutritional status and body composition.

\medterm{Anti-Anxiety Agents} Medicines or other treatments used to reduce clinically significant anxiety.

\synonyms
Anxiolytics

\medterm{Anti-CCP} Alternate index label; see \textit{Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide (Anti-CCP) Antibody}.

\medterm{Anti-CCP Antibody} Alternate index label; see \textit{Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide (Anti-CCP) Antibody}.

\medterm{Anti-Citrullinated Peptide Antibody} Alternate index label; see \textit{Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide (Anti-CCP) Antibody}.

\medterm{Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide (Anti-CCP) Antibody} An autoantibody measured in a blood test that targets citrullinated proteins. A positive result
supports rheumatoid arthritis, especially when joint symptoms are present, but it does not diagnose the disease by itself.

\synonyms
Anti-CCP; Anti-CCP antibody; Anti-Citrullinated Peptide Antibody; Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide; cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody.

\medterm{Anti-DNase B Blood Test} A blood test that measures antibodies against deoxyribonuclease B (DNase B), a protein made by group A
Streptococcus. It helps show a recent past strep infection and may help evaluate rheumatic fever or kidney
inflammation that develops after a strep infection.

\medterm{Anti-Double-Stranded DNA (Anti-dsDNA) Antibody} An autoantibody that targets double-stranded DNA. A blood test for it helps evaluate
systemic lupus erythematosus (lupus); high levels may be associated with active disease, especially kidney
involvement, but results must be interpreted with symptoms and other tests.

\synonyms
Anti-DsDNA Antibody; Anti-Double-Stranded DNA Antibody.

\medterm{Anti-GBM Disease} Autoimmune disease with antibodies against alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in
glomerular and alveolar basement membranes. Presents with crescentic GN and pulmonary
hemorrhage (Goodpasture syndrome). Linear IgG deposits on immunofluorescence. Treatment
requires emergent plasmapheresis, cyclophosphamide, and corticosteroids.

\medterm{Anti-Glomerular Basement Membrane Blood Test} A laboratory test that detects circulating anti-glomerular basement membrane antibodies, which target type IV collagen in renal and sometimes pulmonary basement membranes. Positive results support the diagnosis of anti-GBM disease, including Goodpasture syndrome.

\medterm{Anti-Glomerular Basement Membrane Disease} An autoimmune disease in which antibodies target basement-membrane components in the kidney and sometimes the lungs. It can cause rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis, pulmonary hemorrhage, or both and requires urgent treatment.

\medterm{Anti-IgE Therapy} Omalizumab is a monoclonal antibody that binds free IgE, preventing it from binding to
mast cells and basophils. It reduces allergic inflammation and is approved for
moderate-to-severe persistent allergic asthma. It reduces exacerbations and the need for
oral corticosteroids. It is administered by subcutaneous injection every 2--4 weeks.

\medterm{Anti-IL4 Receptor Therapy} Dupilumab is a monoclonal antibody blocking the IL-4 receptor alpha subunit, inhibiting
both IL-4 and IL-13 signaling. It is approved for moderate-to-severe asthma with
eosinophilic phenotype or oral corticosteroid dependence. It reduces exacerbations and
improves lung function.

\medterm{Anti-IL5 Therapy} Treatment with monoclonal antibodies that block interleukin-5 signaling or its receptor,
thereby reducing eosinophil production or survival. It is used for selected eosinophilic diseases, including some
forms of severe asthma.

\medterm{Anti-Insulin Antibody Test} A laboratory test that detects autoantibodies directed against insulin, which may cause hypoglycemia by binding and releasing insulin unpredictably. It is used to evaluate factitious hypoglycemia and insulin autoimmune syndrome.

\medterm{Anti-MDA5 Dermatomyositis} A clinically distinct subtype of dermatomyositis associated with anti-melanoma
differentiation-associated gene 5 antibodies, characterised by skin-predominant disease,
mechanic's hands, and a high risk of rapidly progressive interstitial lung disease.

\medterm{Anti-Mullerian Hormone} A hormone produced by granulosa cells of ovarian follicles. It is measured in selected
fertility and reproductive evaluations, but it does not by itself determine fertility.

\medterm{Anti-Reflux Surgery} An operation intended to reduce reflux of stomach contents into the esophagus, usually by
reinforcing the gastroesophageal junction. It may be considered for selected patients
with gastroesophageal reflux disease or a hiatal hernia.

\medterm{Anti-Reflux Surgery - Children} Surgical procedure to correct gastroesophageal reflux in children, typically by fundoplication, in which the gastric fundus is wrapped around the lower esophagus. It is considered for severe refractory reflux, aspiration, failure to thrive, or esophageal strictures.



\medterm{Anti-Rust Product Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to anti-rust products. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Anti-Smooth Muscle Antibody} An autoantibody associated especially with autoimmune hepatitis, although low levels can occur in other autoimmune or infectious conditions.

\medterm{Antianginal} A class of medications used to prevent or relieve angina pectoris (chest pain from
myocardial ischemia).

\medterm{Antibacterial} Destroying bacteria or inhibiting their growth; the term may describe a drug, disinfectant, surface treatment, or immune defense.

\medterm{Antibiogram} A periodic summary of antimicrobial susceptibility patterns of bacterial isolates from a
hospital or laboratory. Guides empiric antibiotic therapy by showing local resistance
patterns (e.g., \% of E. coli susceptible to TMP-SMX). Updates annually.

\medterm{Antibodies} Immunoglobulin proteins produced by B cells that bind to specific antigens. There are
five classes: IgG (most abundant), IgM (first produced), IgA (mucosal immunity), IgE
(allergic responses), and IgD. Antibodies neutralise pathogens, opsonise for
phagocytosis, activate complement, and mediate antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity.

\medterm{Antibody} A protein produced by the immune system that specifically binds to a foreign or abnormal substance.

\medterm{Antibody Therapy} Treatment using antibodies to block, activate, or target a specific molecule or cell.

\synonyms
Immunotherapy

\medterm{Antibody Titer Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures the concentration of specific antibodies in serum, expressed as the highest dilution that produces a detectable reaction. Rising titers between acute and convalescent specimens indicate recent or active infection; high single titers suggest prior exposure or immunity.

\medterm{Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotox. (ADCC)} Antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity, in which immune effector cells such as natural killer cells recognize antibodies attached to a target cell and destroy it.
\medterm{Anticholinergic} Blocking the effects of acetylcholine, commonly at muscarinic receptors; these drugs can reduce secretions and smooth-muscle activity but may cause dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, or confusion.

\medterm{Anticholinergic Burden} The cumulative effect of taking multiple medications with anticholinergic properties. It
can cause dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, blurred vision, confusion, and
delirium, especially in elderly patients. Many psychiatric medications (TCAs,
first-generation antipsychotics, some antihistamines) have anticholinergic effects.

\medterm{Anticholinergic Toxidrome} A poisoning pattern caused by blockade of muscarinic acetylcholine receptors, commonly producing dry skin and mouth, dilated pupils, rapid heart rate, urinary retention, reduced bowel sounds, overheating, agitation, or delirium.

\medterm{Anticoagulation} Treatment that reduces blood clot formation by inhibiting the coagulation system. It is used
for conditions such as venous thromboembolism and selected cardiac or vascular disorders.

\medterm{Anticodon} A three-nucleotide sequence at one end of transfer RNA that base-pairs with a
complementary messenger RNA codon during translation, ensuring the correct amino acid is
incorporated into the growing polypeptide.

\medterm{Anticonvulsants} Medications used to prevent or control seizures in epilepsy and other seizure disorders.

\medterm{Antideformity Position} A therapeutic positioning method that places a limb or joint in an alignment that helps prevent or limit a contracture or deformity.

\medterm{Antidepressants} Medicines used mainly to treat depressive disorders; different classes alter serotonin, norepinephrine, dopamine, or other signaling and may also treat anxiety, pain, or related conditions.

\medterm{Antidiabetic} A class of medications used to manage diabetes mellitus by lowering blood glucose levels
through various mechanisms.

\medterm{Antidiabetics-Oral} Oral antidiabetic medicines lower blood glucose through different mechanisms, including increasing insulin release, improving insulin sensitivity, reducing hepatic glucose production, or increasing urinary glucose loss. Selection considers kidney function, hypoglycemia risk, weight, cardiovascular disease, and cost.

\synonyms
Medications Type 2 Diabetes (Antidiabetics-Oral)

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone} A hypothalamic hormone released from the posterior pituitary in response to increased
plasma osmolality or reduced effective circulating volume. It increases collecting-duct
water permeability through V2 receptors and promotes vasoconstriction through V1
receptors.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone (ADH)} Vasopressin, a hormone released from the posterior pituitary that increases water reabsorption in the kidney and, at higher concentrations, constricts blood vessels.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures plasma vasopressin concentration, the hormone responsible for water reabsorption in the kidney collecting ducts. It is used to evaluate diabetes insipidus, syndrome of inappropriate ADH secretion, and disorders of water balance.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone Physiology} Antidiuretic hormone also known as vasopressin is synthesized in the supraoptic and
paraventricular nuclei of the hypothalamus and stored in the posterior pituitary.
Released in response to increased serum osmolality detected by hypothalamic
osmoreceptors and decreased blood volume detected by baroreceptors.

\synonyms
vasopressin is synthesized in the supraoptic and.

\medterm{Antidiuretic Hormone Resistance} Impaired renal response to vasopressin, causing excessive production of dilute urine and
polydipsia. Causes include lithium, hypercalcemia, hypokalemia, chronic kidney disease,
and inherited defects of the vasopressin receptor or aquaporin pathway.

\medterm{Antidote} Substance that counteracts poison toxicity. Specific: naloxone (opioids), flumazenil
(benzodiazepines), N-acetylcysteine (acetaminophen), digoxin immune Fab (digoxin),
chelators (EDTA, deferoxamine, dimercaprol), atropine + pralidoxime (organophosphates),
methylene blue (methemoglobinemia), cyanide antidote kit (nitrite + thiosulfate).

\medterm{Antiemetics} Antiemetics are medicines used to prevent or treat nausea and vomiting. Drug classes act on serotonin, dopamine, histamine, acetylcholine, or neurokinin pathways; choice depends on the cause, age, pregnancy, other medicines, heart rhythm risk, and hydration status.

\medterm{Antiepileptic Drugs} Medications used to prevent and control seizures by modulating neuronal excitability
through various mechanisms. They can be classified by their primary mechanism of action.

\medterm{Antifreeze Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to antifreeze, usually products containing ethylene glycol or propylene glycol. Severity depends on the substance, dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Antifungal Prophylaxis} The use of antifungal agents to prevent invasive fungal infections in high-risk
patients. Common indications include prolonged neutropenia, hematopoietic stem cell
transplantation, and advanced HIV infection. Fluconazole, posaconazole, and micafungin
are used for prophylaxis.

\medterm{Antifungal Resistance} The ability of fungi to withstand the effects of antifungal agents. Resistance can be
intrinsic or acquired. Azole resistance occurs through efflux pumps and target
mutations. Echinocandin resistance occurs through FKS gene mutations. Amphotericin B
resistance is rare.

\medterm{Antigen} A molecule or molecular structure specifically recognized by an antibody, B-cell
receptor, or T-cell receptor. An immunogen is an antigen capable of inducing an immune
response; antigenicity and immunogenicity are related but not synonymous properties.

\medterm{Antigen Detection Test} A diagnostic assay that identifies a pathogen-derived antigen in a clinical specimen
using a specific antibody or related binding reagent. It is generally rapid but may be
less sensitive than nucleic-acid amplification, especially when organism burden is low.

\medterm{Antigen Presentation} Display of antigen fragments on cell surfaces for recognition by T cells.

\medterm{Antigen Receptor} A receptor on an immune cell that specifically recognizes an antigen.

\medterm{Antigen-Presenting Cell} Specialized immune cells that process protein antigens and display peptide fragments
bound to Major Histocompatibility Complex (MHC) molecules on their surface for
recognition by T-cell receptors. Professional APCs: dendritic cells (most potent for
naive T cells), macrophages, and B cells.

\synonyms
APC (antigen-presenting cell)

\medterm{Antigenic Drift} The gradual accumulation of point mutations in viral surface antigens (hemagglutinin,
neuraminidase) during replication, allowing influenza viruses to evade pre-existing
antibodies and cause recurrent seasonal epidemics. Drift is continuous and selected by
host immunity; it is why the seasonal vaccine is updated annually.

\medterm{Antigenic Shift} A major, abrupt change in the antigenic structure of a virus (especially influenza A)
resulting from genetic reassortment between different strains (e.g., human and avian
viruses) infecting the same cell.

\medterm{Antigenic Variation} The process by which pathogens change their surface proteins to evade immune
recognition. Antigenic drift: gradual changes in surface proteins. Antigenic shift:
major changes through reassortment of genome segments. Influenza virus demonstrates both
mechanisms. Trypanosomes and Plasmodium also use antigenic variation.

\medterm{Antigout} A class of medications used to treat gout by reducing uric acid levels or controlling
acute inflammation.

\medterm{Antihistamines} Medicines that block histamine receptors; H1 antihistamines relieve allergy symptoms and some cause sedation, while H2 antihistamines reduce gastric acid secretion.

\medterm{Antihistamines For Allergies} Medicines that block histamine effects and reduce symptoms such as itching, sneezing, and runny nose; sedation and interactions vary by agent.

\medterm{Antihypertensives In Pregnancy} Medications used to treat hypertension in pregnancy.

\medterm{Antileukotriene} A drug class that blocks leukotriene-mediated airway inflammation or bronchoconstriction and is used in selected allergic or respiratory conditions.

\synonyms
Leukotriene modifier; leukotriene pathway inhibitor.

\medterm{Antimetabolite} A compound resembling a substance needed for metabolism or DNA synthesis that disrupts cellular processes; many antimetabolites are used as anticancer or immunosuppressive drugs.

\medterm{Antimicrobial} A general term for antibiotics and other drugs that fight microscopic organisms in the
body, such as bacteria, viruses, fungi, and parasites.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Adverse Effects} Side effects associated with antimicrobial agents. Beta-lactams can cause allergic
reactions. Fluoroquinolones can cause tendon rupture and QT prolongation.
Aminoglycosides can cause nephrotoxicity and ototoxicity. Azole antifungals can cause
hepatotoxicity.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Agent} A substance that kills microorganisms or inhibits their growth, including antibacterial, antiviral, antifungal, and antiparasitic agents.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Desensitization} A procedure that administers gradually increasing doses of an antimicrobial to
temporarily induce tolerance in a person with a documented or strongly suspected immediate allergy. It may be considered
when that antimicrobial is clinically necessary and suitable alternatives are not available.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Dosing Optimization} Strategies to ensure antimicrobial agents achieve therapeutic concentrations at the site
of infection. Optimization includes appropriate drug selection, dosing interval, route
of administration, and duration of therapy. Extended or continuous infusion of
beta-lactams may improve outcomes. Weight-based dosing is important for many agents.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Hypersensitivity Reactions} Immune-mediated reactions to antimicrobial agents that may range from a mild rash to
life-threatening anaphylaxis. The reaction depends on the drug, the patient, and the
type of immune response.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Pharmacodynamics} The relationship between antimicrobial drug concentrations and their effect on
microorganisms. Time-dependent antibiotics (beta-lactams) are most effective when
concentrations remain above the MIC. Concentration-dependent antibiotics
(aminoglycosides) are most effective with high peak concentrations.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Pharmacokinetics} The absorption, distribution, metabolism, and elimination of antimicrobial agents.
Pharmacokinetics varies by drug class, patient factors, and disease state. Renal
impairment affects elimination of many antibiotics. Hepatic impairment affects
metabolism of many antifungals.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Resistance} Microorganisms becoming resistant to antibiotics. Major global health threat.
Prevention: appropriate antibiotic use, infection control.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR)} The ability of a microorganism to survive or grow despite an antimicrobial medicine that would normally inhibit orkill it.

\synonyms
Antimicrobial Resistance

\medterm{Antimicrobial Resistance Mechanisms} Biological processes that allow microorganisms to survive antimicrobial exposure, including
drug inactivation, modification of the drug target, reduced permeability, and active
drug efflux.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Resistance Surveillance} The systematic monitoring of antimicrobial resistance patterns in clinical isolates.
Surveillance programmes track resistance rates over time and guide empiric therapy.
National and international networks share resistance data.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Sensitivity Test} A laboratory test that determines which antimicrobial medicines inhibit or kill a microorganism isolated from a patient.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Stewardship} Coordinated actions that promote appropriate antimicrobial selection, dose, route, and duration
to improve patient outcomes and limit resistance, adverse effects, and unnecessary cost.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Susceptibility Testing} Laboratory methods used to estimate how susceptible a microorganism is to antimicrobial
agents. Methods include disk diffusion, broth microdilution, and automated systems. Results
are interpreted using standardized breakpoints and can help guide antimicrobial therapy.

\medterm{Antimicrobial Therapy} The use of antibacterial, antiviral, antifungal, or antiparasitic medicines to prevent or treat infection. Selection depends on the suspected or identified organism, infection site, severity, resistance patterns, and patient factors.

\medterm{Antimitochondrial Antibody} An autoantibody strongly associated with primary biliary cholangitis, interpreted with cholestatic liver tests and clinical findings.

\medterm{Antimony} A metallic chemical element whose compounds can be toxic; selected antimony compounds, such as the pentavalent agents, have been used to treat leishmaniasis.

\medterm{Antinuclear Antibodies} Alternate index label; see \textit{Antinuclear Antibody}.

\medterm{Antinuclear Antibody} ANA: autoantibodies against nuclear components (DNA, histones, ribonucleoproteins,
etc.), detected by immunofluorescence (HEp-2 cells), reported with pattern (homogeneous,
speckled, nucleolar, centromere) and titer.

\synonyms
Antinuclear Antibodies

\medterm{Antinuclear Antibody Panel} A laboratory test that measures multiple antinuclear antibody specificities, including anti-dsDNA, anti-Smith, anti-RNP, anti-Ro, anti-La, and anti-histone antibodies. It is used to evaluate suspected autoimmune connective tissue diseases such as systemic lupus erythematosus when ANA screening is positive.

\medterm{Antinuclear Autoantibody} An autoantibody directed against components of a cell nucleus.

\synonyms
ANA

\medterm{Antioxidant} A substance that limits oxidation by reacting with oxidizing molecules or free radicals.

\medterm{Antiparietal Cell Antibody Test} A laboratory test that detects autoantibodies against gastric parietal cells, which produce intrinsic factor and hydrochloric acid. Positive results are found in pernicious anemia and autoimmune gastritis and may indicate risk of vitamin B12 deficiency.

\medterm{Antiparkinson} A class of medications used to manage Parkinson disease symptoms by restoring
dopaminergic activity or modulating other neurotransmitter systems.

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Antibodies} Include lupus anticoagulant (prolongs phospholipid-dependent coagulation tests),
anticardiolipin antibodies (IgG/IgM), and anti-beta-2-glycoprotein I antibodies.
Detected on >=2 occasions 12 weeks apart. Clinical criteria: vascular thrombosis or
pregnancy morbidity. Can be transient (infection, drugs) or persistent.

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Antibody} An autoantibody directed against phospholipid-binding proteins. Persistent antibodies may be associated with thrombosis or pregnancy complications, particularly in antiphospholipid syndrome.

\synonyms
APA (antiphospholipid antibody)

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Antibody Syndrome (APLS)} An autoimmune disorder in which antiphospholipid antibodies are associated with abnormal blood clotting, recurrent pregnancy loss, thrombocytopenia, and sometimes migraine or heart-valve abnormalities.
\medterm{Antiphospholipid Syndrome} A systemic autoimmune thrombophilia characterized by arterial and venous thrombosis,
pregnancy morbidity, and persistent antiphospholipid antibody positivity.

\synonyms
APS (Antiphospholipid Syndrome)

\medterm{Antiphospholipid Syndrome (APS)} Alternate index label for Antiphospholipid Syndrome.
\medterm{Antiphospholipid Syndrome - APS} Antiphospholipid syndrome is an autoimmune thrombophilia associated with venous or arterial clots and pregnancy morbidity in the presence of persistent antiphospholipid antibodies.

\medterm{Antiplatelet Agents} Medicines that reduce platelet activation or aggregation and thereby lower the risk of some blood clots.

\medterm{Antiplatelet Medicines - P2Y12 Inhibitors} Antiplatelet drugs such as clopidogrel, prasugrel, and ticagrelor that reduce ADP-mediated platelet activation and thrombosis risk, with bleeding as a major adverse effect.

\medterm{Antiplatelet Therapy} Treatment with medicines that reduce platelet activation or aggregation, such as aspirin or clopidogrel, when clinically indicated to reduce arterial clotting.

\medterm{Antiprotozoal} Destroying protozoa or inhibiting their growth; antiprotozoal medicines treat infections such as malaria, giardiasis, or amoebiasis.

\medterm{Antiretroviral} Active against retroviruses; antiretroviral medicines inhibit different stages of HIV replication and are generally used in combination therapy.

\medterm{Antiretroviral Therapy} Treatment of HIV infection with combinations of antiretroviral agents. ART targets
multiple steps in the viral life cycle including entry, reverse transcription,
integration, and maturation. Combination ART suppresses viral replication and restores
immune function. ART is lifelong therapy.

\medterm{Antiretroviral Therapy (ART)} Combination treatment with antiretroviral medicines that suppress HIV replication, preserve immune function, and reduce the risk of HIV transmission and disease progression.
\medterm{Antisepsis} Use of chemical methods to reduce or eliminate harmful microorganisms on living tissue.

\medterm{Antiseptic} Substances used on wounds to prevent or treat infection; they kill or slow the growth of
diseasecausing organisms, such as bacteria, on the surface of the body.

\medterm{Antiserum} Blood serum containing antibodies against a particular antigen or toxin.

\medterm{Antisocial Personality} A persistent pattern of disregard for other people’s rights, social rules, and responsibilities.

\synonyms
Antisocial personality disorder

\medterm{Antisocial Personality Disorder} A pattern of disregard for and violation of the rights of others occurring since age 15
years. At least three of seven criteria: failure to conform to social norms,
deceitfulness, impulsivity, irritability and aggressiveness, reckless disregard for
safety, consistent irresponsibility, and lack of remorse.

\synonyms
Disorder Antisocial Personality (Antisocial Personality Disorder)
Personality Disorder, Antisocial
Personality Disorder Antisocial (Antisocial Personality Disorder)

\medterm{Antispasmodic Drugs} Medicines that relax smooth muscle and reduce cramping or spasms, most often in the digestive tract.
They may also be used for bladder or bile-duct spasms; the choice depends on the cause and the medicine's risks.

\synonyms
Antispasmodics; spasmolytics.

\medterm{Antispasmodics} Alternate index label; see \textit{Antispasmodic}.

\medterm{Antistreptolysin O Titer} A blood test that measures antibodies against streptolysin O, a toxin produced by group A streptococci. Rising or elevated titers support recent streptococcal infection and help evaluate suspected rheumatic fever or poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis, although a single value does not prove active infection.

\medterm{Antisynthetase Syndrome} An autoimmune disorder associated with antibodies against aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, especially anti-Jo-1.
It may cause interstitial lung disease, inflammatory muscle disease, arthritis, fever,
Raynaud phenomenon, or mechanic's hands.

\medterm{Antithrombin} Natural anticoagulant. Inhibits thrombin, factor Xa. Deficiency causes thrombosis.

\medterm{Antithrombin Deficiency} A rare (1 in 2,000-5,000), strongly thrombophilic inherited disorder (autosomal
dominant). Antithrombin (previously antithrombin III) is a serine protease inhibitor
that inactivates thrombin and factors Xa, IXa, XIa, and XIIa. Heterozygous: 10-50 fold
increased VTE risk, more severe than factor V Leiden.

\medterm{Antithrombin III Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures antithrombin activity or antigen level, a natural anticoagulant that inactivates thrombin and factor Xa. Deficiency is a strong risk factor for venous thromboembolism; acquired decreases occur with liver disease, nephrotic syndrome, or heparin therapy.

\medterm{Antithrombin III Deficiency} An inherited or acquired thrombophilia caused by reduced antithrombin activity, leading
to increased risk of venous thromboembolism.

\medterm{Antithyroglobulin Antibody Test} A laboratory test that measures antibodies against thyroglobulin, a thyroid hormone precursor protein. Elevated levels are found in Hashimoto thyroiditis, Graves disease, and other autoimmune thyroid conditions, and help evaluate thyroid dysfunction of autoimmune origin.

\medterm{Antitoxin} An antibody or preparation that neutralizes a toxin.

\medterm{Antitubercular Agents} Antimicrobial drugs used to treat Mycobacterium tuberculosis infections.

\medterm{Antrochoanal Polyp} A benign, single nasal polyp originating from the maxillary sinus mucosa and extending
through the accessory ostium into the choana and nasopharynx. It is most common in
children and young adults, presenting with unilateral nasal obstruction and rhinorrhea.
Treatment is endoscopic surgical removal.

\medterm{Antrum} A cavity or chamber in an organ, especially the lower part of the stomach near the pylorus or the air-filled maxillary sinus.

\medterm{Anuria} Near absence of urine production, often defined in adults as less than about 100 milliliters in 24 hours.

\medterm{Anus} The terminal opening of the gastrointestinal tract through which feces are expelled. It
is surrounded by the internal smooth-muscle sphincter and external skeletal-muscle
sphincter.

\medterm{Anxiety} A normal emotional response to perceived danger, but anxiety disorders involve
excessive, persistent worry that interferes with daily life. Symptoms can include
restlessness, rapid heart rate, muscle tension, and avoidance behaviors.

\medterm{Anxiety Disorder} A group of psychiatric conditions characterized by excessive fear, anxiety, and related
behavioral disturbances. Includes generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), panic disorder,
social anxiety disorder, specific phobias, and agoraphobia.

\medterm{Anxiety Disorder, Generalized} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Generalized anxiety disorder}.

\medterm{Anxiety Disorder, Social} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Social anxiety disorder (social phobia)}.

\medterm{Anxiety Disorders} Mental health disorders involving excessive fear, worry, or anxiety that is persistent, difficult to control, and disruptive to daily life.

\medterm{Anxiety Separation (Separation Anxiety)} Separation anxiety is excessive fear or distress when separated from an attachment figure, beyond what is expected for developmental stage. It can cause avoidance, physical complaints, sleep difficulty, and school impairment and is treated with behavioral therapy and, when needed, medication.

\medterm{Aorta} Largest artery, from the left ventricle distributing oxygenated blood systemically.
Approximately 30 cm long, 2.5 cm diameter. Four segments: ascending aorta, aortic arch
(brachiocephalic trunk, left common carotid, left subclavian), descending thoracic
aorta, descending abdominal aorta (bifurcating into common iliacs at L4).

\medterm{Aortal} Relating to the aorta, the large artery that carries blood from the left ventricle to the systemic circulation.

\medterm{Aortic} Relating to the aorta, the main artery carrying blood from the heart to the body.

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm} A permanent, localized enlargement of the aorta caused by weakening of its wall. It may occur in the thoracic or
abdominal aorta and can enlarge, dissect, or rupture; management depends on its site,
size, growth, symptoms, and the patient's risk.

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm Repair - Endovascular} Endovascular aneurysm repair (EVAR) is a minimally invasive technique using a stent-graft deployed through femoral artery access to exclude an aortic aneurysm from circulation. It is indicated for suitable anatomy and offers faster recovery than open repair.


\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm Rupture} Full-thickness failure of an aortic aneurysm with bleeding into a body cavity or
surrounding tissue. Ruptured aneurysm commonly causes sudden pain, shock, and collapse
and requires immediate resuscitation and operative or endovascular repair.

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm Screening} Evaluation of selected people for an aortic aneurysm before symptoms develop. Screening
may use abdominal ultrasonography or other imaging according to the person's risk.

\medterm{Aortic Aneurysm, Abdominal} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Abdominal aortic aneurysm}.

\medterm{Aortic Angiography} An imaging study of blood vessels that uses contrast material to identify narrowing, blockage, aneurysm, or abnormal blood flow.

\medterm{Aortic Arch} Curves from second right sternocostal joint superiorly and left, then posteriorly and
inferiorly to T4. From right to left: brachiocephalic trunk, left common carotid, left
subclavian. Related to left recurrent laryngeal nerve (hooking under it), trachea,
esophagus, left phrenic nerve. Arch aneurysms may cause hoarseness and SVC syndrome.

\medterm{Aortic Arch Syndrome} Symptoms and signs caused by narrowing or blockage of the aorta or its major branches. Causes include large-vessel vasculitis, atherosclerosis, thrombosis, and congenital disease; findings may include unequal pulses, limb claudication, or neurologic symptoms.

\medterm{Aortic Body} A small group of chemoreceptor cells near the aortic arch that helps sense blood oxygen and carbon dioxide.

\medterm{Aortic Coarctation} Congenital narrowing of the aorta, usually near the insertion of the ductus arteriosus.
It causes higher blood pressure and stronger pulses in the arms than in the legs, with a
radio-femoral delay; severe neonatal disease may present with shock when the ductus
closes.

\medterm{Aortic Disease} Any disorder affecting the aorta, including aneurysm, dissection, narrowing, or inflammation.

\medterm{Aortic Dissection} A tear in the inner layer of the aorta that allows blood to enter the wall and form a false channel. Stanford type A
involves the ascending aorta; type B does not. Treatment depends on the type, symptoms,
complications, and overall clinical condition.

\synonyms
Dissecting Aneurysm
Dissection Aorta (Aortic Dissection)

\medterm{Aortic Embolism} Obstruction of a systemic artery by material originating in the aorta, such as thrombus
or atheromatous debris, causing distal ischemia.

\medterm{Aortic Graft Infection} Infection involving a prosthetic or endovascular aortic graft, potentially causing
sepsis, pseudoaneurysm, hemorrhage, or aortoenteric fistula. Management usually requires
prolonged antimicrobial treatment and individualized surgical or endovascular source
control.

\medterm{Aortic Injury} Damage to the aorta, often caused by severe trauma or a medical procedure.

\medterm{Aortic Insufficiency} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Aortic valve regurgitation}.

\medterm{Aortic Regurgitation} Incomplete closure of the aortic valve allowing blood to flow backward from the aorta to
the left ventricle during diastole. Causes include bicuspid aortic valve, endocarditis,
aortic root dilation, hypertension, and connective tissue disorders. Murmur: early
diastolic decrescendo murmur at the left sternal border.

\medterm{Aortic Regurgitation Murmur} Early diastolic decrescendo murmur heard best at the left sternal border with the
patient sitting up and leaning forward in end-expiration. Wide pulse pressure,
water-hammer pulses (Corrigan pulse), de Musset sign (head bobbing with each pulse), and
Duroziez sign (femoral bruit with compression).

\medterm{Aortic Rupture} A life-threatening tear through the wall of the aorta causing internal bleeding.

\medterm{Aortic Stenosis} Narrowing of the aortic valve opening, obstructing left ventricular outflow. It is the
most common valvular disease in developed countries, usually caused by calcific
degeneration. Symptoms include angina, syncope, and heart failure (SAD triad).

\medterm{Aortic Stenosis Grading} Severity grading: mild (valve area >1.5 cm2, mean gradient <20 mmHg), moderate (valve
area 1.0-1.5 cm2, mean gradient 20-40 mmHg), severe (valve area <1.0 cm2, mean gradient
>40 mmHg). Very severe (valve area <0.6 cm2, mean gradient >60 mmHg) indicates critical
stenosis. Grading guides timing of intervention.

\medterm{Aortic Valve} A semilunar valve between the left ventricle and aorta, normally composed of right, left, and
noncoronary cusps. It opens during systole and closes during diastole to direct blood flow.
Each cusp forms a sinus of Valsalva from which coronary arteries originate. Opens during
systole; closes during diastole.

\medterm{Aortic Valve Disease} Abnormal narrowing, leakage, or structural change of the valve between the left ventricle and aorta. It may cause
chest pain, breathlessness, fatigue, fainting, or no symptoms initially. Evaluation commonly includes examination
and echocardiography; severe disease may require specialist treatment.

\medterm{Aortic Valve Insufficiency} Leakage of the aortic valve that allows blood to flow back into the left ventricle.

\medterm{Aortic Valve Regurgitation} Backward leakage of blood through an incompletely closing aortic valve, which can make the heart work harder and eventually cause heart failure.

\synonyms
Regurgitation, Aortic Valve

\medterm{Aortic Valve Stenosis} Narrowing of the aortic valve that obstructs blood flow from the left ventricle into the aorta.

\synonyms
Stenosis, Aortic Valve

\medterm{Aortic Valve Surgery - Minimally Invasive} Aortic valve replacement performed through a small incision or port access, avoiding median sternotomy. It may be surgical or transcatheter (TAVR) and is used for aortic stenosis or regurgitation when a less invasive approach is appropriate.

\medterm{Aortic Valve Surgery - Open} Open surgical replacement or repair of the aortic valve through median sternotomy, typically using a mechanical or bioprosthetic valve. It is indicated for severe aortic stenosis or regurgitation causing symptoms or left ventricular dysfunction.

\medterm{Aortitis} Inflammation of the aortic wall.

\medterm{Aortography} Contrast imaging of aorta. Evaluates aneurysm, dissection, occlusion. Largely replaced
by CTA/MRA.

\medterm{Aortopulmonary Window} A congenital opening between the ascending aorta and pulmonary artery.

\medterm{AP} An abbreviation commonly meaning anteroposterior, or directed from front to back; in some contexts it may have another medical expansion.

\medterm{AP, X-Ray} An anteroposterior radiographic projection in which the X-ray beam travels from the front of the body toward the back.

\medterm{Alkaline Phosphatase Activity (APA)} A measurement of alkaline phosphatase enzyme activity in the blood, used to help evaluate
liver, bile-duct, or bone disorders. Results are interpreted with other liver tests and clinical findings.
\medterm{APACHE II Score} Severity scoring system for critically ill patients incorporating physiological
variables, age, and chronic health status to generate scores from zero to seventy-one.
Higher scores indicate greater severity and higher mortality. In acute pancreatitis,
scores of eight or greater indicate severe disease.

\medterm{Apathy} Reduced motivation, emotional responsiveness, or interest in activities.

\medterm{Argon Plasma Coagulation (APC)} A procedure that uses ionized argon gas and electrical energy to coagulate tissue and control bleeding
or treat some superficial abnormal tissue, often during endoscopy.

\synonyms
APC (argon plasma coagulation)

\medterm{APC Tumor Suppressor} The adenomatous polyposis coli protein is a large multidomain tumor suppressor that
functions as a critical negative regulator of the canonical Wnt signaling pathway.

\medterm{APD} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Auditory processing disorder}.

\medterm{Apert Syndrome} An autosomal dominant craniosynostosis syndrome caused by FGFR2 mutations (most commonly
Ser252Trp or Pro253Arg).

\medterm{Apex} The tip or pointed end of a structure; examples include the apex of the heart, formed mainly by the left ventricle, and the upper tip of a lung.

\medterm{Apgar Score} A structured assessment of a newborn's appearance, pulse, grimace, activity, and respiration, usually recorded at 1 and 5 minutes after birth.
It describes immediate adaptation after delivery and helps guide resuscitation; it is not a standalone diagnosis or long-term prognostic test.

\synonyms
Apgar assessment; newborn Apgar score.

\medterm{Aphagia} Complete inability to swallow, which may result from neurologic disease, obstruction, severe pain, or loss of swallowing-muscle function.

\medterm{Aphasia} Impairment of language comprehension or production resulting from brain damage, most
commonly from stroke affecting the left hemisphere (dominant) language areas.

\medterm{Aphasia, Primary Progressive} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Primary progressive aphasia}.

\medterm{Apheresis} A procedure that removes a selected blood component, such as plasma, platelets, or
abnormal cells, and returns the remaining components to the patient or donor.

\medterm{Aphonia} Complete loss of the ability to phonate, distinct from dysphonia in which voice is
present but abnormal. Causes are organic (bilateral vocal cord paralysis, severe
laryngitis, laryngeal trauma, structural lesions) or psychogenic (conversion disorder,
muscle tension dysphonia). Whispered speech and cough are usually preserved.

\medterm{Aphrasia} Loss or impairment of the ability to speak, often used as an older or less precise term related to aphasia.

\medterm{Aphthous Stomatitis} Recurrent painful ulcers of the oral mucosa with a shallow base and erythematous border, commonly
known as canker sores.

\medterm{Aphthous Ulcer} A recurrent, painful ulcer of the oral mucosa. See Canker Sore.

\medterm{Apical} Relating to or located at the apex, or tip, of a structure such as the lung, heart, or tooth.

\medterm{Apical Ballooning Syndrome} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Broken heart syndrome}.

\medterm{Apical Pulse} The heart rate measured by listening over the apex of the heart, usually at the left fifth intercostal space near the midclavicular line.

\medterm{Apicoectomy} Surgical removal of root tip and periapical lesion. Indication: failed root canal
therapy.

\medterm{Aplasia} Complete failure of development of an organ, tissue, or cell line, resulting in absence or severe deficiency of the expected structure.
It may arise from congenital developmental abnormalities, inherited disorders, injury to a precursor cell population, or severe marrow suppression.
Examples include aplastic anemia, renal aplasia, and aplasia of selected hematopoietic or lymphoid cell lines.
Clinical effects depend on the affected structure and may range from an incidental anatomic finding to life-threatening organ failure.

\medterm{Aplastic} Describing failure of a tissue, especially bone marrow, to produce adequate cells; aplastic anemia causes pancytopenia from marrow failure.

\medterm{Aplastic Anemia} A bone marrow failure syndrome characterized by pancytopenia (anemia, leukopenia,
thrombocytopenia) and a hypocellular bone marrow with replacement by fat cells,
resulting from immune-mediated destruction of hematopoietic stem cells.

\medterm{Aplastic Crisis} An abrupt temporary cessation of red-cell production causing a rapid fall in hemoglobin and reticulocyte count.
It most often follows Parvovirus B19 infection in people with shortened red-cell survival, such as those with sickle-cell disease or hereditary spherocytosis.
Symptoms may include pallor, fatigue, tachycardia, and worsening anemia without the usual increase in reticulocytes.
Diagnosis relies on blood counts, reticulocyte measurement, and targeted viral testing; treatment is supportive and directed at the underlying disorder.

\medterm{Apnea} Temporary cessation of breathing. Clinically important apnea may cause low oxygen, carbon-dioxide retention, or
bradycardia.

\medterm{Apnea Of Prematurity} Recurrent pauses in breathing in a premature infant, sometimes accompanied by bradycardia
or oxygen desaturation. It may be central, obstructive, or mixed. Management includes monitoring and, when needed,
respiratory stimulation or support.

\medterm{Apnea Sleep (Sleep Apnea)} Sleep apnea consists of repeated pauses or reductions in breathing during sleep. Obstructive apnea results from upper-airway collapse, while central apnea reflects reduced respiratory drive; untreated disease can cause daytime sleepiness, impaired concentration, hypertension, and cardiovascular risk.

\medterm{Apnea-Hypopnea Index} Number of apneas plus hypopneas per hour of sleep. Apnea = airflow cessation > 10
seconds. Hypopnea = partial obstruction with desaturation or arousal. AHI < 5 normal,
5--14 mild, 15--29 moderate, >= 30 severe.

\medterm{Apneic} Relating to or characterized by apnea, meaning a temporary cessation of breathing.

\medterm{Apneustic Breathing} A breathing pattern characterized by a prolonged inspiratory pause, seen in brainstem
lesions (particularly the pons). It indicates damage to the apneustic center and is a
poor prognostic sign in neurological injury. It may require mechanical ventilation.

\medterm{Apnoea} Temporary cessation of breathing, defined as complete cessation of airflow for at least 10
seconds. Obstructive apnea: airway collapse despite respiratory effort (common in
obstructive sleep apnea).

\medterm{Apoenzyme} The protein component of an enzyme without its required cofactor or coenzyme. Inactive
or minimally active until the cofactor binds. The complete, catalytically active complex
of apoenzyme + cofactor is called the holoenzyme.

\medterm{Apolipoprotein B100} The structural apolipoprotein on atherogenic particles such as LDL and VLDL; its concentration reflects the number of potentially atherogenic lipoprotein particles.

\medterm{Apolipoprotein CII} A cofactor required to activate lipoprotein lipase, the enzyme that hydrolyzes triglycerides in circulating lipoproteins.

\medterm{Apolipoproteins} Proteins associated with lipids in the blood that help transport and metabolize cholesterol and triglycerides.

\medterm{Aponeurosis} A broad, flat sheet of connective tissue that attaches muscle to bone or other tissues.

\medterm{Apophenia} The perception of meaningful patterns or connections in unrelated or random events; marked apophenia may occur in some psychiatric or neurologic conditions.

\medterm{Apoplexy} An older term for sudden loss of neurologic function, historically referring to stroke and sometimes used for hemorrhage or infarction in an organ.

\medterm{Apoptosis} Programmed cell death. Dysregulated in cancer (avoiding death). Pro-apoptotic genes:
BAX, P53. Anti-apoptotic: BCL-2.

\medterm{Apoptosis Extrinsic Pathway} The extrinsic pathway of apoptosis is triggered by extracellular ligands binding to
death receptors belonging to the tumor necrosis factor receptor superfamily, including
Fas (CD95), TNFR1, and TRAIL receptors DR4 and DR5.

\medterm{Apoptosis Intrinsic Pathway} The intrinsic, or mitochondrial, pathway of apoptosis is initiated by intracellular
stress signals including DNA damage, growth factor withdrawal, endoplasmic reticulum
stress, and hypoxia.

\medterm{Apoptotic Body} A membrane-bound fragment of a cell undergoing programmed cell death.

\medterm{Apparent Mineralocorticoid Excess} Rare autosomal recessive disorder caused by deficiency of 11-beta-hydroxysteroid
dehydrogenase type 2 normally converting cortisol to cortisone in the collecting duct.
Cortisol activates mineralocorticoid receptors causing severe hypertension, hypokalemia,
metabolic alkalosis, and low renin with low aldosterone.

\medterm{Appendageal} Relating to skin appendages or structures derived from the skin, including hair follicles, sweat glands, and sebaceous glands.

\medterm{Appendectomy} Surgical removal of the appendix, the most common emergency surgical procedure performed
worldwide. Appendectomy is indicated for acute appendicitis, the most common surgical
emergency with a lifetime risk of 7-8 percent and peak incidence between 10 and 30
years.

\medterm{Appendiceal} Relating to the vermiform appendix, a narrow pouch projecting from the beginning of the large intestine.

\medterm{Appendiceal Adenocarcinoma} A malignant gland-forming epithelial tumor of the appendix. It may present with appendicitis or be discovered incidentally, and its management depends on histologic type, grade, invasion, and peritoneal spread.

\medterm{Appendiceal Cancer} A malignant tumor arising in the appendix; types include neuroendocrine tumors and epithelial neoplasms, which can rupture or spread mucus-producing tumor cells within the abdomen.

\medterm{Appendiceal Rupture} Tearing or perforation of an inflamed appendix, allowing intestinal contents to enter the abdomen and potentially causing peritonitis or an abscess.

\medterm{Appendiceal Tumors} Neoplasms arising in the appendix, including neuroendocrine tumors, mucinous neoplasms, and adenocarcinoma.
Some are found incidentally, whereas others cause pain, obstruction, or abdominal
distention. Evaluation and treatment depend on the tumor type, size, spread, and
pathologic features.

\medterm{Appendicitis} Inflammation of the vermiform appendix, often caused by obstruction of its lumen. It may cause abdominal pain that moves to
the right lower quadrant, nausea, loss of appetite, and fever; diagnosis and treatment
depend on clinical assessment, laboratory tests, imaging, and complications.

\synonyms
Pain Lower Right Abdomen (Appendicitis)

\medterm{Appendix} A blind-ended tubular diverticulum of the cecum, typically 6-10 cm long, rich in
lymphoid tissue. Its position is variable (retrocecal, pelvic, or retroileal); the base
usually projects near McBurney's point, one-third of the distance from the anterior
superior iliac spine to the umbilicus.

\medterm{Appendix Cancer} Cancer that begins in the appendix, including neuroendocrine tumors, mucinous tumors, and adenocarcinoma.

\medterm{Appetite} The desire or drive to eat.

\medterm{Appetite - Decreased} Anorexia, or reduced desire to eat, may result from depression, infection, malignancy, chronic disease, medications, or psychological stress. Persistent unexplained appetite loss can cause unintentional weight loss and malnutrition. Evaluation considers duration, associated symptoms, and underlying etiology.

\medterm{Appetite - Increased} Hyperphagia is abnormally increased hunger or food intake. It may result from endocrine disorders such as hyperthyroidism or diabetes mellitus, hypothalamic lesions, medications, psychological conditions, or recovery from illness. Persistent unexplained increase in appetite warrants clinical evaluation.

\medterm{Appetite Regulation} Complex control by hormones (leptin, ghrelin, insulin, CCK), neural signals,
psychological factors. Leptin signals satiety, ghrelin signals hunger.

\medterm{Applied Behavior Analysis} A behavioral intervention based on learning principles that uses observation, measurement, and reinforcement
to increase useful behaviors and reduce behaviors that interfere with functioning. It
may be used in autism care and other settings, with goals individualized to the person.

\medterm{Apposition} Placement of two structures next to or against each other; in wound care, good apposition means that tissue edges are accurately aligned.

\medterm{Appropriate For Gestational Age (AGA)} Describing a fetus or newborn whose estimated weight or size falls within the expected reference range for the gestational age, commonly between the 10th and 90th percentiles.

\medterm{Apraxia} Inability to perform learned, skilled motor movements despite intact motor function,
sensation, coordination, and comprehension.

\medterm{APS} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Antiphospholipid syndrome}.

\medterm{Aquagenic Keratoderma} Transient, painful whitish thickening and wrinkling of the palms and soles occurring
within minutes of water immersion, accompanied by a prickling or burning sensation. It
is often associated with cystic fibrosis or other sweat gland disorders but can also be
idiopathic or drug-induced. Symptoms resolve shortly after drying the skin.

\medterm{Aquaporin} A membrane water-channel protein that permits rapid movement of water across cells; aquaporin-2 in kidney collecting ducts is regulated by vasopressin.

\medterm{Aquatic Therapy} Exercise or rehabilitation performed in water, using buoyancy and resistance to support movement and
conditioning.

\medterm{Aqueous Humor} A clear, watery fluid that fills the anterior and posterior chambers of the eye,
maintaining intraocular pressure and providing nutrients and oxygen to the avascular
cornea and lens while removing metabolic waste products.

\medterm{Arachnodactyly} Abnormally long and slender fingers or toes. It may occur as an isolated trait or as a feature of connective-tissue disorders such as Marfan syndrome.

\medterm{Arachnoid Cyst} A congenital, benign, cerebrospinal fluid-filled cyst within the arachnoid membrane,
most commonly found in the middle cranial fossa. Most are asymptomatic and discovered
incidentally on imaging, but large cysts may cause headache, seizures, or focal
neurological deficits.

\medterm{Arachnoid Mater} The middle of the three meninges, a delicate avascular membrane lying between the dura
mater and pia mater. The subarachnoid space between arachnoid and pia contains
cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) and major vessels. Arachnoid granulations (villi) project into
the dural venous sinuses and mediate CSF resorption.

\medterm{Arachnoiditis} Inflammation and scarring of the arachnoid membrane and nearby spinal nerves, often causing chronic pain or neurological symptoms.

\medterm{Arbovirus} A virus transmitted by mosquitoes or other member of the arthropod phylum.

\medterm{ARBs} Block angiotensin II AT1 receptors providing renoprotection similar to ACE inhibitors.
Alternative for ACE inhibitor cough. Do not combine ACE inhibitor and ARB due to
increased adverse effects without benefit. Similar renoprotective effects to ACE
inhibitors.
