\medterm{Arciform} Having a curved or arch-like shape, used to describe the configuration of a skin lesion, rash, or other anatomical finding.

\medterm{Arcus Senilis (Corneal Arcus)} A white, gray, or bluish opaque ring in the peripheral corneal stroma separated from the
limbus by a clear zone (lucid interval of Vogt). Caused by lipid (cholesterol)
deposition in the peripheral cornea. Common and benign in elderly individuals (>60
years).

\synonyms
Corneal Arcus

\medterm{Acute Respiratory Disease (ARD)} A general term for a respiratory illness that begins suddenly or over a short period. It is not a
specific diagnosis and may include infections or other conditions affecting breathing; it is different from acute
respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS).

\synonyms
ARD


\medterm{Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)} A serious lung injury in which fluid builds up in the tiny air sacs, making it hard for oxygen to enter
the blood. It can develop after sepsis, pneumonia, major injury, or inhaling vomit, and is not fully explained by
heart failure or fluid overload. It often requires urgent hospital treatment.

\medterm{Area Under The Concentration-Time Curve} The integral of a drug’s plasma concentration over time. AUC estimates total systemic
exposure and is used to compare bioavailability, guide dosing for selected medicines,
and characterize pharmacokinetic interactions.

\medterm{Arenaviruses} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Viral hemorrhagic fevers}.

\medterm{Areola} The pigmented circular area surrounding the nipple. It contains glands that help lubricate and protect the skin during breastfeeding.

\medterm{Argentine Hemorrhagic Fever} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Viral hemorrhagic fevers}.

\medterm{Argininosuccinic Aciduria} Autosomal recessive urea cycle disorder caused by deficiency of argininosuccinate lyase
(ASL gene, 7cen-q11.2). Biochemical hallmark: accumulation of argininosuccinic acid in
blood, urine, and CSF with hyperammonemia, low citrulline, and elevated argininosuccinic
acid.

\medterm{Argyll Robertson Pupil} A small, irregular pupil that constricts during near focusing but responds poorly or not at all to light. It is
classically associated with neurosyphilis but can have other causes; the finding requires
clinical evaluation.

\medterm{Arias-Stella Reaction} A hormonally induced atypical change in endometrial or endocervical glands during pregnancy or exposure to progestins; it is benign and can mimic glandular neoplasia histologically.

\medterm{Arm} The upper limb between the shoulder and elbow; in everyday use, the term may also include the entire upper extremity.

\medterm{Arm CT Scan} A computed tomography study that produces cross-sectional images of the arm using X-rays. It can identify structural abnormalities, injury, inflammation, bleeding, or masses.

\medterm{Arm Fracture} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Broken arm}.

\medterm{Arm MRI Scan} A magnetic resonance imaging study that produces detailed images of the arm using magnetic fields and radiofrequency energy.

\medterm{Arm Pain} Pain in the shoulder, upper arm, elbow, forearm, or related tissues. Causes include muscle or tendon injury, nerve compression, arthritis, vascular disease, and referred pain; sudden severe pain with chest discomfort, weakness, or shortness of breath requires urgent assessment.

\synonyms
Pain in the arm; upper-limb pain.

\medterm{Armpit Lump} A lump or swelling in the axilla, commonly caused by an enlarged lymph node, skin
infection, cyst, or benign growth. Persistent, hard, fixed, enlarging, or unexplained
lumps require medical assessment.

\medterm{Arnold-Chiari Malformation} Herniation of cerebellar tonsils through the foramen magnum into the upper cervical
spinal canal. Type II (myelomeningocele-associated) is the most common form in
pediatrics. Associated with hydrocephalus, syringomyelia, and spinal cord dysfunction.
Symptoms include headache, neck pain, and lower cranial nerve palsies.

\medterm{Aromatase} An enzyme that converts androgens into estrogens in tissues such as the ovaries, adipose tissue, and breast; it is inhibited by drugs such as anastrozole.

\medterm{Aromatase Excess Syndrome} Rare autosomal dominant condition caused by mutations in the CYP19A1 gene leading to
overexpression of aromatase enzyme and excessive estrogen production. Presents in males
with gynecomastia, advanced bone age, and accelerated linear growth with early
epiphyseal closure. In females with precocious puberty, macroclitoris, and virilization.

\medterm{Aromatherapy} Use of essential oils or other plant-derived scents, usually by inhalation or topical application, for perceived relaxation or symptom relief; evidence and safety vary by oil and use.

\medterm{Arousal Index} The number of brief awakenings or arousals from sleep per hour of sleep, usually measured during polysomnography; an elevated value may indicate sleep fragmentation.

\medterm{ARPKD} Alternate index label; see \textit{Autosomal Recessive Polycystic Kidney Disease}.

\medterm{Arrector Pili} A small involuntary smooth muscle attached to a hair follicle that contracts to raise the hair and produce gooseflesh.

\medterm{Arrector Pili Muscle} A small band of involuntary smooth muscle attached to a hair follicle and the
superficial dermis. Sympathetic stimulation causes it to contract, pulling the hair
upright and producing temporary piloerection, commonly called goosebumps. Contraction
also compresses nearby sebaceous glands and may help release sebum onto the skin.

\synonyms
goosebumps.

\medterm{Arrest Cardiac (Sudden Cardiac Death)} Alternate index label for Sudden Cardiac Death.

\medterm{Arrested Caries} A carious lesion that is no longer progressing, often because the surface has become
hard, smooth, and cleansable. Arrested lesions may be monitored and remineralized;
treatment depends on activity, cavitation, cleansability, and patient risk.

\medterm{Arrhythmia} Any abnormality of cardiac rate, rhythm, or site of impulse origin, arising through
enhanced automaticity, reentry, or triggered activity. Classified by rate as
tachyarrhythmia or bradyarrhythmia and by site as supraventricular or ventricular.
Diagnosis rests on the 12-lead ECG and ambulatory monitoring when intermittent.

\medterm{Arrhythmias} Abnormal heart rhythms caused by altered impulse formation or conduction; they range from harmless premature beats to rhythms that impair circulation.

\medterm{Arrhythmogenic Right Ventricular Cardiomyopathy} A genetic cardiomyopathy characterized by fibrofatty replacement of the right
ventricular myocardium, predisposing to ventricular arrhythmias and sudden cardiac
death, particularly during exercise. ECG shows T-wave inversions in V1-V3 and epsilon
waves; diagnosis uses echocardiography, cardiac MRI, and genetic testing.

\medterm{Arsenic} A naturally occurring metalloid that is toxic in many forms; exposure can cause gastrointestinal, neurologic, cardiovascular, and skin effects, while arsenic trioxide has specific therapeutic uses.

\medterm{Arsenic Poisoning} Arsenic poisoning results from acute or chronic exposure to inorganic or organic arsenic. It can cause gastrointestinal illness, cardiovascular instability, neuropathy, characteristic skin changes, anemia, and increased cancer risk; suspected exposure requires poison-control or emergency guidance.

\synonyms
Poisoning Arsenic (Arsenic Poisoning)

\medterm{Arsenic Toxicity} Systemic poisoning from arsenic exposure causing gastrointestinal distress, cardiac
arrhythmias, peripheral neuropathy, and characteristic Mees lines on nails, requiring
chelation therapy with dimercaprol or succimer.

\medterm{Art Therapy} Use of art-making for therapeutic purposes. Emotional expression, coping,
self-awareness.

\medterm{Artemisinin} A plant-derived antimalarial compound from Artemisia annua; artemisinin derivatives are used in combination regimens, especially for falciparum malaria.

\medterm{Arteria Femoralis} The femoral artery, the major artery of the thigh and continuation of the external iliac artery below the inguinal ligament.

\medterm{Arterial Blood Gas} Measurement of arterial pH, carbon-dioxide tension, oxygen tension, bicarbonate,
and related acid-base variables. It helps assess ventilation, oxygenation, and metabolic or respiratory acid-base
disorders.

\medterm{Arterial Blood Gas (ABG)} A test of arterial blood that measures pH, oxygen, carbon dioxide, and usually bicarbonate or related values to assess oxygenation, ventilation, and acid--base balance.

\medterm{Arterial Blood Gas Analyzer} An analyzer that measures arterial pH, partial pressures of oxygen and carbon dioxide,
and usually bicarbonate or calculated acid-base variables. It is used to assess
ventilation, oxygenation, and metabolic or respiratory acid-base disorders.

\medterm{Arterial Catheter} A catheter placed in an artery, commonly the radial artery, for continuous
blood-pressure monitoring and repeated arterial blood sampling. Complications include
hemorrhage, thrombosis, infection, and distal ischemia.

\medterm{Arterial Catheterization} Placement of a catheter in an artery, usually the radial artery, for continuous
blood-pressure measurement and repeated arterial blood sampling. It is used when rapid
hemodynamic or gas-exchange assessment is clinically necessary and carries risks
including bleeding, infection, thrombosis, and ischemia.

\medterm{Arterial Duplex} Ultrasound technique combining B-mode imaging and Doppler flow assessment. Used for
evaluating arterial stenosis, aneurysm, graft patency. Non-invasive, widely available.

\medterm{Arterial Embolism} Sudden blockage of an artery by material carried through the bloodstream, usually a blood clot. It can cause acute limb, brain, kidney, intestinal, or other organ ischemia and requires urgent assessment.

\synonyms
Arterial Thromboembolism

\medterm{Arterial Embolization} Intentional blockage of an artery, often to control bleeding or reduce blood supply to a tumor.

\medterm{Arterial Injury} Damage to an artery that may cause bleeding, narrowing, clotting, or impaired blood flow.

\medterm{Arterial Insufficiency} Reduced arterial blood flow to a tissue or limb, causing ischemic symptoms such as
claudication, rest pain, cool skin, delayed capillary refill, and diminished pulses.
Most commonly caused by atherosclerosis; assessed by ankle-brachial index and duplex
ultrasound.

\medterm{Arterial Line} A catheter inserted into a peripheral artery (most commonly radial, also femoral,
brachial, dorsalis pedis) for continuous blood pressure monitoring and arterial blood
gas sampling. Allen test: assesses ulnar collateral flow before radial artery
cannulation.

\medterm{Arterial Resistance} The opposition to blood flow produced mainly by the diameter and tone of small arteries and arterioles. It contributes to systemic vascular resistance and blood pressure.

\synonyms
Vascular resistance; systemic vascular resistance.

\medterm{Arterial Stenosis} Abnormal narrowing of an artery.

\medterm{Arterial Stick} Needle puncture of an artery, usually to obtain blood for arterial blood-gas or related testing; bleeding and arterial injury are possible complications.

\medterm{Arterial Tension} The pressure or partial pressure measured in arterial blood; depending on context, it may refer to blood pressure or to the tension of an arterial gas such as oxygen or carbon dioxide.

\medterm{Arterial Thromboembolism} Alternate index label; see \textit{Arterial embolism}.

\medterm{Arterial Thrombosis} Formation of a blood clot within an artery.

\medterm{Arterial Ulcer} A chronic wound caused by arterial insufficiency (peripheral artery disease). Located on
distal extremities: toes, heels, lateral malleolus, and sites of pressure. Presents with
punched-out, deep, well-demarcated wound with pale or necrotic base, minimal exudate,
and cool, hairless, shiny skin.

\medterm{Arteries} Blood vessels that carry blood away from the heart. Most arteries carry oxygenated blood, except the pulmonary and umbilical arteries; their walls contain elastic tissue and smooth muscle to withstand and regulate pressure.

\synonyms
arterial vessels

\medterm{Arteriogram} An imaging study of blood vessels that uses contrast material to identify narrowing, blockage, aneurysm, or abnormal blood flow.

\medterm{Arteriole} A small artery that delivers blood to a capillary bed. Arteriolar smooth muscle
regulates vascular resistance and controls tissue perfusion and systemic blood pressure.

\medterm{Arterioles} Small branches of arteries that regulate blood flow into capillary beds through contraction and relaxation of their smooth muscle.

\synonyms
Resistance vessels; small arteries.

\medterm{Arteriosclerosis} Chronic inflammatory disease of medium and large arteries characterized by accumulation
of lipids, inflammatory cells, and fibrous tissue within the arterial wall, forming
atherosclerotic plaques. See Atherosclerosis.

\medterm{Arteriosclerosis / Atherosclerosis} Arteriosclerosis is hardening and thickening of arteries; atherosclerosis is plaque buildup within artery walls that can restrict blood flow.

\medterm{Arteriosclerotic Retinopathy} Retinal vascular changes associated with thickening or hardening of small arteries, often occurring with chronic hypertension or atherosclerotic disease.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Fistula} A surgically created connection between an artery and a vein, usually for hemodialysis.
The increased blood flow enlarges and strengthens the vein for repeated access. The site and time needed for maturation
depend on the patient's vessels and clinical circumstances.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Graft} Synthetic conduit connecting artery to vein for dialysis. Used when veins inadequate for
fistula. Thrombosis and infection more common than AVF. Can be used earlier than
fistula. Declotting with thrombectomy or angioplasty.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Malformation} Abnormal connection between artery and vein without capillary bed. Can bleed or cause
symptoms.

\medterm{Arteriovenous Malformation (AVM)} An abnormal tangle of arteries and veins connected without the normal capillary bed; an AVM can bleed, cause seizures or neurologic symptoms, or produce high-flow complications.

\medterm{Arteritis} Inflammation of an artery.

\medterm{Arteritis, Giant Cell} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Giant cell arteritis}.

\medterm{Arteritis, Takayasu's} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Takayasu's arteritis}.

\medterm{Artery} A blood vessel that carries oxygenated blood away from the heart to the tissues, except
for the pulmonary arteries which carry deoxygenated blood to the lungs. Arteries have
thick, elastic, muscular walls that withstand high pressure and distribute blood
throughout the body. The aorta is the largest artery.

\medterm{Artery Occlusion} Complete or near-complete blockage of an artery.

\medterm{Arthralgia} Pain in one or more joints without objective evidence of joint inflammation such as swelling, warmth, or structural damage. Common causes include viral infections, overuse, autoimmune disease, and early degenerative change; persistent, severe, or multifocal pain warrants clinical evaluation.

\medterm{Arthritis} Inflammation of one or more joints, characterized by joint pain, swelling, warmth, and
stiffness.

\synonyms
Joint Inflammation (Arthritis)


\medterm{Arthritis Ankylosing Spondylitis (Ankylosing Spondylitis)} Alternate index label for Ankylosing Spondylitis.

\medterm{Arthritis Degenerative (Osteoarthritis)} Alternate index label for Osteoarthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Gout (Gout (Gouty Arthritis))} Alternate index label for Gout (Gouty Arthritis).

\medterm{Arthritis Infectious (Septic Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Septic Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Juvenile (Juvenile Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Juvenile Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Lyme (Lyme Disease)} Alternate index label for Lyme Disease.

\medterm{Arthritis MCTD (Mixed Connective Tissue Disease)} Alternate index label for Mixed Connective Tissue Disease.

\medterm{Arthritis Plant Thorn (Plant Thorn Synovitis)} Alternate index label for Plant Thorn Synovitis.

\medterm{Arthritis Pseudogout (Pseudogout)} Alternate index label for Pseudogout.

\medterm{Arthritis Psoriatic (Psoriatic Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Psoriatic Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Quackery (Quackery Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Quackery Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Reactive (Reactive Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Reactive Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Reiters (Reactive Arthritis)} Alternate index label for Reactive Arthritis.

\medterm{Arthritis Sarcoid (Sarcoidosis)} Alternate index label for Sarcoidosis.

\medterm{Arthritis Scleroderma (Scleroderma)} Alternate index label for Scleroderma.

\medterm{Arthritis Sjogrens Syndrome (Sjogren's Syndrome)} Alternate index label for Sjogren's Syndrome.

\medterm{Arthritis SLE (Systemic Lupus)} Alternate index label for Systemic Lupus.

\medterm{Arthritis Still (Still's Disease)} Alternate index label for Still's Disease.

\medterm{Arthritis, Basal Joint} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Thumb arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Degenerative} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Osteoarthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Gouty} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Gout}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Infectious} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Septic arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Juvenile Idiopathic} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Juvenile idiopathic arthritis (JIA)}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Osteoarthritis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Osteoarthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Psoriatic} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Psoriatic arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Reactive} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Reactive arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Rheumatoid} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Rheumatoid arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Septic} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Septic arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthritis, Thumb} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Thumb arthritis}.

\medterm{Arthrocentesis} Aspiration of joint fluid for diagnostic or therapeutic purposes. Indications: septic
arthritis (urgent, crystals), inflammatory arthritis (gout, pseudogout), hemorrhagic
effusion (trauma, coagulopathy), and therapeutic drainage for symptomatic relief.

\medterm{Arthrodesis} Surgical fusion of a joint by removing or preparing articular surfaces and stabilizing
the bones until osseous union occurs. It relieves pain and provides stability at the
cost of joint motion.

\medterm{Arthrogram} An image of a joint obtained after contrast material is introduced into or around the joint, used to evaluate structures such as cartilage, ligaments, or the joint capsule.

\medterm{Arthrography} Contrast injected into joint for imaging. Used for shoulder, knee, hip evaluation. MR
arthrography more common now.

\medterm{Arthrogryposis} A group of congenital disorders characterized by multiple joint contractures present at
birth, caused by decreased fetal movement (fetal akinesia) during development.

\medterm{Arthrogryposis Multiplex Congenita (AMC)} A group of congenital conditions characterized by multiple joint contractures present at birth, often with muscle weakness or abnormal development of nerves, muscles, or connective tissue.
\medterm{Arthropathy} Any disease or disorder affecting a joint.

\synonyms
Joint disease

\medterm{Arthroplasty} Surgical reconstruction or replacement of a joint to relieve pain, restore alignment, or
improve function. It may be partial or total and is commonly performed for advanced
degenerative joint disease, fracture, inflammatory arthritis, or other destructive joint
conditions.

\medterm{Arthropod Vector} An arthropod, such as a mosquito or tick, that transmits an infectious agent between hosts.

\synonyms
Disease vector

\medterm{Arthroscope} A small illuminated instrument used to view the inside of a joint.

\medterm{Arthroscopy} Minimally invasive joint visualization and intervention via a rigid arthroscope inserted
through small portals, used for meniscal and ligamentous repair, chondroplasty, loose
body removal, and synovectomy.

\medterm{Arthrosis} Degenerative disease of a joint, characterized by progressive cartilage loss and changes in the underlying bone; osteoarthritis is the most common example.

\medterm{Arthus Reaction} A localized immune-complex reaction causing inflammation at the site of antigen exposure.

\synonyms
AI

\medterm{Articular Cartilage} Smooth cartilage covering the ends of bones within a joint, allowing low-friction movement and distributing load.

\medterm{Articular Cartilage Injuries} Chondral/osteochondral lesions: Outerbridge grading (I: softening, II: fibrillation
<50\%, III: fibrillation >50\%, IV: subchondral bone exposed). OCD (osteochondritis
dissecans): subchondral bone necrosis, fragment separation (knee: lateral femoral
condyle, elbow: capitellum, ankle: talar dome).

\medterm{Articular Ligament} A band of connective tissue that stabilizes a joint by connecting bone to bone.

\synonyms
Joint ligament

\medterm{Articulation} A joint or connection between bones; the term also refers to the production and clarity of speech sounds by the vocal organs.

\medterm{Artifacts} Image distortions not representing true anatomy. Types: motion, beam hardening, metal,
aliasing, partial volume. Recognition important for accurate interpretation.

\medterm{Artificial Abortion} An induced termination of pregnancy using medication, a procedure, or both, rather than a spontaneous miscarriage.

\medterm{Artificial Heart} A mechanical device that replaces or supports the pumping function of the heart.

\medterm{Artificial Insemination} Placement of sperm into the reproductive tract by a medical procedure to assist conception.

\synonyms
Intrauterine insemination

\medterm{Artificial Insemination By Donor (AID)} Intrauterine insemination using sperm from a donor other than the intended mother’s partner to attempt fertilization and pregnancy.
\medterm{Artificial Insemination By Husband (AIH)} Intrauterine insemination using sperm from the woman’s male partner; it is also called homologous insemination.
\medterm{Artificial Insemination By Husband (AIH)} Placement of sperm from a woman's husband or male partner into her reproductive tract to achieve pregnancy, usually by intrauterine insemination.

\medterm{Artificial Pacemaker} An implanted device that delivers electrical impulses to maintain an appropriate heart rate when
the heart's natural conduction system is inadequate.

\medterm{Artificial Rupture Of Membranes} The intentional rupture of the amniotic sac with a small hook-like instrument (Amnihook)
during a cervical exam to induce or augment labor. Indications: induction or
augmentation of labor, placement of internal monitors (fetal scalp electrode,
intrauterine pressure catheter), and assessment of amniotic fluid (meconium staining).

\medterm{Artificial Saliva} A product used to moisten the mouth and relieve symptoms of dry mouth.

\synonyms
Saliva substitute

\medterm{Artificial Skin} A biological or synthetic material used to cover wounds and support skin regeneration.

\synonyms
Skin substitute

\medterm{Artificial Urinary Sphincter} An implanted device that restores urinary continence by allowing controlled compression and release of the urethra. It is used mainly for selected cases of stress urinary incontinence and requires surgical placement and follow-up.

\medterm{Artificial Ventricle (Total Artificial Heart)} A pulsatile, pneumatically or electrically driven biventricular replacement that removes
the native ventricles. Indications: end-stage heart failure bridging to transplant or
destination therapy. Requirements: surgical implantation, external power source,
anticoagulation.

\synonyms
Total Artificial Heart

\medterm{Arytenoid Cartilage} The arytenoid cartilages are a pair of small, pyramid-shaped cartilages located on the
posterior aspect of the larynx, articulating with the superior border of the cricoid
cartilage lamina, and they serve as the attachment points for the vocal cords and the
intrinsic laryngeal muscles that control vocal cord movement.

\medterm{ASA Physical Status Classification System} A system developed by the American Society of Anesthesiologists to assess and
communicate a patient's pre-operative physical status and co-morbidities. It ranges from
ASA I (a normal healthy patient) to ASA VI (a declared brain-dead patient whose organs
are being removed for donor purposes).

\medterm{Asbestos-Related Disorders} Asbestos exposure can cause pleural plaques, diffuse pleural thickening, asbestosis, lung cancer, and mesothelioma after a long latency. Risk increases with cumulative exposure and smoking, and evaluation may include occupational history, imaging, and lung-function testing.

\medterm{Asbestosis} Diffuse pulmonary fibrosis caused by prolonged inhalation of asbestos fibers. It may cause progressive
shortness of breath and increases the risk of lung cancer and mesothelioma.

\medterm{Ascariasis} Infection with the soil-transmitted roundworm Ascaris lumbricoides.

\medterm{Ascending Aorta} From left ventricle at the aortic valve to the sternal angle (approximately 5 cm),
enclosed in pericardium. Gives rise to right/left coronary arteries at the aortic
sinuses. Most common site of aneurysm and dissection, especially in Marfan syndrome,
bicuspid aortic valve, and hypertension.

\medterm{Ascending Aortic Aneurysm} Abnormal enlargement of the ascending portion of the aorta. It may be associated with a
bicuspid aortic valve, connective-tissue disease, hypertension, or atherosclerosis.

\medterm{Ascending Colon} Extends from cecum to hepatic flexure below the liver, approximately 15-20 cm,
retroperitoneal (fixed to posterior abdominal wall). Anteriorly: abdominal wall.
Posteriorly: right kidney. Absorbs water/electrolytes and compacts fecal matter. Tumors
may present as a palpable right-sided mass.

\medterm{Ascites} Abnormal buildup of fluid in the abdominal cavity, which can cause abdominal swelling. It is most often caused by high blood pressure in the liver's portal veins due to cirrhosis, but it may also result from cancer, heart failure, kidney disease, or pancreatitis.

\medterm{Ascitic Fluid} Excess fluid collected in the abdominal cavity.

\synonyms
Ascites

\medterm{Ascorbic Acid} Vitamin C, a water-soluble vitamin needed for collagen formation, wound healing, antioxidant activity, and normal immune and connective-tissue function.

\medterm{Ascus} An abbreviation for atypical squamous cells of undetermined significance, a Pap-test finding in which cervical squamous cells are abnormal but not clearly precancerous.

\medterm{ASD} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Atrial septal defect (ASD)}.

\medterm{Aseptic} Free from disease-causing microorganisms, or performed using methods intended to prevent microbial contamination; it does not necessarily mean sterile.

\medterm{Aseptic Loosening} Loss of fixation of an implant without active infection, commonly caused by mechanical
stress and a biologic response to wear particles. It may cause pain, radiographic
lucency, instability, and need for revision surgery.

\medterm{Aseptic Meningitis} Inflammation of the meninges without evidence of typical bacterial meningitis, often caused by viruses or medicines.

\medterm{Aseptic Necrosis} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Avascular necrosis (osteonecrosis)}.

\synonyms
Avascular Necrosis (Aseptic Necrosis)

\medterm{Aseptic Technique} A set of laboratory practices preventing contamination of sterile sites, cultures, and
specimens by unwanted microorganisms. Key elements include hand hygiene, sterile fields
and instruments, surface disinfection, and minimal exposure of sterile items to air and
touch, forming the basis of reliable clinical and culture work.

\medterm{Asherman Syndrome} Intrauterine adhesions or scar tissue that partially or completely obliterate the
uterine cavity, most often after uterine instrumentation or pregnancy-related curettage.
It may cause hypomenorrhea or amenorrhea, infertility, recurrent miscarriage, or
abnormal placentation.

\medterm{ASL} American Sign Language, a visual language used by many Deaf and hard-of-hearing people.

\medterm{ASOT} Antistreptolysin O titer, a blood test measuring antibodies to streptolysin O produced by group A Streptococcus.

\medterm{Asp} The standard amino-acid abbreviation for aspartic acid, an acidic amino acid incorporated into proteins and involved in cellular metabolism.

\medterm{Aspartame} A low-calorie artificial sweetener made from two amino-acid components; people with phenylketonuria must avoid it because it provides phenylalanine.

\medterm{Aspartate Aminotransferase (AST)} An enzyme found mainly in the liver, heart, and skeletal muscle; increased blood AST can indicate injury to these tissues, especially liver injury.

\medterm{Aspartate Aminotransferase (AST) Blood Test} A laboratory test that measures serum AST activity, an enzyme present in liver, heart, skeletal muscle, and red blood cells. Elevated AST indicates tissue injury and, when interpreted with ALT, helps evaluate hepatocellular damage, myocardial infarction, or muscle disease.

\medterm{Aspartic Acid} A nonessential amino acid incorporated into proteins and involved in the urea cycle, nucleotide synthesis, and other metabolic pathways. It is not the same as aspartame, an artificial sweetener that contains aspartic acid.

\medterm{Asperger Syndrome} A former diagnostic term for a neurodevelopmental condition characterized by persistent social-communication
difficulties and restricted or repetitive interests or behaviors, without the intellectual or language impairment
traditionally associated with classic autism. Current diagnostic systems generally classify it within autism
spectrum disorder; support is individualized.

\synonyms
Disorder Asperger (Asperger Syndrome)
Syndrome Asperger (Asperger Syndrome)

\medterm{Aspergillosis} A spectrum of diseases caused by the fungus Aspergillus (most commonly A. fumigatus).
Allergic forms: allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA, in asthma or cystic
fibrosis -- wheezing, eosinophilia, pulmonary infiltrates), allergic Aspergillus
sinusitis.

\synonyms
Aspergillus Infection (Aspergillosis)

\medterm{Aspergillosis Precipitin} An antibody test that may support evaluation of exposure or immune response to Aspergillus; results must be interpreted with symptoms, imaging, and other tests.

\medterm{Aspergillus Infection (Aspergillosis)} Alternate index label for Aspergillosis.

\medterm{Asphalt Cement Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to asphalt cement products. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Asphyxia} A life-threatening lack of oxygen due to drowning, choking, or an obstruction of the
airways. Management: remove obstruction, rescue breathing, CPR if needed. Foreign body
airway obstruction: Heimlich maneuver (abdominal thrusts) for conscious adults; back
blows and chest thrusts for infants.

\medterm{Asphyxia Neonatorum} Failure of a newborn to establish adequate breathing at or shortly after birth.

\synonyms
Birth asphyxia

\medterm{Aspirate} To withdraw fluid, cells, or tissue with suction, usually through a needle; to aspirate can also mean to inhale material into the airway.

\medterm{Aspirate (Substance)} Material withdrawn from a body cavity, lesion, or airway by suction.

\synonyms
Aspirated material

\medterm{Aspiration} The inhalation of foreign material (oropharyngeal secretions, gastric contents, food,
liquid, or blood) into the larynx and lower respiratory tract. Aspiration pneumonitis
(Mendelson syndrome) is a chemical lung injury caused by acidic gastric fluid.

\medterm{Aspiration Pneumonia} Infectious pneumonia from aspiration of oropharyngeal secretions. Risk factors:
dysphagia, impaired consciousness, enteral feeding. Common pathogens: anaerobes,
gram-negative bacilli. Treatment: antibiotics covering oral flora.

\medterm{Aspiration Pneumonitis} Chemical lung injury caused by inhalation of gastric contents, characterized by
bronchospasm, hypoxemia, pulmonary edema, and atelectasis. Risk factors include full
stomach, emergency surgery, obesity, pregnancy, gastroesophageal reflux, bowel
obstruction, and decreased consciousness.

\medterm{Aspiration Risk} The likelihood that food, liquid, saliva, or gastric contents will enter the larynx or
lower respiratory tract. Risk rises with dysphagia, delirium, stroke, neuromuscular
disease, reflux, poor dentition, and reduced consciousness; assessment and prevention
are multidisciplinary.

\medterm{Aspirin (Salicylate) Toxicity} Mixed respiratory alkalosis and metabolic acidosis. Early: tinnitus, hyperventilation,
respiratory alkalosis. Late: metabolic acidosis, fever, seizures, coma. Treatment:
activated charcoal, urine alkalinization (sodium bicarbonate), hemodialysis if severe
(renal failure, pulmonary edema, severe acidosis, very high levels).

\synonyms
Salicylate

\medterm{Aspirin And Heart Disease} Aspirin inhibits platelet aggregation and may prevent recurrent cardiovascular events in selected patients; routine primary prevention requires individualized assessment of benefit and bleeding risk.

\medterm{Aspirin Overdose} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to Aspirin. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Aspirin-Exacerbated Respiratory Disease} A triad of asthma, nasal polyps, and aspirin sensitivity due to COX-1 inhibition. It
involves overproduction of cysteinyl leukotrienes. Leukotriene receptor antagonists are
specifically effective.

\medterm{Assay} A laboratory procedure used to detect or measure a substance, activity, or biological response.

\medterm{Assist-Control Ventilation} Ventilator delivers a set tidal volume at a set rate. Every breath receives the full set
volume. Guarantees minimum rate and minute ventilation. Risk of respiratory alkalosis
and auto-PEEP.

\medterm{Assisted Breech Delivery} Vaginal breech delivery with operator assistance after spontaneous delivery to the
umbilicus.

\medterm{Assisted Circulation} Mechanical or procedural support used to maintain blood flow when the heart cannot pump adequately.

\synonyms
Mechanical circulatory support

\medterm{Assisted Delivery With Forceps} Obstetric application of metal forceps to the fetal head during the second stage of labor to facilitate delivery when progress is arrested. Indications include prolonged second stage, fetal distress, or maternal exhaustion; complications include maternal lacerations and fetal facial nerve palsy.

\medterm{Assisted Living} Residential setting with assistance for ADLs. Less intensive than nursing home.

\medterm{Assisted Reproductive Technology} ART: fertility treatments involving manipulation of oocytes/sperm/embryos: in vitro
fertilization (IVF), intracytoplasmic sperm injection (ICSI), intrauterine insemination
(IUI, some definitions), embryo cryopreservation, gamete donation, and surrogacy.

\medterm{Assistive Device} Equipment that helps a person perform activities, move safely, communicate, or maintain independence.

\medterm{Assistive Device Training} Instruction in selecting, fitting, using, and maintaining devices such as walkers, canes, or crutches, including safe walking and stair techniques.

\medterm{Association} A relationship or co-occurrence between two variables, findings, or conditions; association alone does not prove that one causes the other.

\medterm{Association Cortex} Cerebral cortex involved in integrating information and supporting memory, language, judgment, and complex behavior.

\medterm{Association Study} Comparison of allele frequencies between cases and controls. Genome-wide association
studies (GWAS) identify common variants with small effects on complex diseases.

\medterm{Astereognosis} Inability to identify an object by touch alone despite intact primary somatosensory
modalities (touch, vibration, proprioception), indicating dysfunction of the
contralateral parietal association cortex (typically the postcentral gyrus region or the
dominant inferior parietal lobule).

\medterm{Asterixis} Brief, irregular, non-rhythmic lapse of sustained posture causing a flapping movement,
best elicited by asking the patient to hold the arms extended with wrists dorsiflexed
("hold arms up") for 30 seconds.

\medterm{Asthenia} Abnormal lack of physical strength or energy. It is a symptom that may occur with illness, malnutrition, or medication effects.

\medterm{Asthenic} Characterized by reduced strength or a slender, lightly built body habitus; in clinical descriptions it may refer to physical weakness or, less precisely, an asthenic constitution and should be distinguished from asthenia as a symptom.

\medterm{Asthenopia} Eye strain or visual fatigue, often experienced as aching, burning, blurred vision, headache, or difficulty
focusing after prolonged visual tasks. Refractive errors, dry eye, poor lighting, and extended
screen use may contribute.

\medterm{Asthenozoospermia} Reduced sperm motility, which may contribute to difficulty conceiving.

\synonyms
Asthenospermia

\medterm{Asthma} A chronic inflammatory disorder of the airways characterized by recurrent episodes of
wheezing, breathlessness, chest tightness, and cough. Airflow limitation is often
reversible. It involves eosinophilic inflammation, mucus hypersecretion, and smooth
muscle hypertrophy.


\medterm{Asthma - Control Drugs} Long-term asthma medicines, especially inhaled corticosteroids, reduce airway inflammation and prevent exacerbations; they do not replace a rapid-relief medicine for acute symptoms.

\medterm{Asthma - Quick-Relief Drugs } Rapid-relief asthma medicines, commonly inhaled short-acting beta-2 agonists, relax bronchial smooth muscle for prompt symptom relief; frequent use signals inadequate control.

\medterm{Asthma Adult-Onset (Adult-Onset Asthma)} Alternate index label for Adult-Onset Asthma.


\medterm{Asthma And School} School-based asthma care includes an action plan, access to prescribed rescue medicine, trigger reduction, and staff training to recognize worsening breathing.

\medterm{Asthma Attack} A sudden worsening of asthma caused by increased airway narrowing and inflammation.

\medterm{Asthma Complexities} Asthma varies in trigger, severity, airway inflammation, and response to treatment. Difficult-to-control symptoms may reflect poor inhaler technique, ongoing exposure, incorrect diagnosis, comorbid disease, or severe asthma requiring specialist assessment.

\medterm{Asthma Exacerbation} An acute or subacute worsening of asthma symptoms and airflow limitation, typically
causing increased wheeze, cough, chest tightness, or breathlessness.




\medterm{Asthma Medications} Asthma medicines include controller treatments that reduce airway inflammation and reliever treatments that quickly relax airway muscle. The regimen is guided by symptom frequency, lung function, exacerbation risk, age, pregnancy, technique, and response.

\medterm{Asthma Overview} Asthma is a chronic disorder of variable airway inflammation and narrowing that causes wheezing, cough, chest tightness, or breathlessness. Symptoms fluctuate with triggers, and management combines an individualized action plan, inhaled medicines, trigger reduction, and monitoring.

\synonyms
Bronchial Asthma (Asthma Overview)



\medterm{Asthma, Exercise-Induced} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Exercise-induced asthma}.

\medterm{Asthma, Occupational} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Occupational asthma}.

\medterm{Asthma-COPD Overlap} Features of both asthma and COPD in the same patient. Characterized by persistent
airflow limitation with features of both diseases: airway inflammation (eosinophilic and
neutrophilic), bronchial hyperresponsiveness, and partially reversible obstruction.
Treatment: inhaled corticosteroids plus long-acting bronchodilators.

\medterm{Asthma: Over The Counter Treatment} Over-the-counter products do not replace prescription asthma evaluation or a rescue plan. Some may temporarily relieve symptoms but can delay treatment of dangerous airway obstruction; worsening breathlessness, difficulty speaking, cyanosis, or poor response to a reliever is an emergency.

\synonyms
Asthma OTC Medication (Asthma: Over The Counter Treatment)

\medterm{Astigmatism} A refractive error caused by an irregular curvature of the cornea or lens, resulting in
light rays being focused at multiple points rather than a single point on the retina,
leading to blurred or distorted vision at all distances.

\medterm{Astigmatism Overview} Astigmatism is an irregular curvature of the cornea or lens that prevents light from focusing at one retinal point. It can cause blurred or distorted vision, eyestrain, and headaches and is corrected with glasses, contact lenses, or selected procedures.

\medterm{Astringent} A substance that contracts tissue and reduces secretions or minor bleeding.

\medterm{Astrocyte} A star-shaped glial cell that supports neurons, maintains the blood-brain barrier, and regulates the nervous-system environment.

\medterm{Astrocytoma} A glioma arising from star-shaped cells called astrocytes. Graded I-IV by the WHO; grade
IV (glioblastoma) is the most aggressive and common primary malignant brain tumor in
adults.

\medterm{Asymmetrical} Unequal in shape, size, distribution, or appearance between corresponding sides; asymmetry of a pigmented lesion can be a warning feature requiring assessment.

\medterm{Asymptomatic} Having a disease or abnormality without noticeable symptoms.

\medterm{Asymptomatic Adults} Adults who report no symptoms attributable to the condition or disease being assessed.
Asymptomatic status does not exclude subclinical disease, risk factors, or early pathology.
Evaluation may involve history, examination, screening tests, or targeted investigations according to age, risk, and clinical guidelines.
The term is used in screening, preventive medicine, epidemiology, and studies of disease detected before symptoms develop.
\medterm{Asymptomatic Bacteriuria} Bacteria detected in urine without urinary symptoms; treatment is selective, such as during pregnancy or before certain urologic procedures.

\medterm{Asymptomatic Bacteriuria In Pregnancy} The presence of significant bacterial growth in a pregnant person's urine
without urinary symptoms. It is identified by urine culture and is treated because it increases the risk of symptomatic
urinary infection and kidney infection during pregnancy.

\medterm{Asymptomatic Carrier} A person who harbors a pathogen and can potentially transmit it despite having no symptoms of the associated infection.
\medterm{Asymptomatic HIV Infection} HIV infection without current symptoms; ongoing viral replication can damage immunity, so diagnosis, monitoring, and antiretroviral treatment remain important.

\medterm{Asymptomatic Infections} Infections present without noticeable symptoms.

\synonyms
Subclinical infections

\medterm{Asystole} Complete cessation of cardiac electrical activity, presenting as a flat line on the ECG
with no QRS complexes, P waves, or other organized electrical activity.

\medterm{Ataxia} Lack of voluntary coordination of muscle movements, resulting from dysfunction of the
cerebellum, posterior columns, or peripheral nerves. Types: cerebellar (gait and limb
ataxia, dysarthria, nystagmus), sensory (impaired proprioception causing sensory ataxia,
positive Romberg sign), and vestibular (vertigo, nystagmus, nausea).

\medterm{Ataxia Telangiectasia} An inherited disorder causing progressive coordination problems, visible small blood vessels, and immune deficiency.

\synonyms
A-T

\medterm{Ataxia-Telangiectasia} An autosomal-recessive disorder usually caused by biallelic ATM variants, characterized by progressive ataxia, telangiectasias, immune deficiency, and increased cancer risk.

\medterm{Ataxic Breathing} An irregular, unpredictable breathing pattern with random variations in rate and depth,
seen in medullary lesions. It is the most dangerous respiratory pattern and often
precedes respiratory arrest. It requires mechanical ventilation.

\medterm{Atelectasis} Complete or partial collapse of lung tissue, resulting in loss of aeration. It may be
caused by airway obstruction (resorption atelectasis), pleural disease (compressive
atelectasis), surfactant dysfunction (dissolution atelectasis), or scarring
(cicatrization atelectasis). It impairs gas exchange and may predispose to infection.

\synonyms
Lung Collapse

\medterm{Athelia} Congenital or acquired absence of one or both nipples.

\medterm{Atherectomy} Mechanical removal of atherosclerotic plaque. Types: directional, laser, rotational,
orbital. Used for peripheral arterial disease.

\medterm{Atherectomy (Vascular)} Removal or modification of atherosclerotic plaque from inside a blood vessel, usually with a
catheter-based device.

\synonyms
Vascular

\medterm{Atheroembolic Renal Disease} Cholesterol crystal embolization to kidneys after vascular procedures. AKI with livedo
reticularis and peripheral eosinophilia. Renal biopsy shows cholesterol clefts. Poor
prognosis with progressive renal failure.

\medterm{Atheroma} A localized accumulation of lipid, inflammatory cells, and fibrous tissue within an arterial wall, forming part of an atherosclerotic plaque.

\synonyms
Atheromatous plaque; arterial plaque.

\medterm{Atherosclerosis} Chronic inflammatory disease of medium and large arteries characterized by accumulation
of lipids, inflammatory cells, and fibrous tissue within the arterial wall, forming
atherosclerotic plaques. Initiating event is endothelial dysfunction from risk factors
(hypertension, smoking, diabetes, dyslipidemia).

\medterm{Atherosclerotic} Relating to atherosclerosis, the buildup of lipid-rich plaque and fibrous tissue in arterial walls that can narrow arteries or promote thrombosis.

\medterm{Atherosclerotic Plaque} An atherosclerotic plaque is a lesion within the arterial intima composed of a necrotic
lipid core covered by a fibrous cap, representing the pathological substrate of
atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease.

\medterm{Atherothrombotic Stroke} An ischemic stroke caused by thrombosis or artery-to-artery embolism arising from atherosclerotic disease in a large artery supplying the brain.

\synonyms
Large-artery atherosclerotic stroke; atherothrombotic ischemic stroke.

\medterm{Athetosis} Slow, involuntary, writhing, sinuous movements predominantly of the distal extremities,
sometimes also affecting the face, neck, and trunk, arising from dysfunction of the
basal ganglia (putamen and globus pallidus).

\medterm{Athlete Foot} Tinea pedis: dermatophyte infection (Trichophyton rubrum, T. mentagrophytes) of the
feet, common in those who wear occlusive footwear or use communal showers. Forms:
interdigital (scaly, fissured, macerated between toes—most common), moccasin (diffuse
plantar scaling), vesicular.

\medterm{Athlete's Foot} A superficial fungal infection of the feet, usually affecting the spaces between the
toes and causing itching, scaling, maceration, or fissures. It is also called tinea
pedis.

\synonyms
tinea.
Athletes Foot (Athlete's Foot)
Ringworm Of The Foot
Tinea Pedis (Athlete's Foot)

\medterm{Athletes Foot (Athlete's Foot)} Alternate index label for Athlete's Foot.

\medterm{Atlanto-Occipital Dislocation} Traumatic separation of the atlas from the occipital condyles, a highly unstable injury
with high mortality often associated with spinal cord injury and requiring emergent
immobilization.

\medterm{Atlas} First cervical vertebra articulating with occipital condyles at the atlanto-occipital
joint for nodding movement. Lacks a body and spinous process; has anterior/posterior
arches and lateral masses. Transverse foramina transmit vertebral arteries. The anterior
arch has a facet for the axis dens.

\medterm{Atlas And Axis Joint} The atlantoaxial joint between the first cervical vertebra, the atlas, and the second, the axis; it permits much of the head's rotation.

\medterm{ATM} An abbreviation that may mean the temporomandibular joint or ataxia-telangiectasia mutated, a protein kinase involved in DNA-damage repair; context determines the meaning.

\medterm{Atom} The smallest unit of an element that retains that element's chemical properties, consisting of a nucleus surrounded by electrons.

\medterm{Atonic} Lacking normal muscle tone or contractile strength; an atonic bladder, for example, does not contract effectively to empty urine.

\medterm{Atopic} Relating to an inherited or acquired tendency to develop allergic reactions such as eczema, allergic rhinitis, or asthma.

\medterm{Atopic Dermatitis} A chronic, relapsing inflammatory skin disease characterized by pruritus, xerosis, and
eczematous lesions. It reflects epidermal-barrier dysfunction and immune dysregulation,
with flares triggered by irritants, allergens, infection, stress, or climate.

\synonyms
Allergy Eczema (Atopic Dermatitis)

\medterm{Atopic Dermatitis (Eczema)} A chronic inflammatory skin condition causing itchy, dry, irritated, or rash-covered skin, often associated with an atopic tendency.

\synonyms
Dermatitis, Atopic

\medterm{Atopic Dermatitis - Children - Homecare} Home management of childhood eczema using regular emollients, gentle skin cleansers, and topical anti-inflammatory agents as prescribed. Trigger avoidance, infection prevention, and appropriate clothing help reduce flares and improve quality of life.




\medterm{Atopic Eruption Of Pregnancy} Pruritic eczematous skin eruptions occurring in pregnancy, presenting as eczematous
patches or pruritic urticarial papules and plaques, typically resolving after delivery.

\medterm{Atopic Rhinitis} Allergic inflammation of the nasal lining causing sneezing, itching, congestion, or discharge.

\synonyms
Allergic rhinitis

\medterm{Atopy} The genetic predisposition to develop immediate (IgE-mediated, type I) hypersensitivity
reactions to common environmental allergens (pollen, dust mites, foods, pet dander).

\medterm{Atovaquone–Proguanil} An antimalarial combination used for prevention and treatment of Plasmodium falciparum
malaria and selected travel-related malaria regimens.

\medterm{ATP} Adenosine triphosphate, the principal energy-transfer molecule in cells; hydrolysis of its phosphate bonds powers many cellular processes.

\medterm{ATP Synthase} Complex V of electron transport chain. Uses proton gradient to synthesize ATP.
Rotational catalysis mechanism.

\medterm{Atransferrinemia} Very rare autosomal recessive disorder of iron transport caused by mutations in the TF
gene (3q22.1) leading to absence or severe deficiency of transferrin.

\medterm{Atresia} Congenital absence, closure, or severe narrowing of a normal body opening or passage, such as the intestinal, biliary, or pulmonary outflow tract.

\medterm{Atresia Of Bile Ducts} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Biliary atresia}.

\medterm{Atria} The two upper chambers of the heart, which receive blood from the systemic and pulmonary veins and pass it to the ventricles.

\medterm{Atrial} Relating to an atrium, especially one of the two upper chambers of the heart.

\medterm{Atrial Ablation} A procedure that destroys selected heart tissue to prevent or control an atrial arrhythmia.

\synonyms
Cardiac ablation

\medterm{Atrial Arrhythmia} An abnormal heart rhythm originating in the atria.

\medterm{Atrial Fib} Atrial fibrillation, an irregular and often rapid atrial rhythm that can reduce coordinated heart contraction and increase the risk of blood clots and stroke.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation} The most common sustained cardiac arrhythmia, characterized by rapid, disorganized atrial activity with an irregular ventricular response. ECG typically shows absent organized P waves and irregularly irregular RR intervals. Associated conditions include hypertension, ischemic or valvular heart disease, cardiomyopathy, atrial septal defect, and thyroid dysfunction, especially hyperthyroidism. Alcohol, myocarditis, pericarditis, pulmonary embolism, pneumonia, and cardiac surgery can precipitate AF; sick sinus syndrome may coexist as tachycardia-bradycardia syndrome. Some cases have no identified cause (formerly called lone atrial fibrillation).

\synonyms
AF (Atrial Fibrillation)
Afib (Atrial Fibrillation)

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation (AF)} Alternate index label for Atrial Fibrillation.
\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation (AFib) Warning Symptoms} Alternate index label for AFib) Warning Symptom.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation Ablation} A catheter-based procedure that creates lesions, usually around the pulmonary veins,
to reduce the electrical triggers of atrial fibrillation. It may be considered for
symptomatic patients when medicines are ineffective, not tolerated, or not preferred.
Results and the need for repeat treatment vary with the type of atrial fibrillation and
the person’s clinical features.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation AFib Treatment Drugs} Medicines for atrial fibrillation may slow the ventricular rate, restore or maintain rhythm, or reduce stroke risk with anticoagulation. Choice depends on symptoms, heart structure, kidney function, bleeding risk, other diseases, and whether rhythm control is appropriate.

\medterm{Atrial Fibrillation And Atrial Flutter} Atrial fibrillation produces chaotic atrial activity, while atrial flutter produces a rapid organized circuit; both can cause palpitations and increase stroke risk.



\medterm{Atrial Flutter} A rapid, regular atrial rhythm (typically 250-350 bpm) caused by a macro-reentrant
circuit, most commonly in the right atrium. ECG shows sawtooth flutter waves best seen
in leads II, III, and aVF, with regular or irregular ventricular response depending on
AV conduction ratio (commonly 2:1 or 4:1).


\medterm{Atrial Myxoma} A usually benign primary heart tumor, most often in the left atrium, that may obstruct blood flow or produce emboli and constitutional symptoms.

\medterm{Atrial Natriuretic Peptide} A peptide hormone secreted by atrial myocytes in response to atrial stretch from volume
expansion. ANP promotes natriuresis and diuresis by increasing glomerular filtration
rate, inhibiting sodium reabsorption in the collecting ducts, and reducing renin and
aldosterone secretion.

\medterm{Atrial Septal Defect} Congenital opening in the interatrial secundum allowing left-to-right shunting of blood.
Small defects may close spontaneously. Larger defects cause right heart volume overload,
right atrial and ventricular dilation, pulmonary hypertension, and eventually right
heart failure. Paradoxical embolism may occur with right-to-left shunting.

\medterm{Atrial Septal Defect (ASD)} A congenital opening between the heart's upper chambers that permits abnormal blood flow between the atria.

\medterm{Atrial Septum} The wall separating the right and left atria; an atrial septal defect is an abnormal opening in this wall.

\medterm{Atrial Standstill} Absence of normal electrical and mechanical activity in the atria.

\medterm{Atrial Tachycardia} Supraventricular tachycardia arising from an atrial focus outside the sinus node, often
focal with rates 100-250 beats per minute with discrete P waves of different morphology
from sinus P waves.

\medterm{Atrial Thrombosis} Formation of a blood clot within an atrium of the heart.

\medterm{Atrioventricular (AV)} Relating to an atrium and ventricle, especially the atrioventricular valves or the conduction system connecting the atria and ventricles.

\medterm{Atrioventricular (AV)} An abbreviation for atrioventricular, relating to an atrium and ventricle or to the conduction pathway between them.
\medterm{Atrioventricular Block} Delayed or interrupted electrical conduction between the atria and ventricles of the heart.

\medterm{Atrioventricular Bundle} Conducting fibers that carry electrical impulses from the atrioventricular node to the ventricles.

\synonyms
Bundle of His

\medterm{Atrioventricular Canal Defect} A congenital heart defect involving abnormal development of the septum and atrioventricular valves; also called an atrioventricular septal defect.

\synonyms
an atrioventricular septal defect.
Septal Defect, Atrioventricular

\medterm{Atrioventricular Nodal Reentry Tachycardia} A rapid regular heart rhythm caused by a reentry circuit near the atrioventricular node; commonly abbreviated AVNRT.

\medterm{Atrioventricular Nodal Reentry Tachycardia (AVNRT)} A rapid regular heart rhythm caused by a reentrant electrical circuit involving or near the atrioventricular node.

\medterm{Atrioventricular Node} Located in the posterior-inferior region of the interatrial septum near the coronary
sinus. It receives impulses from the SA node via internodal pathways and delays them by
approximately 120-200 milliseconds before transmitting them to the ventricles. This
delay allows atrial contraction to complete before ventricular contraction.

\medterm{Atrioventricular Valves} The heart valves between the atria and ventricles: the tricuspid valve on the right and the mitral valve on the left.

\medterm{Atrium} One of the two upper chambers of the heart that receive blood returning to the heart.
The right atrium receives deoxygenated blood from the superior and inferior venae cavae
and the coronary sinus; the left atrium receives oxygenated blood from the four
pulmonary veins.

\medterm{Atrophoderma Maculatum} A rare skin condition causing localized, depressed patches of altered pigmentation and tissue thinning.

\medterm{Atrophy} Reduction in the size and functional capacity of a tissue, organ, or muscle caused by loss or shrinkage of its cells.
It may result from disuse, denervation, inadequate blood supply, malnutrition, endocrine deficiency, chronic disease, or aging.
Atrophy can be reversible when the underlying cause is corrected, but prolonged injury may produce permanent structural loss and fibrosis.
Clinical assessment identifies the distribution, time course, associated weakness or dysfunction, and the underlying neurologic, vascular, nutritional, or systemic cause.

\medterm{Atrophy Vaginal (Vaginal Dryness And Vaginal Atrophy)} Alternate index label for Vaginal Dryness and Vaginal Atrophy.

\medterm{Atropine} An anticholinergic drug that blocks muscarinic acetylcholine receptors; it can increase heart rate, reduce secretions, dilate the pupils, and treat selected poisonings or bradycardia.

\medterm{Atropine In Organophosphate Poisoning} A muscarinic antagonist used to reverse bronchorrhea, bronchospasm, bradycardia, and
other muscarinic effects of organophosphate toxicity. Treatment is titrated to drying of
respiratory secretions and improvement in ventilation, not to pupil size alone.

\medterm{Attachment (Dental)} Retentive device connecting denture to implant or tooth. Precision attachments preferred
over bar attachments.

\synonyms
Dental

\medterm{Attachment Disorder} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Reactive attachment disorder}.

\medterm{Attack Rate} The proportion of an at-risk population that develops a specified illness during a
defined outbreak or exposure period. It is a cumulative incidence measure commonly used
for foodborne and other localized outbreaks.

\medterm{Attention} The ability to select, focus on, and sustain mental processing of relevant information while limiting distraction.

\medterm{Attention Deficit Disorder (ADD)} An older term for attention-deficit disorder, now generally classified within attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, especially presentations dominated by inattention.

\medterm{Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD)} A neurodevelopmental disorder involving persistent inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity that begins in childhood and impairs functioning in more than one setting.

\medterm{Attenuated} Weakened or reduced in force, severity, concentration, or biological activity; an attenuated vaccine contains a weakened form of a microorganism.

\medterm{Attenuation Correction} Compensation for photon absorption in tissue. CT-based (PET/CT), transmission source
(SPECT), or MR-based (PET/MRI).

\medterm{Attributable Risk} Difference in risk between exposed and unexposed. AR = incidence exposed - incidence
unexposed.

\medterm{Atypia} Cytologic or architectural abnormality that deviates from the expected appearance of a
cell or tissue. Atypia may be reactive, degenerative, premalignant, or malignant and
must be interpreted with architecture, clinical context, and ancillary studies.

\medterm{Atypia Morphology} Abnormal cellular appearance or architecture that may be benign, reactive, precancerous, or cancerous depending on context.

\synonyms
Cytologic atypia

\medterm{Atypical} Not typical of the usual appearance, behavior, structure, or course of a condition; atypical does not by itself indicate whether a finding is benign or malignant.

\medterm{Atypical Antipsychotics} A class of modern psychiatric medications used to treat schizophrenia and bipolar
disorder. They work by balancing dopamine and serotonin levels in the brain, which helps
reduce symptoms like hallucinations and delusions with fewer movement-related side
effects than older antipsychotics.

\medterm{Atypical Endometrial Hyperplasia} A premalignant proliferation of endometrial glands with cytologic atypia, now commonly classified as endometrial intraepithelial neoplasia; it carries a risk of concurrent or subsequent endometrial carcinoma.
\medterm{Atypical Facial Pain} Chronic facial pain without identifiable cause. Psychological factors may contribute.

\medterm{Atypical Features} A specifier for depressive episodes characterized by mood reactivity, significant weight
gain or increased appetite, hypersomnia, leaden paralysis, and a long-standing pattern
of interpersonal rejection sensitivity. Atypical depression often presents in younger
patients and may respond preferentially to MAOIs or SSRIs rather than TCAs.

\medterm{Atypical Fibroxanthoma} A superficial, usually low-grade malignant fibrohistiocytic tumor of sun-damaged skin in older adults; it may resemble squamous-cell carcinoma or melanoma and is treated by complete excision.

\medterm{Atypical Genitalia} External genital anatomy that does not have typical male or female features, often because of differences in sex development.

\medterm{Atypical Hyperplasia Of The Breast} An overgrowth of abnormal cells in breast tissue that is not cancer but may increase future breast-cancer risk.

\medterm{Atypical Lymphocyte} A lymphocyte with an unusual appearance, often reflecting immune activation or infection.

\medterm{Atypical Measles Syndrome (AMS)} A rare severe illness formerly reported after exposure to measles virus in people who had received an inactivated measles vaccine; it can cause unusual rash, high fever, and pneumonia.

\medterm{Atypical Pneumonia} Pneumonia with a gradual onset, relatively mild chest findings, and prominent systemic
or extrapulmonary symptoms. Common causes include Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydophila
pneumoniae, and respiratory viruses.

\medterm{Atypical Spitz Nevus} A melanocytic proliferation with Spitz-like features and atypia that may resemble melanoma; diagnosis relies on clinicopathologic correlation and, when needed, expert histopathologic review.

\medterm{Atypical Teratoid Rhabdoid Tumor (ATRT)} A rare, aggressive cancer of the brain and spinal cord that occurs mainly in infants and young children.

\synonyms
ATRT
Atypical Teratoid Rhabdoid Tumor
Atypical Teratoid/Rhabdoid Tumor Brain Cancer

\medterm{Audiogram} A chart that shows a person’s ability to hear at different pitches or frequencies.

\medterm{Audiologist} A health professional who assesses hearing and fits hearing aids.

\medterm{Audiology} The science and clinical evaluation of hearing, balance, and related disorders.

\medterm{Audiometry} Measurement of hearing sensitivity at different sound frequencies and intensities, usually using headphones or insert earphones.

\medterm{Auditory Acuity} The sensitivity or sharpness of hearing, commonly assessed by determining the softest sounds a person can detect at different frequencies.

\medterm{Auditory Area} A region of the cerebral cortex that processes sound information.

\synonyms
Auditory cortex

\medterm{Auditory Fatigue} Temporary reduction in hearing sensitivity or listening performance after prolonged or intense sound exposure.

\medterm{Auditory Information Processing Disorder} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Auditory processing disorder}.

\medterm{Auditory Nerve} The portion of the vestibulocochlear nerve that carries hearing signals from the inner ear to the brain.

\medterm{Auditory Ossicle} One of the three small middle-ear bones that transmit sound vibrations.

\synonyms
Ear ossicle

\medterm{Auditory Pathway} The neural route carrying sound information from the inner ear to the auditory cortex.

\medterm{Auditory Perception} The brain's process of detecting, recognizing, and interpreting sounds received through the auditory system.

\medterm{Auditory Processing Disorder} Difficulty recognizing or interpreting sounds despite normal or near-normal hearing sensitivity.

\medterm{Auditory Threshold} The quietest sound intensity a person can detect at a specified frequency.

\synonyms
Hearing threshold

\medterm{Auer Rods} Rod-shaped inclusions formed from fused azurophilic granules in myeloid precursor cells. They strongly
support a myeloid neoplasm, especially acute myeloid leukemia, but diagnosis requires
immunophenotyping, genetic studies, and clinical correlation.

\medterm{Auger Electrons} Low-energy electrons emitted during electron capture decay. Short range (nanometers),
high linear energy transfer. Potential for targeted radiotherapy if localized near DNA.

\medterm{Augmentation Mammaplasty} Surgical enlargement of the breasts using implants or, in selected procedures, transferred fat; risks, expected results, and suitability depend on anatomy and the chosen technique.

\medterm{Augmentation Mammoplasty} Surgery to increase breast size or alter breast shape, commonly using implants or fat transfer.

\synonyms
Breast augmentation

\medterm{Augmentation Of Labor} The stimulation of spontaneous contractions that are judged to be inadequate to achieve
progressive cervical dilatation. Most commonly uses oxytocin (Pitocin) infusion. Also
includes artificial rupture of membranes (AROM, amniotomy).

\medterm{Aura} A temporary sensory, visual, or other neurological symptom that may precede a migraine or seizure.

\medterm{Aural Fullness} A sensation of pressure, blockage, or plugged feeling in the ear. Causes include eustachian-tube dysfunction,
middle-ear fluid, cerumen impaction, inner-ear disorders, and temporomandibular-joint disease.

\synonyms
Ear pressure; plugged-ear sensation; blocked-ear sensation.

\medterm{Aural Polyps} Abnormal tissue projections in the ear canal or middle ear, often associated with chronic infection, inflammation, or cholesteatoma and requiring otologic evaluation.

\medterm{Auricle} The visible external portion of the ear that projects from the side of the head,
designed to collect and channel sound waves into the ear canal.

\medterm{Auricular Hematoma} A collection of blood between the cartilage of the external ear and its perichondrium, usually after blunt trauma.
Without prompt drainage and compression, it may cause cartilage necrosis and permanent cauliflower-ear deformity.

\synonyms
Pinna hematoma; subperichondrial auricular hematoma.

\medterm{Auscultation} The act of listening to sounds produced within the body, typically using a stethoscope.
Essential for assessing heart sounds, lung sounds, bowel sounds, and vascular bruits.
Normal findings: S1 and S2 heart sounds, vesicular breath sounds, normal bowel sounds.
Abnormal: murmurs, crackles, wheezes, rubs, bruits.

\medterm{Auscultatory Gap} A transient silence between the first and second sounds of blood pressure measurement,
sometimes heard in hypertensive patients. Can lead to underestimation of systolic
pressure if not recognized.

\medterm{Austin Flint Murmur} A mid-diastolic, low-pitched rumbling murmur heard at the apex, caused by the
regurgitant jet from severe aortic regurgitation striking the anterior mitral leaflet.

\medterm{Autism And Communication} Autism can affect social communication, language development, nonverbal interaction, and the use of communication for shared attention or relationships. Individualized speech-language therapy, visual supports, augmentative communication, and respectful environmental accommodations can improve participation.

\synonyms
Adults Autism (Autism And Communication)

\medterm{Autism Spectrum Disorder} Neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by persistent deficits in social communication
and social interaction across multiple contexts, restricted and repetitive patterns of
behavior, interests, or activities. Symptoms present in early developmental period and
cause clinically significant impairment.

\synonyms
Autism

\medterm{Autism Spectrum Disorder - Asperger Syndrome} A former diagnostic label for autism spectrum disorder in a person without intellectual disability or clinically significant early language delay. Current diagnostic systems classify this presentation under autism spectrum disorder rather than as a separate disorder.

\medterm{Autism Spectrum Disorder - Childhood Disintegrative Disorder} Childhood disintegrative disorder, also called Heller syndrome, is a rare condition involving marked loss of previously acquired language, social, and adaptive skills after apparently typical early development. Modern classifications place it within autism spectrum disorder, and evaluation should exclude other neurologic causes.

\medterm{Autistic Savant} A person with autism who has an unusually advanced, specific ability—such as in music, art, calculation, or memory—relative to overall functioning.

\medterm{Autoantibodies} Antibodies that mistakenly react with the body's own cells or tissues.

\medterm{Autoantibody} An immunoglobulin that recognizes and binds an endogenous antigen. Autoantibodies may be
pathogenic, as in autoimmune hemolytic anemia, or may serve as laboratory markers of
autoimmune disease.

\medterm{Autoantigen} A normal body molecule mistakenly targeted by the immune system in an autoimmune disease.

\medterm{Autoclave} Sterilization device using high-pressure steam. Parameters: 121°C, 15 psi, 15-30
minutes. Essential for infection control.

\medterm{Autoerythrocyte Sensitivity} A rare syndrome of painful bruising or purpura attributed to an abnormal reaction to a person’s own red-cell components. The diagnosis is one of exclusion after bleeding disorders, medication effects, and other causes of bruising are assessed.

\medterm{Autogenic Drainage} A breathing technique using controlled breathing at different lung volumes to mobilize
secretions from peripheral to central airways. It involves breathing at low lung volumes
(collect), mid lung volumes (loosen), and high lung volumes (evacuate). It requires
patient training and cooperation.

\medterm{Autograft} Tissue transplanted from one site to another within the same individual. Autografts are
genetically identical to the recipient and generally avoid alloimmune rejection,
although donor-site morbidity may occur.

\medterm{Autoimmune} Relating to an immune response directed against the body's own cells, tissues, or molecules.

\medterm{Autoimmune Associated Epilepsy} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Autoimmune epilepsy}.

\medterm{Autoimmune Disease} Autoimmune diseases result from loss of immunological self-tolerance, leading to
immune-mediated attack on the body's own tissues.

\medterm{Autoimmune Diseases} Autoimmune diseases arise when immune responses target the body’s own tissues, producing organ-specific or systemic inflammation and damage.

\medterm{Autoimmune Disorder} A disease caused by an abnormal immune response against the body's own cells, tissues,
or organs. Autoimmune disorders may be organ-specific or systemic and can produce
inflammation, tissue injury, functional impairment, or circulating autoantibodies.

\medterm{Autoimmune Encephalitis} Brain inflammation caused by antibodies or other immune responses directed against neuronal proteins.

\medterm{Autoimmune Encephalopathy} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Autoimmune encephalitis}.

\medterm{Autoimmune Enteropathy} Rare immune-mediated disease causing persistent diarrhoea and small-intestinal mucosal
injury, usually with villous atrophy and crypt hyperplasia. It may occur in children or
adults and can be associated with other autoimmune disorders or immune dysregulation.

\medterm{Autoimmune Epilepsy} Recurrent seizures caused by an immune attack on the brain or by inflammation associated with an autoimmune disorder.

\medterm{Autoimmune Gastritis} Chronic atrophic gastritis caused by antibodies against parietal cells leading to
achlorhydria and vitamin B12 deficiency. Associated with pernicious anemia and increased
risk of gastric carcinoid tumors and type 1 gastric neuroendocrine tumors. Anti-parietal
cell antibodies are present in approximately ninety percent of patients.

\medterm{Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia} AIHA: hemolytic anemia due to antibodies directed against red blood cells. Warm AIHA
(IgG, 37C—idiopathic, SLE, CLL, drugs: methyldopa, penicillin): splenic sequestration,
spherocytes, positive direct antiglobulin (DAT).

\medterm{Autoimmune Hepatitis} Chronic hepatitis caused by immune-mediated destruction of hepatocytes characterized by
interface hepatitis with lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate, elevated transaminases,
hypergammaglobulinemia, and autoantibodies.

\medterm{Autoimmune Keratitis} Corneal inflammation associated with an immune-mediated disorder, sometimes occurring with
rheumatoid arthritis, lupus, or granulomatosis with polyangiitis. It may cause pain,
redness, corneal opacity, and reduced vision and requires urgent ophthalmic assessment.
Treatment is directed at the cause and may include anti-inflammatory or immunosuppressive
therapy.

\medterm{Autoimmune Liver Disease Panel} A laboratory test that measures autoantibodies associated with autoimmune hepatitis and other autoimmune liver disorders, including antinuclear antibody, anti-smooth muscle antibody, anti-liver kidney microsomal antibody, and anti-mitochondrial antibody. Results help distinguish autoimmune hepatitis from primary biliary cholangitis and primary sclerosing cholangitis.

\medterm{Autoimmune Pancreatitis} Chronic inflammatory condition characterized by dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate with
IgG4-positive plasma cells representing the pancreatic manifestation of IgG4-related
disease. Type 1 is more common affecting older men with elevated serum IgG4 and
extrapancreatic involvement.

\medterm{Autoimmune Polyendocrine Syndromes} APS-1 (APECED, AIRE mutation): autosomal recessive, chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis,
hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency (Addison's), plus type 1 diabetes, autoimmune
thyroiditis, vitiligo, alopecia, pernicious anemia, hepatitis, asplenia.

\medterm{Autoimmune Polyendocrinopathy (APECED)} An inherited autoimmune syndrome, also called autoimmune polyendocrine syndrome type 1, characterized by chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis, hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency, and other autoimmune disorders.
\medterm{Autoimmune Polyglandular Syndrome (APS)} A group of disorders in which autoimmune disease affects multiple endocrine glands; manifestations vary by type and may include adrenal insufficiency, hypoparathyroidism, and thyroid disease.
\medterm{Autoimmune Thyroiditis} Immune-mediated inflammation and damage of the thyroid gland.

\synonyms
Hashimoto thyroiditis

\medterm{Autoimmunity} An immune response against self-antigens; it can be harmless or can cause autoimmune disease when it damages tissues or disrupts their function.

\medterm{Autoimmunity: Mechanisms} Loss of tolerance: central (thymic negative selection defects, AIRE mutation -> APS-1)
and peripheral (Treg dysfunction, anergy failure, ignorance loss). Molecular mimicry:
cross-reactivity between pathogen and self (rheumatic fever: Strep M protein + cardiac
myosin; Guillain-Barre: Campylobacter + GM1 ganglioside; reactive arthritis).

\medterm{Autoinflammatory Diseases} Disorders of innate immune regulation that cause recurrent or persistent inflammation without the disease-specific autoantibodies typical of autoimmune disorders. They may cause fever, rash, joint inflammation, or organ damage and can be inherited or acquired.

\medterm{Autoinoculation} Transfer of an infectious agent from one body site to another on the same person, often by touching or scratching.

\medterm{Autologous} Derived from or involving the same individual who will receive it, such as an autologous blood transfusion or stem-cell transplant.

\medterm{Autologous Blood Donation} Autologous blood donation involves preoperative collection and storage of the patient's
own blood for intraoperative or postoperative transfusion. It eliminates risks of
alloimmune reactions, transfusion-transmitted infections, and immune modulation.

\medterm{Autologous Fat Transplant} A procedure that removes a person's own fat from one body area and transfers it to another area for soft-tissue volume restoration or contouring.

\synonyms
Autologous fat grafting; fat transfer; lipofilling.

\medterm{Autolysis} Self-digestion of cells or tissues by their own enzymes, commonly occurring after cell death and contributing to decomposition.

\medterm{Automated External Defibrillator} A portable device that analyzes heart rhythm and delivers an electrical shock when it detects a shockable cardiac arrest rhythm such as ventricular fibrillation.

\medterm{Automatic Dishwasher Soap Poisoning} Toxic effects caused by excessive exposure to automatic dishwasher detergents. Severity depends on the dose, route, timing, coexisting conditions, and clinical findings; suspected poisoning requires prompt assessment.

\medterm{Autonomic Disorder} Dysfunction of the autonomic nervous system, which regulates involuntary cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, sudomotor, urinary, sexual, and pupillary functions. It may be primary or secondary to conditions such as diabetes, neurodegenerative disease, autoimmune neuropathy, infection, or medication effects, and may cause orthostatic hypotension, abnormal heart-rate responses, sweating abnormalities, gastrointestinal dysmotility, bladder dysfunction, or sexual dysfunction. Evaluation and treatment depend on the underlying cause and the organs affected.

\medterm{Autonomic Dysreflexia} Acute, potentially life-threatening sympathetic discharge in a person with spinal-cord
injury at or above approximately T6, triggered by a noxious stimulus below the lesion.
It causes sudden hypertension, headache, flushing or sweating above the lesion, and
reflex bradycardia or other heart-rate changes.

\medterm{Autonomic Ganglion} A cluster of nerve-cell bodies in the autonomic nervous system that relays signals to organs.

\medterm{Autonomic Nervous System} The part of the peripheral nervous system that regulates involuntary functions,
including heart rate, vascular tone, digestion, sweating, pupil size, and bladder
activity.

\medterm{Autonomic Neuropathy} Damage to nerves that control involuntary functions such as heart rate, blood pressure, digestion, sweating, bladder
