function, and sexual function. Diabetes is a common cause, but other diseases, toxins, and medicines may contribute.
Symptoms depend on the affected organs and require evaluation of the underlying cause.

\synonyms
Neuropathy, Autonomic

\medterm{Autonomic Neuropathy Diabetic (Diabetic Neuropathy)} Alternate index label for Diabetic Neuropathy.

\medterm{Autonomy, Patient} A patient's right and capacity to make informed, voluntary decisions about medical care, including accepting or refusing treatment.

\medterm{Autophagy} Cellular self-degradation process delivering cytoplasmic components to lysosomes for
recycling. Macroautophagy: phagophore forms, expands to autophagosome (LC3-II on
membrane), fuses with lysosome (autolysosome) for degradation. Selective autophagy:
mitophagy (damaged mitochondria), xenophagy (pathogens), and aggrephagy (protein
aggregates).

\medterm{Autophobia} An excessive or irrational fear of being alone; it may occur as a specific phobia or alongside other anxiety conditions.

\medterm{Autopsy} A postmortem examination of a body to determine cause and manner of death, performed by
a forensic pathologist (medical examiner/coroner) or, for hospital autopsies, a
pathologist.

\medterm{Autopsy (Medicolegal)} Systematic external examination and surgical dissection of a deceased body performed
under legal authority by a forensic pathologist to determine the Cause, Manner, and
Mechanism of Death, document injuries, collect evidence, and assist judicial
investigations.

\synonyms
Medicolegal

\medterm{Autopsy (Post-Mortem Examination)} Specialized surgical procedure performed on a deceased body to determine the cause of
death, mechanism of death, manner of death (natural, accidental, suicide, homicide, or
undetermined), state of health prior to death, and to document injuries or disease
processes.

\synonyms
Post-Mortem Examination

\medterm{Autoradiography} An imaging technique that uses radioactive labels to show the location of substances in tissue.

\medterm{Autosomal Dominant} Autosomal dominant inheritance occurs when one altered copy of a gene can produce a trait or disorder; an affected heterozygous parent may transmit it to about half of offspring.

\medterm{Autosomal Dominant Inheritance} Inheritance in which one pathogenic variant on a non-sex chromosome can cause a trait or disorder. An affected heterozygous parent may transmit it to about half of the children.

\medterm{Autosomal Dominant PKD} Most common hereditary kidney disease affecting 1 in 400 to 1000. PKD1 chromosome 16
more severe with earlier ESRD than PKD2 chromosome 4. Progressive cyst growth causes
hypertension, pain, hematuria, infections, and eventually ESRD. Screen family members
with ultrasound. Tolvaptan may slow cyst growth.

\medterm{Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease} An inherited disorder causing progressive growth of numerous renal cysts that enlarge
the kidneys and destroy parenchyma, leading to hypertension, flank pain, and eventual
kidney failure. Associated with hepatic cysts and intracranial aneurysms.

\synonyms
ADPKD

\medterm{Autosomal Dominant Tubulointerstitial Kidney Disease} An inherited kidney disorder caused by pathogenic variants in genes such as UMOD, REN, or MUC1. It causes progressive loss of kidney function with relatively bland urine findings; some subtypes cause early gout or anemia.

\medterm{Autosomal Inheritance} Transmission of a genetic trait through one of the 22 non-sex chromosomes. Autosomal
dominant traits may appear in successive generations, whereas autosomal recessive
disease generally requires pathogenic variants on both homologous chromosomes.

\medterm{Autosomal Recessive} Autosomal recessive inheritance generally requires pathogenic variants in both copies of a gene; unaffected carrier parents can have an affected child.

\medterm{Autosomal Recessive Disorders} Disorders caused when a person inherits pathogenic variants in both copies of a gene, usually one from each parent; carriers with one altered copy are often unaffected.

\medterm{Autosomal Recessive Inheritance} Inheritance in which a disorder generally requires pathogenic variants in both copies of a gene. Carrier parents typically have a one-in-four chance of an affected child in each pregnancy.

\medterm{Autosomal Recessive PKD} Rare childhood disorder from PKHD1 mutation. Bilateral enlarged kidneys with fusiform
dilation of collecting ducts. Associated with congenital hepatic fibrosis. Severe forms
cause pulmonary hypoplasia in utero. Variable severity from perinatal lethal to
presentation in childhood or adolescence.

\medterm{Autosomal Recessive Polycystic Kidney Disease} An inherited cystic kidney disorder, usually presenting in infancy or childhood, caused by pathogenic variants commonly affecting PKHD1; it may cause enlarged echogenic kidneys, impaired kidney function, liver fibrosis, and hypertension.

\synonyms
ARPKD

\medterm{Autosome} Any chromosome other than a sex chromosome.

\synonyms
Non-sex chromosome

\medterm{Avascular Necrosis} Death of bone tissue caused by impaired blood supply. It commonly affects the hip or shoulder and
may cause pain, collapse of the joint surface, and arthritis. Risk factors include
corticosteroid exposure, alcohol use, trauma, sickle-cell disease, and systemic lupus
erythematosus. Treatment depends on the site and stage and may include observation,
joint-preserving surgery, or joint replacement.

\medterm{Avascular Necrosis (Aseptic Necrosis)} Alternate index label for Aseptic Necrosis.

\medterm{Avascular Necrosis (Osteonecrosis)} Death of bone tissue caused by impaired blood supply. It commonly affects the femoral
head and may lead to subchondral collapse, secondary arthritis, and need for
joint-preserving surgery or arthroplasty.

\synonyms
Osteonecrosis
Ischemic Bone Necrosis

\medterm{Average Life Expectancy After A Liver Transplant?} Life expectancy after liver transplantation varies with the original disease, recipient health, graft function, rejection, infection, cancer recurrence, and adherence to follow-up. Lifelong immunosuppression, surveillance, preventive care, and management of cardiovascular and metabolic risks are essential.

\medterm{Avian Flu (Avian Influenza (Bird Flu))} Avian influenza is infection with influenza A viruses adapted primarily to birds. Human infection is uncommon but can be severe after contact with infected birds or mammals; exposure history, respiratory symptoms, and public-health testing guide evaluation and treatment.

\medterm{Avian Influenza} Influenza caused by avian-adapted influenza A viruses, acquired mainly through contact
with infected birds or contaminated environments. Human infection can cause severe
pneumonia, respiratory failure, and occasionally systemic disease.

\medterm{Avian Influenza (Bird Flu)} Avian influenza is infection with influenza A viruses adapted primarily to birds. Human infection is uncommon but can be severe after contact with infected birds or mammals; exposure history, respiratory symptoms, and public-health testing guide evaluation and treatment.

\medterm{AVN} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Avascular necrosis (osteonecrosis)}.

\medterm{AVNRT} Alternate Mayo Clinic label; see \textit{Atrioventricular nodal reentry tachycardia (AVNRT)}.

\medterm{Avoidance Behavior} Deliberate behavior intended to prevent contact with a feared, distressing, or threatening situation.

\medterm{Avoidant Personality Disorder} A pattern of social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy, and hypersensitivity to negative
evaluation.

\medterm{Avolition} A severe lack of motivation to initiate and perform self-directed, purposeful
activities. It is a negative symptom of schizophrenia and is seen in severe depression.
It manifests as neglect of personal hygiene, inability to complete tasks, and social
withdrawal.

\medterm{Avulsed Tooth} A tooth completely displaced from its socket by trauma. It is a dental emergency;
management depends on patient age, extraoral dry time, storage medium, root development,
and periodontal-ligament viability, and may require urgent replantation by a dental
professional.

\medterm{Avulsion} An injury in which tissue is forcibly torn from the body by shearing or pulling. Common
forms include dental, fingertip, nail, and degloving avulsions. Management focuses on
hemorrhage control, debridement, tissue preservation, and urgent consultation.

\medterm{Avulsion Fracture} A fracture in which a fragment of bone is pulled away by the tendon or ligament attached
to it, usually during sudden forceful contraction or injury. Common sites include the
pelvis, ankle, elbow, and fingers; evaluation considers displacement, joint involvement,
skeletal maturity, and associated soft-tissue injury.

\medterm{Avuncular} Relating to an uncle or resembling the kindly, familiar manner traditionally associated with an uncle.

\medterm{Awake Fiberoptic Intubation} An intubation technique performed while the patient remains responsive and breathing
spontaneously, using topical airway anesthesia and a flexible bronchoscope. It is one option
when difficult airway management is anticipated and maintaining oxygenation is important.

\medterm{Awareness} Conscious recognition of oneself, surroundings, thoughts, or sensations.

\synonyms
Consciousness

\medterm{Awareness Under Anesthesia} Unintended awareness or recall of events during general anesthesia. It may involve
hearing, sensation, or pain and can be associated with significant psychological distress after the procedure.

\medterm{Axial Skeleton} The bones of the head, neck, and trunk, including the skull, spine, ribs, and sternum.

\medterm{Axial Spondyloarthritis} An inflammatory rheumatic disease primarily affecting the sacroiliac joints and spine, causing chronic inflammatory back pain and stiffness; it includes radiographic and non-radiographic forms.

\medterm{Axial Tomography, Computerized} Computed tomography, or CT, an imaging method that uses X-rays and computer processing to create cross-sectional images of the body.

\medterm{Axilla} Pyramidal space between the upper lateral thorax and medial arm. Bounded by anterior
fold (pectoralis major/minor), posterior fold (latissimus dorsi/teres major), medial
wall (ribs/serratus anterior), and lateral wall (humerus). Contains axillary
artery/vein, brachial plexus cords, and lymph nodes.

\medterm{Axillary} Relating to the armpit or to structures located in the axilla, including axillary lymph nodes and vessels.

\synonyms
Armpit-related; pertaining to the axilla.

\medterm{Axillary Artery} The continuation of the subclavian artery, running from the outer border of the first
rib to the lower border of teres major, where it becomes the brachial artery. Divided
into three parts by the pectoralis minor.

\medterm{Axillary Lymphadenectomy} Surgical removal of lymph nodes from the armpit.

\synonyms
Axillary node dissection

\medterm{Axillary Nerve} From posterior cord (C5-C6), passing through the quadrangular space with the posterior
circumflex humeral artery. Innervates deltoid and teres minor; provides sensation to the
regimental badge area (lateral deltoid skin). Vulnerable in anterior shoulder
dislocations and surgical neck humeral fractures.

\medterm{Axillary Nerve Dysfunction} Impairment of the axillary nerve, often from shoulder trauma, dislocation, compression, or surgery. It can cause weakness of shoulder abduction, deltoid wasting, and numbness over the lateral shoulder.

\medterm{Axis} Second cervical vertebra with the dens (odontoid process) projecting superiorly, serving
as a pivot for atlas rotation, allowing approximately 50 percent of cervical rotation.
Has a large spinous process and flat superior articular facets.

\medterm{Axis Vertebra} The second cervical vertebra, which supports rotation of the first cervical vertebra and head.

\synonyms
C2; axis

\medterm{Axolemma} The cell membrane surrounding an axon.

\medterm{Axon} The single long process of a neuron that conducts action potentials away from the cell
body toward the axon terminal for synaptic transmission.

\medterm{Axon Fasciculation} Bundling of developing axons into organized nerve tracts.

\medterm{Axon Guidance} The developmental process directing growing axons toward their correct targets.

\medterm{Axon Hillock} The region of a neuron where action potentials are usually initiated.

\medterm{Axon Terminal} The end of a nerve-cell axon that releases chemical messengers to communicate with another cell.

\medterm{Axonal Transport} Movement of proteins, organelles, and other materials along an axon.

\medterm{Axoneme} The internal microtubule-based structure of a cilium or flagellum.

\medterm{Ayurveda} A traditional system of medicine from India that emphasizes balance among bodily principles and uses individualized diet, lifestyle practices, and herbal or other treatments.

\medterm{Azole Antifungals} A class of antifungal agents that inhibit the enzyme lanosterol 14-alpha-demethylase, a
cytochrome P450 enzyme essential for ergosterol synthesis in fungal cell membranes. The
depletion of ergosterol and accumulation of toxic methylated sterols disrupt membrane
integrity and function.

\medterm{Azoospermia} Absence of spermatozoa in the ejaculate after appropriate centrifugation and microscopic
examination. It may be obstructive, with sperm production preserved but outflow blocked,
or nonobstructive, with impaired spermatogenesis.

\medterm{Azotaemia} Azotaemia is excess nitrogen-containing waste, especially urea and creatinine, in the blood, usually reflecting reduced kidney filtration, increased production, or altered renal blood flow.

\medterm{Azotemia} Accumulation of nitrogen-containing waste products in the blood, usually because the kidneys are not filtering adequately.

\medterm{Azygos Vein} A vein that drains the posterior intercostal veins and empties into the superior vena
cava. It forms an important collateral pathway when the SVC is obstructed. On chest
imaging, enlargement of the azygos vein may indicate SVC obstruction, heart failure, or
portal hypertension.

\medterm{Azygous Vein} A large vein that drains the posterior thoracic wall and mediastinum, ascending along
the right side of the vertebral column to empty into the superior vena cava. It provides
collateral venous return when the superior or inferior vena cava is obstructed.

\medterm{Labor: Signs And Symptoms} Labor is the process of regular uterine contractions that produces progressive cervical effacement and dilation and leads to birth. Possible signs include increasingly regular and painful contractions, rupture of membranes, bloody show, pelvic pressure, and back pain; urgent assessment is needed for heavy bleeding, decreased fetal movement, severe pain, or suspected complications.

\medterm{Primary Clear Cell Adenocarcinoma Of The Vulva} A rare primary vulvar clear-cell adenocarcinoma, usually showing Müllerian differentiation. Diagnosis requires histopathologic examination and immunohistochemical correlation while excluding metastatic clear-cell carcinoma from sites such as the kidney or gynecologic tract.
